# Chapter 1: The Sea Opens

Thilak tipped the last flask over the horn cup and watched the black thread break in the middle.

"That's it," he said. "That's the third tree."

Nobody spoke. The hearth ticked. Rain dripped from the turf roof into the bucket by the door, as it had dripped for four months. Forty-odd people sat on the packed floor of the pepper hunters' shelter, and every one of them was looking at a cup with less than a thumb of resin in it.

"Noon bark," Thilak said. He wiped his hooked knife on his thigh. "Pachan's map says these three trees run best at midday. They run best. It's still a trickle. Seven more days of rain, by my count, and I can give you a pinch a night, split even. Or I can give the whole cup to the ones whose nails split first and let the rest bear it."

"Seven days," Pavi said, from the wall. Her curled forepaw of a left hand lay in her lap. "Then the roads open and the Resin House groves are ours to tap properly."

"If the sky keeps its word," said Thilak. "It hasn't so far."

Neela counted the room. Habit. Forty-two odiyans, herself and Vellan among them, Vellan sitting by the door where he always sat, turning a lump of pale wood in his big hands. One husband, at the hearth, with his back to the wall. Two exits: the low door and the smoke hole. Three people with the tired grey look under the eyes that meant the ache was close.

She had that look herself. She had stopped checking the mirror of the water bucket.

"Hands," she said. "Even split first."

Twenty-seven hands went up. She counted twice. Then she asked for the other plan, and eleven went up, and four people looked at their knees and didn't vote at all. Those four were the ones who needed the cup most, and they would not say so.

"Even split," Neela said. "And the four who didn't raise a hand get theirs first, because they didn't raise a hand."

A pause. Then Meenu laughed, a short surprised bark, and the room let out its breath. Thilak nodded and began dividing the cup into forty-two with the tip of his knife, careful as a man halving a pepper berry.

The plank leaned against the wall by the hearth, the lamp-black names in rows down its face. It had come up the hill lashed to a packhorse, and the hill had not been kind to it. Neela took the lump of chalk from its notch and went down the list. Each name got a mark when its share was set. Thulasi. Kumaran, called Kuttu. Madhavi. Meenu. Pavi. On and on, in the order the forty had asked for, until only the last blank remained, under all the others.

She wrote her own name there. Neela. Then, smaller, beside it, because she was learning to, *Indu*.

"Last," said Kamalakshi.

She said it from the corner, where she sat with her chin on her knees. Kamalakshi had laughed for the first time on this hill, three months back, and had been making up for it since. "Look at her. Twenty-seven years old, and she still thinks if she stands at the back of the line, nobody will notice she's in charge."

"Nobody has noticed," Neela said.

"The goat has noticed. The goat has *written a letter*."

The room went. Even Vellan's shoulders moved. Neela looked at the hearth, because she had learned where to look, and Devan was not laughing, quite. His mouth had gone crooked at one corner. Thirty-two years old, a man who had given away a fort and an iron seal worn by eleven wardens, and he still waited a full breath to see whether a joke was safe before he let it show.

She loved the corner of his mouth. She filed that thought where she filed all of them, to look at later.

The plank had shifted in the laughing. The crossbar, a split bit of wild pepper vine bound to the top, had loosened again in the damp. Neela knelt, pulled the old lashing free, and tied it anew without looking. Two turns round the bar, a loop through, the second loop doubled back under itself and drawn tight against the first. A fisherman's hitch. The kind that held a net to a boat in a swell and could be undone by one pull on the right end.

Ammu had said a net-mender behind the sheds taught them both. Neela had never remembered the lesson. The knot had simply always been in her fingers, the way her own name hadn't.

She sat back on her heels and looked at it. Her hands had stopped. They did that, lately, when the knot was done.

"Master," Kuttu shouted from the door. "Master, come out. *Come out.*"


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cloud had gone.

For four months the hill had worn it like a lid. Now it lay in long grey rags below the shelter, torn open, and above them the sky was a clean washed blue, so sharp it hurt. Neela stepped out onto wet turf and stopped, because the world had grown a floor.

The hills dropped away in ridges, each paler than the last, green going to blue going to nothing. Past the last ridge, a line of silver. It was wide, flat, and brighter than the sky.

And the sea.

"Never seen it from here," Thilak said, behind her. He sounded as if someone had hit him. "Twenty-two years a member and I've never seen it from here."

"It's not the sea I'm looking at," said Vellan.

He had come up the slope with his limping, dragging step, slow, a big man made smaller by something heavier than the hill. He pointed with the wooden bird.

Neela looked at the water's edge.

Sails. White at first, small as grains of rice on a plate. Then, as her eyes learned them, more. They sat in the shallows off a pale curve of beach, and she began to count, because counting was what she did when something frightened her. Ten. Twenty. They overlapped each other. Thirty. Forty. The wind carried a faint sour smell up the slope, and her stomach turned before her mind knew why. She went on counting. Fifty. Fifty-six. Sixty, near enough.

She knew the curve of that beach.

Never from above. Nobody had ever shown her. But the wide bend of the bay, the low headland like a bent knee on its left, the dark smudge of land behind the sand: her body knew them as it knew the knot. Nellimala. Indu, of Nellimala. A day below Kettamala on the coast side, and under the lantana now, a ring of stone footings that nobody had lived in for twenty-one years.

The wind turned. Down in the gully a bank of lantana flowered orange and pink in the first sun, and its crushed-leaf stink rose up the slope and hit the back of her throat.

She breathed through her mouth. One. Two. Three.

A hand settled between her shoulder blades, light, and did not stay. Devan was beside her. He did not touch her again. He looked at the sea the way he looked at ground he meant to hold.

"Sixty," she said.

"Fifty-eight," said Devan. "Two are fishing boats from the beach. They'll be towing." He was quiet for a moment. "Sixty."

"Is that a raid?"

"No." His voice was flat, the voice he used for facts. "A raid is six boats. A good season, twelve. The treaty let them land a few at a time, by the passes the council sold them, so the coast burned a village at a time and the queen could tell herself it was bandits." He nodded at the water. "Nobody controls sixty."

"The treaty's broken."

"It broke." He said it without heat. "Ittan was the treaty's hand, and Ittan is dead. Muthu seals the pass now, and Muthu seals it honestly, with no Keeper's wax to wave a cart through. Every raider's token is a stick of dead wood." He looked at her, brief. "We did that. You and I and a few months of being right. We shut the door the queen kept open, and the Thurai have stopped knocking."

A thought rose in her that was too quick to be kind. *Then it's ours.* She pushed it down, and looked at the beach again, at the sand where a village had been, and saw the first dark threads of smoke lift from the headland.

"The coast is paying for it," said Devan.

"Yes."

Behind them, no one said anything. Forty-odd people stood in the wet turf with the sun on their faces, and not one of them had a name for what they were looking at.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kesu's grandson came up the goat path at noon, running when he should have crawled.

Neela knew him by his legs first, thin brown legs scraped to the knee and streaked with red laterite, and then by the single braid that had been his grandfather's idea of a haircut. He reached the shelter and went straight down on one knee in the mud, chest heaving. Thilak shoved a gourd at him. He drank, coughed, and drank again.

"Burning," he said. "The coast villages. Pazhayangadi's gone. Kalladi's holding, but there are a hundred of them on the road. They came ashore at first light, and they didn't *sneak*. They had drums."

Neela looked for Thulasi without meaning to. She stood by the plank, a salt-boiler's daughter of Pazhayangadi, who had told Neela once, shyly, that she could still smell the pans. Her hand had come up over her mouth. Her eyes were on the boy and did not move. Pavi, nearest, put a shoulder against hers and said nothing.

"The pass villages?" Devan was crouched in front of the boy, one hand open on his knee. He had not raised his voice.

"Loading carts. Every cart. The old men are carrying the little ones on their backs. My grandfather says to tell you he's staying, he'll hold the well and be damned, but my mother's at the foot of the path with the goats and the pot." The boy gulped air. "And the captain won't come."

"Muthu."

"He's on the gate chamber stair with a paper. He reads it to anyone who asks. *The crown holds Kodiyur and the forts.* The garrison stays inside the walls. The hill villages are to bear what comes." He swallowed. "He doesn't say it like a man who likes it. But he says it."

Nobody moved. Neela watched Devan's face, and it did nothing at all, which was how she knew.

"Then there's another," Pavi said quietly, and stepped aside.

Behind her stood a tapper's runner, a long lad Neela had seen at the Resin House gate, with the stained forearms of a boy who cut bark. He had come up the east slope in the same hour and had been waiting politely to be noticed. He held his cap in both hands.

"From Pachan, Master," he said. "He says the groves are quiet. Nobody's come. He says to tell you everyone's fed." He hesitated. Pachan was a careful man who had once given a ten-year-old a sweet at a long table, and it seemed his runners were careful too. "And he says Kirri hardly lifts her head now. She eats when he sets the dish in. She doesn't come to the bars."

Neela said, "Thank you."

It came out in her ordinary voice, which surprised her.

*Kirthika, a boatwright's daughter, sold at five.* A fishing boat of her own, with a basket for her anklet, a sign to send down the coast. Kirri had cut the bells off that anklet and never taken it off, even to bathe, and was wearing it still, on a civet's thin back leg, in the straw.

Neela folded the thought flat and put it away. There was nothing to do with it today. That was the worst of it. Every grief in the shelter came with a day's walk between it and a hand to reach.

"Thilak," she said. "Give the boys bread."

She had gone three steps when she heard Devan stand.

He had not spoken. He did not speak now. But the room had a way of going quiet around him, even a room that had stopped being afraid of him, and when she turned he was standing in front of the hearth with his hands at his sides, looking, not at her, but past her, at the plank on the wall with its forty-one names.

"I'd like to put something to the Company," he said.

Kamalakshi's head came up. Several people shifted. The word *Company* still sat strangely in the shelter. Nobody had agreed to it. It had started as a joke of Kuttu's about the plank, and then it had stopped being a joke.

"I hold no office," said Devan. "Muthu has the seal, and the queen's order forbids him to send a man down. That order isn't mine to break, and I'd not ask him to. But the Monkey Pass is the only cart road from the coast into the hills, and it is open to anyone who walks it. If those ships put men ashore with any sense, they will be in the gorge in three days. A hundred of them on a road forty paces wide. Behind it are the pass villages, and behind those are the pepper terraces, and behind those are you."

He took a breath.

"I mean to go down and hold the gorge. I won't ask any of you to come. A man who asks that has settled it for you already. I ask only your leave for myself, since I go without leave from a crown that wouldn't give it, and without a garrison, which Muthu cannot spare." He did not blink. "If the hands say no, I sit by this fire and hate it."

Neela stood very still.

He wasn't looking at her. That was the thing. He had not turned his head once. He was speaking to Pavi, to Kuttu, to Thilak with his hooked knife, to the girl called Kamalakshi with Vellan's carved bird turning over in her lap. To forty-two people who held a plank and a cup of thin resin between them, and no power on earth over him.

Asking her would have been easy. She would have said yes, and he would have gone, and nobody would ever have known that he'd needed asking. He could have called on rank, or told them what he'd do. In all the months she had watched men obey him, she had never seen him ask permission to fight, and then bind himself to the answer.

"Is this a vote?" Thilak said slowly.

"If you'll have it as one."

A short silence.

"Then you'll want it carried by hands," said Kamalakshi, and stood up, brushing damp straw from her skirt. "Everyone who thinks the warden of nothing should walk down a mountain and stand in front of sixty ships."

"Fifty-eight," said Vellan.

"*Fifty-eight.*" She raised her own hand first.

Neela watched the hands go up. Not all at once. Thilak's, then Pavi's, then Meenu, who was pressed against the wall as she had been pressed against another wall once, then Kuttu's, slow, with his jaw working. Thulasi's, last of that row, with the other hand still half over her mouth. Madhavi, who had once closed her teeth on Devan's thigh. Hands, and hands, and hands. Four did not rise. She did not count them yet.

Devan stood and waited, and didn't move his eyes. The corner of his mouth was no longer crooked.

Her own hand went up last. She felt every eye in the room go to it.

"Carried," said Thilak, rough. "Go on, then."

Devan nodded once. Then, finally, he looked at her. It was only a look, a plain question with a plain answer in it, and she gave it back to him with her hand still in the air.

*Yes. Go. I'll be a hand behind you.*

By the door, Vellan got to his feet, the wooden bird fisted at his side. "Forty paces wants more than one man," he said. "I'm slow on a hill. I'll start now."

"Pachan's tappers know the scree," said Thilak, already reaching for the sack. "I'll send a runner."

Nobody had asked either of them. Devan looked from one to the other and, for once, had nothing to say.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The sun went early behind the western ridge, as it always did on that hill, and the long gold light went the colour of watered honey.

Neela sat on the flat stone outside the door with a bowl of cold rice on her knee and did not eat. Below the shelter the goat path wound down through the thorn in a pale thread. Devan and Vellan were working out the gorge between them with a stick in the mud, the heads of two old soldiers bent together, one grey, one not yet. Thilak was packing hooked knives into a sack. Somewhere behind her Kamalakshi had started a song, a rude one, and been told to stop it, and not stopped.

She was watching the path because she always watched the path. So she saw the woman first.

A long way down, a pin of green against the grey scree where the path left the thorn. She climbed steadily. She did not hurry, and she did not rest. She was alone. Neela looked for the second, the third, the rest of the shape behind her, the way you look for the hunters behind a hare. There was only the one.

*Not a hunter's pace. Not a runner's.* A tapper's? No. Too even.

At the last bend, where the path became rock, the woman stopped and held out both hands, empty, palms forward. She had meant to be seen doing it. The light caught her sash, bright green, knotted at the left hip, and under it the pale grey of a travelling cloak.

Neela's bowl slid off her knee and rolled.

The woman came on. Closer, her face sharpened: a long plain face, a few years past thirty, a mouth held very still. Her sleeves came down to the knuckle on each hand. She had pulled them there, deliberately, and held them with her thumbs, the way a girl holds her cuffs against the cold.

Neela had seen that woman before, at the padippura of Kettamala, on a wet evening, with a sash that same green. She had said two words in passing, quiet, to a girl with split nails, and walked on as if she hadn't.

*It passes.*

Neela stood. Behind her the song broke off. A stick scraped on stone as Devan came up beside her, and she felt rather than saw his hand go to the place at his hip where a sword would be, and find nothing.

The woman reached the last flat slab of rock before the shelter door and stopped there, six paces off, hands still open. She did not look at Vellan. She did not look at Devan. She looked at Neela, directly, for the length of a long breath, and her face did not change at all.

Then she lowered her hands, slowly, and let go of her cuffs with her thumbs. They rode back up her forearms.

At the left wrist, in the last gold light, lay a single seam, pale and flat, a finger long. Neela had seen a great many wrists like it, every one of them her own kind. They were never smooth.

# Chapter 2: It Passes

Devan stepped between the woman and the door before Neela could, and he did not apologise for it.

The sun had already gone behind the western ridge. What was left lay on the scree in a thin copper wash that would last a quarter hour. When it went, the forty would want the dark, the cup and the low room to themselves. He had said three words to the room the moment he saw the green sash: *Nobody smears tonight.* Kuttu had groaned. Nobody had asked why. They were learning what he was afraid of.

The woman stopped on the flat slab, six paces off. Vellan stood a pace behind Devan's right shoulder and said nothing, which for Vellan was loud. Neela was at his left. He had not asked for that either, and he did not move away from it.

"I am alone," the woman said. "I carry no resin and no flask. You may look."

"I will."

She pushed her sleeves back, left and then right, and held out her wrists in the last of the light. The left bore the single flat seam he remembered from the passage outside Ittan's guardroom, where a cuff had slipped for the length of a breath. The right bore its twin. Both had been pared, and neatly. Somebody had cut away a girl's worth of layered ridges and left a line a finger long.

Vellan breathed in through his nose, slow, twice. Then he gave one small shake of his big head. No tamarind. No scorched pine. She was clean.

"Your name," Devan said.

"Usha."

He waited for the rest of it. There was no rest. She had given it the way a person sets down a knife, handle first.

For three days her escort had slept in his south range and eaten Janu's rice. They had answered to First, Second, Third and Fourth. Janu had tried each of them with a plate and a scolding and got nothing back. Not one had offered a name to anyone at Kettamala. This one had walked up a mountain to hand hers over, and he did not know what it was meant to buy.

"You came to give us a name," he said.

"I came to give you this." She took a letter from inside her sash. It was folded square and tied with green thread, and the thread was sealed under a thumb of green wax. "I was told whoever holds the hill would take it. The Company, if there is one yet."

*Company.* So the word had climbed down the hill ahead of her, or she had guessed it. He filed that, and took the letter.

Even in poor light the wax was clear. The sunbird was pressed deep, wings spread wide, and at the tip of the left wing sat the little chip he knew as he knew his own scars. The same chip marked the payment writ in the oilcloth at the bottom of his pack, which he had carried up the hill rather than leave it in the shutter frame. That seal had bought his death. This one was asking for something.

He turned the letter to the last of the light. Where the thread crossed the wax, a second bird had been pressed so lightly that he nearly missed it. It was smaller and faint, wings folded close to its body. He had seen that bird in the margins of Ittan's tally book, and on the stubs of thirty cut leaves. He kept his face still.

"Read it aloud," Neela said quietly. "They should hear it."

The doorway behind them had filled. He could feel the forty stacked in it, breath held. He broke the wax. It gave with the soft crack of a leaf.

The hand was a clerk's, round and even. The words were not a clerk's.

*To those who came down from the Resin House and have no master: I, Nirmala, Queen of Varanad, greet you. I do not call you rogues. I offer a pardon under my seal for every name on your plank, and the crown's vouching in place of a master's, so that no one need hunt you. Come to Kodiyur as my night-guard, and be fed, and be sworn, and be safe. The war will end. When it ends, those without a vouching will be hunted, and I will not be able to prevent it. I would rather you were mine than anyone's prey.*

*The crown will hold Kodiyur and the forts. The hill villages must bear the raiders as they always have.*

*I wait on your answer.*

Nobody spoke. Devan read the last lines again in his head, and then once more, slower, and the cold started in his hands.

*As they always have.*

Six boats. Twelve in a good season. A village burned at a time and a purse of silver coin in the right hand, and the queen telling herself it was bandits. *As they always have* was the treaty written in her own words, a line she must have thought no one could read. She wanted forty knives behind her walls. She had sixty ships on the beach, and she had written that the hill would bear it. Nobody in that sentence had been counted but him.

His goal, which had been a vote at a fire an hour ago, went hard as set mortar. He held the Monkey Pass, with no office and no seal and no leave from the woman whose chipped sunbird lay in his hand. Nothing in that letter would change it, and nothing in an answer could.

"The pardon is real," Usha said. "I would not say it if it weren't."

"I believe you believe it."

Her mouth moved, not a smile. "That is a careful way to say it, warden."

"I've no office. Devan will do."

He folded the letter along its creases. "How did you find the shelter?"

"The pass-village watcher walks the hill edge at dawn and reads the scent-posts." She said it like a woman reciting a route. "His log goes to a cell at Kettamala, and from there to whoever opens the prisoner's post. That is the queen's household. Every Resin House post went cold in a single night. Forty horses left dung on the goat path." She glanced at Vellan. "A child could have followed it. So I did."

"And Rema's escort left by that path."

"We did."

He waited again. She gave him nothing more, and he could not say whether that was discipline or a wall.

Behind him a boot scraped grit. Neela had moved, no more than a step, and now she stood level with Usha, close, her bandaged left hand held loose at her side. Her voice came pitched low, but the evening was still and the slab was small.

"It passes," Usha said to her.

The same two words she had spoken in the padippura at dusk, about a girl's split nails. Devan had not been meant to hear them then, and he heard them now. Neela's head tilted. Her face went careful.

"Does it," she said.

"Not the ache. The rest." Usha's thumbs had found her cuffs again. "I heard a page burned of itself, at the Resin House, two nights past the new moon. In the household that is not a thing one hears twice. What did the naming cost?"

Devan did not move. He had never told Neela he would not speak of it. He had simply not known anyone would ask her aloud.

Neela did not look at him. She could have said *that is mine to keep*. She could have lied, as she had lied for twenty-one years to anyone with a face and a question. She answered in her dry flat voice:

"He cuts himself, I get the welt. I cut myself, he gets it. Nicked his chin shaving three days back and I found out over my rice." A short breath. "A deep wound goes both ways. A mortal one drags the other down with it. For life, both of us."

"All of it?"

"That's all of it. It's also everything."

She said it to a stranger in the open, on a rock, in front of forty people and a bear. She said it the way one reports the weather in one's own body. *My hand. His hand. The same hand.* Usha's face did a very small thing at the eyes and then stilled. She turned half away to look down the dark path, and Devan stood holding the folded letter and looked at her back, the green sash knotted at the hip.

He had known about the welts since the Resin House wall. He had felt a hook clip Neela's ribs and said *then it's mine*. That was not new. What was new was that the queen's own agent now held it, in Neela's voice, and would carry it back down to a woman who wrote *prey* in a kind hand. His body was a known lever on hers. A spear that went through him in the gorge was a spear aimed at Neela, and the queen's household could now count on that as surely as he could.

Usha turned back. "Understood," she said.

"I doubt it," said Neela. Her tone was not unkind.

The copper had left the scree. The first stars came out thin and grudging over the sea. Devan saw Usha look once at the doorway, at the faces there, and he stepped half a pace to the left so she saw him instead.

"You'll sleep under the lee wall," he said. "Thilak will bring you food. Nobody crosses the slab after dark. It's not a threat. It's a rule of the house."

"I know the rule," she said. "Better than you."

"Then you'll keep it better than we do."

She almost smiled again.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The fire burned low and the shelter slept in lumps. Thilak had given Usha rice and a blanket and watched her eat it. Vellan had gone to sit across the slab from her, his back against the stones, the pale wooden bird turning in his fingers. Neela lay on her side at the far end of the hearth with her hand under her cheek. Her breath hitched on the ninth count. He had learned it by heart.

Devan went out behind the shelter.

A roof post stood at the back, where the lean-to for the packhorse met the wall. It was a squared length of old chestnut, silvered and cracked, set deep in the turf, and it reached about a man's throat. In the dark he could not see the grain. He had no need to.

He stood square. He took a breath through the nose and let it go. Then he raised his right hand, flat, the first two fingers joined and the others folded under, and began the slow drill.

He had made it up at twenty-five, the winter after the ford, when he had stopped hitting living things and could not stop hitting wood. It was a stripped form: one strike, a stride in, a turn of the wrist. Each step took a count of ten. At the end the two fingers stopped a hair short of the post, at the place where on a man the notch below the ear would be. A hair. He knew exactly how wide it was, because he had spent seven years measuring it.

He did it again. And again. He was not thinking of the queen, or of sixty ships. He was thinking about the width of a hair and how it differed from nothing, and how his hand, tonight, wanted to close the gap.

*Not yet,* he told it. *Not on a post.*

He finished the tenth strike and lowered the hand. His breath was even. The chestnut had not been touched.

The pack lay under the lean-to eave. He knelt beside it, undid the flap and found the oilcloth by feel. It was the size of two palms and tied with red thread. He did not unwrap it. He knew what lay inside without a lamp. A strip of hide, a chit split in two, and the queen's payment writ with its green wax and its chip at the left wing tip.

*Death by beast, in the warden's chamber, before the pass closes.*

He pressed his thumb on the knot of red thread, the way a man tests a scar to see whether it still hurts.

It did. The same woman had written *I would rather you were mine than anyone's prey*, and had meant it, probably. She answered every widow's petition with her own hand. Rema had said so. She signed death writs for loyal servants who got in the way. Both were true, and he had spent a night with his head full of the second, and tonight he knew the first as well.

He tied the flap and sat back on his heels. The hill was quiet. Somewhere far down, past the dark of the scree, a red smudge lay on the edge of the world, low and steady. Pazhayangadi, or what was left of it.

He went in and lay down at Neela's back, careful of her left hand, and counted her breaths until his own fell into step with them. He did not sleep for long.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He woke before the light and did not move.

Through the smoke hole the sky had gone from black to the flat grey that came before colour. The shelter was a heap of blankets. Through the gap of the door he saw a figure already up and quiet at the lee wall, boots in her hand. Usha put them on without a sound and straightened her sash at the left hip.

Devan eased himself clear of Neela's sleeping weight. She was awake. He knew it by the way her breath did not hitch, deliberate and even. Her eyes were shut and she was listening, as he was, with the whole of her skin.

Outside, the cold had a bite. The turf was soaked grey and the sea lay under a mist, and the green sash was already at the lip of the slab. Usha did not turn to look at the door. She walked three steps and stopped, and Neela came past Devan's shoulder in her wrap with her bare feet on the wet rock. She had not asked leave to go first and he had not offered it.

They stood a pace apart. Usha said something. It took no more than a breath or two, and her lips moved too little for him to read from the doorway. Neela did not answer. Usha dipped her head, once, and then she was gone down the thorn path as lightly as she had come up it, a pin of green going to grey.

Neela turned and walked back to him. Her face had nothing in it at all.

"Tell me," he said.

"She said, *The leaf sleeps in the queen's room.*" Neela's voice was low and even, the way she spoke when she was counting something. "And then, *If you come, come by the sea stair at the noon gun.*"

"The leaf."

"A leaf of pages. One that could be read." She wet her lips. "The crown leaf. She means the thirty."

The wind lifted the corner of the letter in his pocket. He had not known he was holding it.

A woman who had given her name to a stranger and shown her scars on a bare hillside. A woman who had asked what the naming cost, and listened to the answer as if it were the only thing in the world she wanted to know. She had come alone, with open hands, and left a door.

Or a snare. The same hinges served both. He had been in the border service long enough to know that, and married long enough to know that Neela knew it too.

He looked at the path where the green had gone. The mist was thickening into the gorge, and there was no knowing what waited at the bottom of it.

"Door or snare," Neela said.

"Kodiyur is four days at a hard ride," she went on, flat, counting. "The raiders are in the gorge in three."

"So the door costs the pass I swore to the fire I'd hold," said Devan, "or it costs you going alone."

"Yes."

He looked at her bare feet on the wet rock, then at the path.

"I can't tell," he said.

It was the truth. It was the only kind of answer he had ever given her, and she took it, as she always did, and put her cold hand into his.

# Chapter 3: Two Votes

They laid the plank across two hearthstones and sat round it like a table, forty-three people and a bowl of cold ash.

Devan drew in the ash with a stick. A long crooked line, the gorge, and across it a short bar. "The shelf," he said. "Forty paces wide at the mouth. Narrower under the shelf, where Raman died. A hundred men in single file can be held there by a few who know the ground, and not by many who don't. Behind that line are Kesu at his well, the pass villages, and Parukutty's children. Minnu is six. Chandu is nine." He set the stick down. "I hold the narrows. I ask the Company to hold it with me."

He said it plainly. He had bound himself to their hands, and everyone in the room knew what it cost him to ask.

Neela waited until the ash stopped moving. Then she took the stick.

"I want to go south," she said.

She made a second mark, well away from the first, at the bottom of the hearth. Not a line. A cliff. "The queen sent a letter and a woman with open hands, and she sent a door. *The leaf sleeps in the queen's room.* Thirty pages of the night-guard, in one place, while every eye in Kodiyur is on the sea. She will never watch her own room less than she does this week." She tapped the cliff. "Once the war ends, she can send those thirty anywhere. They'd be hers, or they'd be the raiders' bait. I want a small party, by the sea stair, at the noon gun."

"Four days there," said Thilak. He was a broad man who spoke slowly and meant every word. "Four back, if all goes well. The raiders are three days off. Neela, I'm a tapper. I tapped nightbark beside the sons of half the pass villages. If the narrows fall while we're counting stairs in a palace, I don't know how we look them in the face."

"They'll look at our backs," said Pavi, from the wall. "While we hold the gorge with forty and thirty sit in cages made of paper. Thilak, you were twenty-two years a member. You know what it is to have your name in someone's drawer."

"I know it better than you."

"Then you know it won't wait for spring."

It went round the plank. Kuttu said the villages were people, and the thirty were people, and he'd hold a gorge or climb a stair but he would not be asked to weigh them. Meenu, who rarely spoke, said she had been in a cage the width of a hunter's arm, and would give a season of nights to see the door of another one open. Somebody answered that doors did not matter to a village that had burned. Somebody answered that doors were all that mattered to those behind them.

Madhavi leaned forward. She had once closed her teeth on Devan's thigh, and he had not held it against her.

"Say it's a snare," she said. "The queen wants forty-one knives behind her walls. She sends a clean-wristed woman with a pardon, and a hint of a way in. Why send the hint? Because she wants us in the palace, where she can use the word." She looked at Neela. "She has seen every one of those thirty turn. She doesn't need a page to cage them. She won't need a page to keep *us*, once we've walked in."

"Or," said Pavi, "Usha is one of the thirty. Her wrists were cut. Her cuffs came down over them like a girl hiding a bruise. A prisoner sent that message, and it is the one hinge she could reach."

"Or she's both," said Kuttu. "Snare and door. It happens."

"Or the queen means the door and means the snare," said Thilak. "She can want both. She has wanted both for twenty years."

"Then answer me one thing," Madhavi said, still looking at Neela. "Who knows the stair?"

The ash had gone quiet. Neela made herself answer without turning it.

"Usha's sentence."

"Which is?"

"*The sea stair at the noon gun.*"

"That's all of it?"

"That's all of it. It's thin." She did not look away. "I'd want better before I put a foot on it. But the vote is whether we go at all."

Madhavi sat back. "That's honest," she said. "I don't like it any better."

Neela let them argue on. Every guess was a good one. None of them could be tested before the noon gun. She looked at Devan across the ash and found him watching her, not the room, with the face he wore when he was seeing something exactly.

"Neela."

It was Kamalakshi. She had not spoken since the argument started. She sat with her knees drawn up and the carved wooden bird turning in her fingers, and her voice came quiet.

"If I hold my own page," she said, "can anyone else ever hold me?"

The room went still. Neela had read her page to her in the shed, with Pavi at her shoulder, and Kamalakshi had stood in the sun afterwards for a long time and then eaten two bowls of rice without a word. She had a girl's face still, and she was asking a plain question, as if the answer would be a floor she could stand on.

Neela said the rule as she had learned it, word for word, because anything else would be a lie in a kind coat.

"A page held by its own subject cannot be read against her by anyone else."

Kamalakshi nodded slowly. "Anyone else."

"Anyone."

It sat right. It was the same answer Ammu had given her in the straw, and Neela watched it settle into the room and take. Yet something under it rang wrong, like the eleventh step of the west stair, where her foot had come down and found a hollow. A page, a reading, a voice. She turned it over once. There was a gap in it somewhere, and she could not name the shape of what was missing.

She let it lie. The room had moved on.

"I'll say one more thing," Vellan said, from the door.

He had not spoken all morning. He stood with his shoulder on the frame and the pale wood bird gone from his hand, because Kamalakshi had it. He looked tired and old and very large.

"A gorge wants a wall," he said. "I'm a wall. I'm no use on a palace stair, and no use to a sneak, and no use at all with my leg to a woman who needs to be quiet." He rubbed his jaw. "I go where I'm useful. I go to the gorge. Don't ask me to like the stair."

Nobody did.

"Hands, then," Neela said.

She said it before Devan could, and he did not look as if he minded. In front of forty others, neither of them said husband, or warden, or master. Neither had those words left. They had been handed the same two words, *vote* and *hands*, and they used them.

"Those who say the whole Company holds the narrows, and none goes south."

She counted. Twenty hands. Devan's was among them, steady and open, and he did not look at her as it rose.

"Those who say a party goes south for the thirty, and the rest hold the narrows."

She counted twice. Twenty-two. One person had not raised a hand either time, and it was Thulasi, who was looking at her knees.

Neela put her own hand up for that one, and kept it there, and felt it shake.

"Carried," said Thilak, rough. "By two."

Nobody cheered. Pavi let out a breath and put her forehead on the heel of her curled left hand. On the far side of the plank, Devan folded his arms and looked at the ash, where his short bar and Neela's cliff sat eleven inches apart.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They sorted the rest in a quarter hour, because there was no time to do it slowly.

Six would go. Neela, because she had asked and knew the lanes of that city, or thought she might. Pavi, because the thirty were hers in a way Neela was only learning. Thilak, who said he would go with a face like a man swallowing a nail, because he had twenty-two years of knowing what a master looked like and the guard would trust it. Madhavi, because she distrusted the whole plan and would catch a lie in it. Kuttu, because he could carry. Meenu, because she asked, and had never asked for anything before.

The resin came last, and was nearly a quarrel.

"The gorge is cold and long," said Kamalakshi. "Thirty-six hold it. They'll need every pinch."

"The stair is a dark stair," said Pavi, "and a palace hasn't got the cover of a wood."

"The noon gun's daylight."

"The night after it isn't."

Neela raised a hand. "Even, as before. Every pinch the same. The six carry theirs in a leaf. Nobody takes a double for a good reason, because everyone has a good reason." She let them look at one another. "Hands."

The count came out thirty-six pinches for the gorge and six for the road. Devan took none, and nobody mentioned it. She wrote the marks on the plank herself, and Thilak cut the cup. Meenu, who had cried over a roll leaf in the Resin House, laughed out loud for no reason anyone could name. It ran through the room like a spilled dish. For a moment they were only people eating and arguing in the rain's last week, with their heads full of ships.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Devan found her on the slab at the edge of the turf, with the lamp-black plank lashed on her knees and a coil of Thilak's twine in her hands.

The mist had cleared to the edge of the thorn. Down past the last ridge the sea was a long pewter bar, and the smoke over Pazhayangadi had thinned to a thread. A packhorse stamped somewhere behind the shelter. Someone swore at it in a rude song.

He sat down on the stone beside her, a hand's width off. For a while he said nothing.

"I voted against you," he said.

"I know." She did not look up from the knot. "I counted your hand."

"I counted yours." A pause, dry as chaff. "You voted for yourself."

"Somebody had to."

He made a sound she had heard him make three times: low, in his chest, hardly a laugh. "Pavi was almost sorry for me, I thought. She doesn't look at me like that."

"She looks at you like a wall she's decided to like."

"That's worse."

"It's the same."

Neela drew the twine tight, and the plank went flat against its backing, and she sat with her hands on it. The fisherman's hitch looked back at her.

"I didn't think you'd lose it so well," he said.

"I didn't think you'd lose it at all." She looked at him then. "The minute it went against you, you stayed in your seat with your hand where I could see it."

"I told them I'd keep to it."

"I know what you told them. I wondered what it would cost."

"Four days," said Devan, and the dryness went out of his voice as if a hand had closed on it. "You'll be on the road to Kodiyur and I'll be in the narrows. If a spear opens me there, you'll know it sitting in an inn with a cup in your hand."

"And if a stair finds me, you'll know it with a sword in yours."

"Yes."

Neither of them said the rest. It was in the plain facts: they had chosen, in front of the others, to put a hundred miles of country between two bodies that could not be hurt apart. It would have been simpler to stay. It would have been simpler to tell the room.

"One question each," Neela said, "and no passes."

"That's not the rule."

"I'm changing it. You first."

He considered the sea. "Did you want me to say no?" he asked. "When you stood up. Did you want me to take it, and say I'd come with you, and leave the narrows to Vellan?"

"Yes."

He took that without moving.

"And I'd have hated you for it by the second day," she said. "I wanted you to offer, so I could be the one to refuse. Does that make sense?"

"It does." A breath. "Your turn."

She turned the plank over in her hands.

"Ammu would have told me not to go," she said. "She thought being unseen was the only safe thing in the world. She might have been right, for her. If the thirty don't want it—if they've been given a comfortable cage and a green sash and told it's mercy—" She stopped. "Do I have the right to walk in and open the door?"

He did not answer quickly. He had a habit, which she had learned, of answering a hard question only after he had held it a full breath.

"No," he said. "You have the right to *ask*. That's different. If you ask and they say no, then you walk out and leave a lamp." The corner of his mouth was crooked again. "I learned it from someone who counts servants."

Her face went hot, and she looked away at the sea. Four days at a hard ride. The noon gun. The sea stair.

*Usha's sentence.* She had said it in the council, and called it thin, and the room had still given her the vote. Now it sat in her head with nothing around it. Where did the stair start? Which side of the rock? Did it have a gate, a hunter, a drain? Was it one stair or a dozen, cut into the face of the cliff? She had walked the lanes behind the Kodiyur fish market once with a stick of cinnamon in her pocket and a dead man's name in her mouth. She had never seen the palace except as a pale shape from the quay.

And the only one who had given them the stair was the queen's own woman. If the sentence was a snare, it would be a good one, and a map drawn from it would be worse. Pavi would not know. Thilak would not know. Whatever Neela built on it, she could not check it against anything.

One person alive could. One person had sold the crown thirty pages over twenty years and must have carried them through that house himself, up whatever stairs the house had. He knew the doors, and the sound each one made, and the hour the guard changed.

Chathu.

She had last seen him gagged against the dusk, a soft grey man with an uneven foot and a stare like a ledger. After the assize Rema had ordered him south in a closed cart, with her roll, to wait for the high court. Neela had watched the cart go. Then Usha had said that a cell's post at Kettamala ended in the queen's household, and that the leaf slept in the queen's room. A man the household could read was a man the household could keep. He was under the palace rock, then, or near it. She could not be sure. But if she was right, he was the one man who had climbed the stairs she meant to climb.

She sat with the plank in her hands and the sea in front of her, and remembered a tin of jaggery-sesame sweets, still warm, sitting on a gatepost.

*A gift is the start of a bill.* Twelve tins, and then a thirteenth. Every kindness of his had come with a ledger line.

"What is it?" said Devan.

He had seen it. Of course he had.

"Nothing yet," she said. She had never lied to him since the night she told him about the other one. She did not lie now. She said, "I've found the hole in it."

"Tell me."

She looked at him, at the grey in his hair, the seven pale scars across a right hand that would be four days of road away from her by the end of the week.

"Not yet," Neela said. "Let me see whether I can bear it."

# Chapter 4: Walls Only

The bay went over the lip with his forelegs folded, and his scream came back off the rock a breath after.

Neela had the rope. Everyone near the first bad place had the rope. It ran from a thorn stump through Thilak's fists and round Devan's back, and it burned through Neela's palm before she knew she had closed it. Down on the scree the horse found his feet. Kuttu laughed once, high and cracked, and then there was only the sound of forty-three people breathing hard.

"Next," Devan said.

His voice was level. A red line was rising across the palm of his right hand. The matching sting in her own palm throbbed, and neither of them mentioned it.

Rema's escort had strung three ropes on the goat path, in the dark, on the night they climbed to Kettamala. Neela had hated them for it. Now she could have kissed each knot. The first place was the lip. The second was a chimney of loose laterite where the horses went down on their haunches and the people went down on their backs. The third was the thorn shoulder above the old well, where the path narrowed to a foot's width with air on the left.

She counted all of it. Forty-one steps to the first rope. Nineteen horses, borrowed from the pass villages with a promise written on a tally stick. Two hundred and six breaths on the chimney. She counted the five who would go south with her, too: Pavi, Thilak, Madhavi, Kuttu, Meenu. They were meant to peel off at the fort by first light and take the sea road to Kodiyur. She watched each of them over the rope, one by one, like coins she could not afford to lose.

At the third place Madhavi stood with her back to the drop, talking to a grey mare in a low steady stream. It was a made-up song about turnips. The mare walked down it.

Vellan went last of the people. He took the third rope one-handed, his bad leg dragging, and said nothing the whole way down. Thilak waited at the bottom with a hand out. Vellan looked at it for a moment. Then he took it.

They slept one night under the thorn, with no resin and no fire. Devan walked the line twice in the dark. The second time he stopped by Neela's blanket, said nothing, and put his hand flat on the stone beside her head. She put hers over it. His palm was hot and raw. So was hers.

In the morning the langurs were gone quiet.

She heard the silence on the long last slope, with the roofs of the lower village a smudge below. Every day for months the troops had barked their warnings off the gorge wall, a racket she'd learned to sleep through. Now the cliff gave back only the wind. Devan had stopped before she had. He stood with his head turned toward the cart road, which lay out of sight past the shoulder.

"Strangers," she said.

"On the road." He started walking again. "Walk faster."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kettamala came up out of the red hill at dusk, the laterite walls the colour of old blood. Neela felt her shoulders loosen before she could stop them. She had never wanted to come back to this place. She had married into it with a leopard's tooth sewn in her waistband.

The padippura gate stood shut. A horn sounded from the wall: one long note and two short, the code for unarmed strangers, not many. It was Bhaskaran's horn, and Bhaskaran's lungs, and it was the least welcoming music she had ever loved.

The wicket opened. Muthu came out alone.

He was the same big, broad man, with a grin already on his face and his arms already wide. "Fort's open! Come and be fed, you thieves, you walking disasters, you—" His eye went down the line of forty-three strangers, the lamp-black plank on its packhorse, Vellan at the back with his hood up. The grin held for a count of three and then it slid off.

The iron seal hung on a cord at his belt, a disc cut with a hill and a gate. Eleven wardens had worn it. It looked wrong on him, as a borrowed coat does.

"Devan," he said.

"Muthu."

"You look terrible."

"I've been told."

Muthu laughed, short, and the laugh did not last. He took a folded paper from his sleeve instead. Green wax, with a sunbird pressed deep. "I'd rather you heard it from me than from the wall."

"Read it to them, Muthu. They should hear it too."

Muthu held the paper at arm's length, like a man holding a snake by the neck.

"*Kettamala shall hold its walls only. The garrison shall make no sally beyond the padippura. The garrison shall harbour no rogue, unvouched, or odiyan under pain of the crown's displeasure.*" He lowered it. "It's signed by the queen's household, not by her. I'd like to tell you that makes a difference."

"Does it?"

"It makes me feel better."

Behind Neela somebody drew a short sharp breath. She did not turn. She was the rogue. They were all the rogue, forty-two of the Company, standing on the road with their own free names on a plank. Muthu knew it. His eyes passed over her face without catching, the way a man's do when he has decided not to look at a thing.

His mouth had gone flat. She watched it. This was not the stillness of his lying. She'd learned that tell from Devan, and she knew it. This was the other quiet, the one in which a man stands between two doors and holds his breath.

"The lower court is the garrison's yard," Muthu said at last, to the air above their heads. "Drovers pass through it on their way up to the pass in a season like this. Drovers with horses, who want feeding. Nobody's put a drover on the rolls in my time. I'll not start tonight."

It was a hole in a wall, and a narrow one. Devan inclined his head.

"Thank you."

"Don't." Muthu turned, and the gate swung wide. "I'm not doing it for you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Janu fed them as if she were feeding a wedding.

Neela had not known there could be so much rice in one place. It came out of the kitchen in cauldrons carried by a line of girls and Chinna with her bad hip, and it spread across the lower court under the wall in the last of the light. There was curd. There was a fish stew that smelled of tamarind and made Meenu cry before she had tasted it. Janu stood in the middle of it all in a stained white mundu, scolding everyone she could reach.

"Sit. You, sit. Not there, there's a drain. Eat."

Neela held out a hand for a bowl.

Janu put it down on the stone beside her.

She did it quickly, and kindly, and she did not look away. It was only that her fingers had stayed on the rim, and the rim stayed between them. Her right hand was wrapped in a cloth, and she said that was a burn from the pot. The bandage was clean, and the burn was not fresh. Neela marked that, and the way Janu's eyes dropped, once, to the plain gold bangle at her wrist, and came up again.

*She kissed me on the forehead before we left.* On the forehead, then, and not the hand. Neela knew what that meant. She had stood at the foot of the west stair and told this woman about the hands that had held the lamp that morning, and the hands that had found the body. Janu had forgiven what she could carry. A hand taking a bowl from Neela's was more than she could carry yet.

It hurt, and it was fair. Neela did not ask. She bent and lifted the bowl off the stone herself, so Janu would not have to hand it over twice.

"Eat," Janu said, and went away to scold someone else.

The well stood at the north end of the court, with its low coping and a ring of wet stones. Neela had walked past it sixty times as a bride. Now she saw that it was ringed with people.

Not garrison. Women, mostly, with a cloth spread under each cluster and a child or two asleep on bundles. There were about thirty of them, and they sat in the exact posture of people who had been told to wait and had not yet been told for what.

She took her bowl across to them. Devan watched her go, and did not follow.

"Sit," said a stout woman with a baby, and moved a bundle for her. "You're the warden's wife."

"Neela."

"Chellam. My man tapped toddy on the Kalladi road till the raiders came up the coast." The baby spat. "We got out. That one nearly didn't."

Neela asked who else, and was told, one by one: Gouri, whose mother was somewhere on the road still; a carter's widow who gave no name but gave her a rice cake; an old man called Saroja's father, who wasn't Saroja's father at all but liked to say so, because Saroja sang at the pass-village well and he was proud of it. Neela said each name once under her breath. They went into her like pegs into a board.

She had reached the far side of the well when she saw the small face.

Minnu, six years old, with a rag doll under her chin and a smear of curd on her lip. Beside her sat a thin woman with a cough, holding a nine-year-old boy's hand in both her own.

Parukutty looked up and knew her at once.

"My lady," she said, and began to stand.

"Don't." Neela put out a hand, palm down, as if to a goat. "Please. I'm only eating."

Parukutty sat. "The pass-village men told us the Thurai were two days off. We left our house last night."

Chandu stared at Neela's bandaged left hand. "Is it true my father's spot is in the gorge?"

"Yes," she said. She had never lied to a child, and she wasn't going to begin now. "The warden's going to stand there."

Chandu thought about this. "All by himself?"

She looked across the court. Devan stood at the foot of the wall with a bowl he wasn't eating, talking to Thilak, the grey in his hair gone silver under the torches. He had no seal, no office, no men beyond the ones the road had given him.

"No," Neela said. "Not by himself."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Muthu came for them after the bowls were stacked.

He led them up the east stair, not the west. Neela noticed, and said nothing. He took them through the long dark of the gate chamber and shut the door behind them, and only then did he drop onto the bench under the arrow slit like a man who had been holding a wall up with his back.

"I can't do it," he said.

"I know," said Devan.

"Don't *know*. Argue." Muthu rubbed his face with both hands. "Sixty men on the rolls. I can order them to sit on a wall, and I have. I can't order them out of it. Half of them are Bhaskaran's lads with mothers in the lower village, and none of them has seen an army." He looked up. "I keep my oath to the border. I swore it, on this iron, with you standing there. But I can't hand the garrison to a man who has no office, not even if it's you. They'd follow me, and they'd be hanged for it, and the pass would still fall."

"I haven't asked you to."

"No. You haven't." Muthu's mouth twisted. "That's what makes it hard."

Neela stood against the door and counted its hinges. Three. Devan did not argue. Then he laid his palm flat on the table, with the rope-burn red across it, and said:

"Then I won't ask tonight."

Muthu's eyes closed. "Ask me again," he said, "when you have a reason I can write down. A writ, a count of ships. A thing the high court would read and not laugh at. I'll hold the pass for you, Devan. I'll hold it with a pen if I can't hold it with a spear."

"I'll bring you one."

"Don't bring me a speech."

"I never have."

Muthu laughed, finally, a rough bark. Then he stood and went to the door. He stopped with his hand on it, not looking at Neela.

"Janu's put the cloth on the south bed," he said. "I wasn't told. If anyone asks I've given the warden's guests a roof for one night as a courtesy to the border."

"Guests," said Devan.

"For one night. It's all the word will stretch to."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The guest room off the south range was the one Rema's escort had used. Someone had swept it and put fresh straw in the corners, and left a lamp. There was one bed, with a clean cloth on it.

Neela checked the door. One door, and the window on the north side, at a drop she liked. She sat on the end of the bed and unwound her wrap.

He did not come in at once. He stood at the doorway with his shoulder against the frame and looked across the court.

The warden's chamber was at the end of the north range, up its own stair. From here she could see only the stair door at the top, shut, with one thin line of lamplight under it. Muthu slept there now, in the bed that had been Devan's. Devan did not look at the door. He looked at the well, at the sleeping women, at the dark where the horses were tethered. He looked everywhere except at that line of light, like a man walking round a hole in a floor.

"You haven't looked at your door," Neela said. "Not once. Why?"

"I won't answer that."

He said it flat and quick, and she let it lie. They'd made the rule. One pass each, once in a life, and she'd spent hers on the worst question she could think of. He had a right to his. She turned and started on the knot of her sash.

Behind her, the doorframe creaked as he shifted.

"I miss the creak," he said.

She stopped.

"The third slat. Of the shutter." He was not looking at her. He had his hand on the wood of the frame, and his voice sounded as if it had been handed to him in the dark and he was reading it by feel. "For six years I slept with my back to the wall and listened for something at that window. Then you came, and it was you. I heard it every night. The slat, and then the sill, and then nothing." His thumb moved. "And at dawn it creaked again, and I knew you were home."

"I pressed it on purpose," Neela said. "The first night. To see if you'd wake."

"I know."

"You didn't."

"I was awake."

"I *know*, Devan." It came out less dry than she meant. She got up, crossed the straw and stood in front of him. He still hadn't turned. "I was a thief in your house for forty days, and I rang a bell on your window every night."

"It was the best sound in the fort."

She took his rope-burned hand. She did not hold it carefully, and her own palm stung where it lay against his. He let out a breath that was almost a laugh.

"You told me you wouldn't answer."

"I know what I said." He looked down at her then. "I thought you ought to have it anyway. That's all I've got that's not a plan."

She did not say anything clever. She stood with his burned palm pressed to hers, raw against raw, and put her forehead against his collar. It was a poor sort of home. It was the only one she'd chosen.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She woke to red.

It lay across the ceiling in a long shaking bar. She was on her feet before she had finished the thought, with a borrowed knife in her hand. She'd given hers away in a different life.

The beacon on the shoulder had gone up.

Bhaskaran's lads laid it each dry season, a stack of resin pine on the hill edge below the lightning boulder, to be lit if anything came up the gorge. A pass-village boy sat watch beside it. She had never seen it burn. Now it blazed, orange and wide, in the black square of the north window. Beyond it, at the foot of the gorge, a second smaller flame winked and went out, and winked again.

A horn started on the wall. Bhaskaran's. It began a long note.

Devan was already at the door.

He'd taken a spear from the rack by the lintel, one of Bhaskaran's, left as a courtesy and not a gift. His boots were on. He moved with his shoulder turned from her, fast and quiet, the way he did everything that mattered. He didn't mean to go without telling her. He meant to be in the court before the horn finished, and tell her from there. She had watched him decide things that way for forty days.

She dropped the knife on the bed, crossed the room in three steps and caught his wrist.

He stopped, half through the door.

"Tell me before you go," Neela said.

The horn ended. In the quiet, she could hear the cistern chain clank once on the wheel, and the long dull roar of the fire on the hill.

Devan turned in the doorway with the spear in his left hand. His wrist was hot in her fingers. Behind him the lower court was filling with faces.

"The advance guard," he said, quick and low. "They're in the gorge already. Three days was wrong. I'm going to the boulder. I've no leave, and I've no seal, and I've a Company I can ask." His throat moved. "I'm asking you first."

It hit her chest like a dropped stone. Not the order. The asking.

Five people were sleeping in the south range who were meant to be on the sea road by first light. Thirty pages lay in a queen's room four days away, and every hour spent in the gorge was an hour off the stair. Neela let that cost stand in her mouth for one breath. Then she let go of his wrist.

"Then I'm coming," she said, and turned back to the bed, her hand already out for the knife.

Beyond the window, the second flame in the gorge winked out again, and did not come back.

# Chapter 5: The Lightning Boulder

Corporal Kandan came over the wall without leave, and he brought nine men to prove it.

Devan heard them before he saw them, ten sets of boots on the cart road above the boulder. They did not hide. They came down from the dark with spears held crosswise and the beacon burning orange on their faces. Kandan walked in front, the scar through his brow bright as a split in wood.

Devan waited in the road with a borrowed spear and no seal. He felt every inch of that.

"Sir," Kandan said.

"Not tonight." Devan kept his voice level. "Muthu didn't send you."

"Muthu told us to sit on the wall. We sat on it till the beacon went. Then we got down off the other side." Kandan's jaw worked. "He'll be shouting by now."

"I won't order you to stay."

"I know you won't. That's why we came."

Behind Devan, the scree shifted with a long, soft, shingling noise, like rain on a roof of loose slate. Thirty-six shapes lay along the slope above the lightning boulder, low and still, their eyes catching the fire. They had slipped behind the rocks while the nine were still on the stair. Devan had asked them first, in the lower court, and every hand had gone up before he finished the question. At his right shoulder stood a spotted shape with a long tail that moved once, slowly, against his knee.

He didn't look at it. He looked at the nine.

"I'll say what I mean to do," he said. "Then you'll tell me yes or no. I'll take no. Anyone who goes home tonight goes home with my thanks."

Kandan's eyes flicked past him to the scree and came back. He said nothing. He had called Neela a witch to her face once, at a barracks door, the week Raiu burned. Devan did not ask him to take it back.

Devan drew the gorge in the dust with the spear butt. "Twenty-odd men, an advance guard. They came faster than I thought. I was wrong about three days. There's a picket where the cart road narrows, here. And somewhere along the lip a man holds a bronze mirror. At first sun he answers the ships, and the ships learn the road is open."

"Somewhere?" Sankaran said.

"The lip is half a mile of scree. Too long a front to search before dawn." He glanced up the slope. "If the mirror stays dark, whoever commands the ships has to decide whether to land the rest into a gorge that's gone quiet. It buys a day. I need a day."

"And if he flashes?" Vasu asked. He was the young one with ducks for a mother. Devan knew that because Neela had written it on a roof tile, and he had read the tile twice.

"Then we've lost the pass. So ten of you hold the road at the cut, with spears, and nobody chases past the boulder. I want one of them alive, to talk."

"You want us to hold a road while they go up a hill," said Sankaran. "Alone."

"I want you to hold it with me."

Nobody spoke. On the hill the beacon popped and threw a spark.

"Behind me are things you were told not to look at," Devan said. "They're on your side. Face the road. If you turn round, you may carry what you see for the rest of your life, and so may they. I'd rather you never did."

Kandan stared at him. Then he grounded the butt of his spear in the road and said, quite slowly, "Raiu held the stair at your door."

"Yes."

"You sent him for oil."

"Yes."

"I'd have asked you why, if you'd come back to the wall to be asked." Kandan's mouth twisted. "I'll hold your road."

One at a time, the others grounded their spears. Sankaran. Kuttan, who could not write and had had a letter written for his mother. Vasu, last of the three he knew, and then six more. Devan said each name aloud as the butt struck stone. He had never learned the names of ten men in an hour before. He had a woman with a lamp-black plank to thank for it.

Kandan turned his back on the scree. The rest followed.

Devan bent his head toward the spotted shape. "The lip," he said, very low. "Look. Don't take him yet."

She went up the scree without a sound, and the dark closed over her.

The bear had come down the slope to Devan's left side. It was breathing through its nose.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The picket stood where the road pinched between the rock and the drop. He was a short man in a dark coat with one hand on a fire-basket he had not lit, and he looked toward the beacon, not toward the boulder. They had not yet learned to look behind.

Devan walked out of the dark into the orange light with the spear low, where the man could see it.

"Kettamala holds this road," he said, in the coast tongue every trader half-spoke.

The picket's head came round. His hand left the basket and went to his hilt.

Eight years was a long time to keep a rule. He had sworn on the west stair never to hold a throat. He had no oath about this, and that was the worst of it. There was only the spear in his hands, and the road, and the choice.

He knew a hundred places on a man's body. He knew the door under the skull, and the notch below the ear. He turned the point away from every one of them.

The thrust went in under the collarbone, high and to the side, where it would bleed and hurt and kill nobody if the man was lucky. It pinned the coat to the rock behind him. The picket cried out, short and furious, and Devan let go of the shaft before the man's knife came up.

It came up anyway, from a second man he had not seen, crouched in the lee of the basket. The edge went across Devan's right forearm, a hot thin line. He took the second man's wrist in his left hand and turned it until the knife dropped, and the bear came out of the dark past his hip like a landslide.

It did not kill. It put the man down and stood on him. That was all.

Devan stood with blood running into his palm and the picket pinned beside him, groaning. He had struck first, openly, on a stranger's road, with nobody's life on the edge of it but his own choice. He felt it down to the soles of his feet, and what he felt was not shame.

Behind him, Kandan said hoarsely, "That was clean."

"It was meant to be."

The bear's flank pressed warm against his left side. The thing that had killed his sister, and Raiu, kept the dark off him. Devan did not move away. He had decided that on the hill above the Resin House. Deciding it again was harder.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The bear's head swung toward the thorn before Devan heard anything. A low rumble ran through its ribs.

"You've taken the door man," said a voice from the scrub. "That's the smaller half of the work."

Every spear came round. Devan raised his hand, and they stopped.

The man who came out of the thorn was old and bent, in a grey wrap, with a staff he did not seem to need. He kept to the edge of the firelight and held his empty hands out from his sides. He smelled of woodsmoke and dried fish.

"Koman," he said. "From the village above the gorge. I walked the hill edge for the Resin House eleven years, reading posts that weren't mine to name. The House has not paid me since the wood fell, three moons back." He sniffed, a long dry dig of the nose. "My nose is nobody's. I thought I might offer it to somebody."

Devan looked at him. A watcher. The man who had logged Neela's bare nights and carried them to Chathu. He could say it, and it would be fair. He could also see a thin man who had stood in the rain for three moons with nothing coming, and who had walked down a dark road to a stranger's spear.

"What does it tell you?"

Koman turned his face up into the wind off the coast. His eyes were half shut. "Twenty-three men, counting the two you hold. Twenty below the bend, sweating the long sweat of men who've marched three days. Fish, tar, unwashed wool." He stopped. "One more, not on the road. Up the lip, left of the cut. Cold sweat, and brass. A man holding a thing he's afraid to drop."

"Your mirror," Vasu breathed.

"What do you want for it?" Devan said.

"The House's wage. Seven panam to the moon." The old man smiled thinly. "I do not expect it."

"You'll have it. From my pocket, not the crown's. I've no office to pay you from, so it's my word." Devan held out his hand, palm up, cut and bleeding. "It's cheaper than coin."

"I've found it holds better," Koman said, and put his dry hand in the blood and shook.

Devan turned his head toward the scree, not his body. "Left of the cut," he said. "Alive, if you can."

The slope breathed out, and thirty-five shapes went up into the black.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The slope was empty.

Devan could not see them. He could hear them: the slide of loose stone, a pad of paws, then nothing. A good deal of nothing. He stood in the road with Kandan at his right and the dead basket cold in front of him, and counted. Six hundred. He had set the number in his head at the boulder, because dawn would not wait for a man to feel ready. The sky over the pass went from black to the colour of wet slate.

At first sun the mirror would flash. At first sun, too, every odiyan on that hill would stop being what she was, in plain sight of anyone who looked up. He had thought that through at the boulder, and he'd asked no one to hurry. They knew.

Five hundred and forty. The east went from slate to pearl.

At five hundred and ninety the top of the cliff took a thread of gold. And on the lip, small and bright as a dropped coin, something winked.

It winked again, stuttering, half uncovered. Devan's hands shut on the spear. Six hundred. His count ran out, and the glint was still there, swinging toward the sea.

Then it went out.

The lip stayed dark. The gold crept down the rock above it and found nothing to strike. There was a short grunting cry up there, a clink of metal on stone, and a hush that went on too long. The spear shaft was slipping in his sweating palm. He set the butt on the road to keep hold of it, and started breathing again.

Nothing flashed. A little while later a soft chuffing came down off the scree, and a scrape, and a leopard came out of the shingle with something wrapped in leather held in her jaws. She walked up to Devan and set it at his feet.

It was a bronze disc, the size of a plate, wrapped in oiled hide. He turned it over. On the back, scratched small and neat beside the handle, was a bird with its wings folded.

He did not know what he was seeing. He put his thumb over it, and put the mirror in his belt.

The leopard sat down against his leg. Her flank was shaking. The sky behind her turned gold at the edge.

"Face the road," Devan said. "All of you. Now."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They came down from the cold light on two legs, in the cloths he had brought on Kuttu's packhorse and left wrapped behind the boulder. He heard the rustle and the muttering and Meenu's laugh, shaky and surprised. He did not turn his head. Kandan kept his eyes on the road and the veins stood in his neck.

When Devan did turn, it was because he heard a heavy tread on rock.

Vellan sat on the lightning boulder, wrapped in a grey blanket, his bad leg stretched out and his big hands hanging between his knees. His face was grey and old and very tired. He was looking at Devan, who had not asked him to look.

"The lad on the road," Vellan said. "Your corporal. He's the one who stood with Raiu."

"Yes."

"I could smell the grief on him." The big man's mouth shifted. "I'll not ask him to forgive me. I'd not ask you."

"Don't."

"No." Vellan rubbed his jaw. The gorge wind lifted the edge of his blanket. "She said something, your sister," he said. "At the end."

The spear slid from Devan's hand and rang on the stone.

He had waited six years for any word. He had a drawer of notes in a camphor chest and a slate with Unni's name on it. He had never had her voice, her last sentence, the one sound that was not in a box. And the man who had it was sitting on a rock in a blanket.

"Say it," he said. His voice did not sound like his.

"When I've earned saying it."

"You don't get to set a price on my sister's words."

"I'm not setting it on you." Vellan's eyes did not waver. "I'm setting it on me. I'll tell you when I've earned the right, and not before. If I'm dead first, I'll find a way."

Devan stood with his fists shut. He could have forced it. There were a dozen ways to force a man. He opened his hands.

"Then earn it," he said, and turned his back, because that was the only answer he could give.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


His forearm had opened while he wasn't thinking. The sleeve was dark and stiff to the wrist.

"Sit," said Neela.

She had come up beside him in a cloth the colour of dust, her hair wet at the temples. She had a new bruise on her cheekbone and a look he knew, tired and amused and furious all at once. She held out her right arm. Along the outside of the forearm, red and raised and weeping at the edge, ran a thin shallow line.

"Third thing I felt on the lip," she said. "I had the mirror man's wrist in my teeth when you took that knife. I nearly dropped him."

"You didn't."

"I nearly did." She dropped to her knees on the stone and took his arm. "Sit, Devan."

There were ten men within five paces. Kandan had half turned. Vasu was staring, Sankaran had an elbow in Vasu's ribs, and old Koman leaned on his staff with his head tilted like a bird's. Devan had been taught how to take a wound. Take it alone. Wrap it behind a door. Let nobody see you need the cloth.

He sat down on the stone.

Neela tore a strip from the hem of her cloth. She cleaned the cut with water from Kandan's gourd, which Kandan had held out without being asked. Her hands were quick and rough and did not tremble. She wound the strip twice and tied it, and her thumb pressed once, hard, on the knot.

"It will stay," she said.

"It will."

She stood. She did not go back to the others. She walked to Kandan with the gourd in her hand, the man who had called her witch at a barracks door, and held it out to him.

"Kandan," she said.

Aloud. His name, and nothing else. Sankaran stopped breathing. Vasu's grin went out of his face.

Kandan looked at the gourd, and at her. He took it. He did not look away. He turned it once in his scarred hands, and said one word.

"Neela."

It was all he said. Devan watched Sankaran lower his eyes from the pair of them, and then lift them again, and not look at his boots. Not one of the nine turned from her.

"Done," Neela said, and went to stand at Devan's shoulder.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The man from the road sat propped against the rock with a rag stuffed under his collarbone, grey-faced, glaring. Koman had named him in a murmur: a sergeant, going by the cord at his shoulder. Devan crouched in front of him with the bleeding arm resting on his knee.

"You'll live," he said, in the coast tongue. "If the wound is kept clean. I'd like to know what you came for."

The sergeant spat. Then he looked at the bandage, and at the woman standing at Devan's shoulder, and at the old man with the staff. Perhaps it was the fact that nobody had yet hit him again.

"Cutting-saws," he said at last, thick-tongued. "Mortar lime. Barley for seed. Not for burning."

"For what?"

"For the hills." The man shifted. "The admiral's orders were plain. Take the pepper hills and hold them through the dry season. Not strip them. *Hold* them. We were told to leave the vines standing, and the villages that gave way."

Devan did not move. He had spent three days bracing for a raid, for fire on the coast and a quick hard push up the gorge. A raid came and went. It went back down to the ships with what it carried.

This one had come with seed.

"Hold them against whom?" Neela said quietly, in the same tongue.

"Against whoever comes." The sergeant looked up at her. "Your queen is a sensible woman. We were told she would not come."

Nobody on the road said a word. Kandan's face had gone white under the scar, and behind him the first sun cleared the east wall and fell full on the stones.

"Then they don't go home in spring," said Vasu, very small.

Devan looked down the gorge to the sea. It was forty paces wide at the mouth. He had ten men, an old nose, thirty-six who changed with the dark, and a day.

# Chapter 6: Lantana

The resin was thinner than lentil water, and Neela spread it as if it were gold.

Thilak had coaxed it out of the three noon trees before the Company left the shelter. One finger-width for each pair of wrists, and not a drop to waste. She tipped the pinch the Company had voted her onto two fingers and set them against the scars at her throat. The old burn woke at once. She breathed through it, and smeared the inside of each wrist, and slid the plain gold bangle up her forearm out of the way.

Dusk was coming down the gorge like water poured into a bowl. Below the lightning boulder, Kandan's nine stood on the cart road with their backs to her and their spears grounded. Nobody had told them to face away tonight. They did it anyway.

Devan sat on the foot of the boulder, with Kannan's white pebble in his cheek.

"Say it back," he said. It came out round and slow, as if he were talking through a plum. He had never once complained of it.

"You sound like Muthu with a cold."

"Say it back."

"Count what I can. Tents, horse lines, anything with wheels. Don't fight. Don't stop to be clever." She tied off the thought with a count of her own, three breaths. "Be at the cache by grey."

"And?"

"And if I smell your mirror man's friends, I run."

"You count first."

"I'll count while running."

His mouth moved around the pebble. It was not quite a smile, but it was kin to one.
