She looked at the stone in his cheek. Once it had been the one thing standing between her and a cage. A sleeping man says things, and Devan had seen her turn. Now there was no cage left to open. Since the night at the Resin House, nobody could close one on her without her own voice beside theirs. The pebble was only a habit now, the way you keep a door propped that no longer sticks.

"You don't need it," she said.

"I know." He shifted it with his tongue. "I like having something in my mouth that isn't an opinion."

Muthu had asked for a thing he could write down. A count of ships was no use from a leopard's mouth, but a count of tents was. Pavi and the others would go south as soon as the gorge let them. Tonight there were numbers to fetch.

She pulled her sash off over her head and laid it with the day cloth in the crack under the boulder, where the stone made a dry shelf. Then she stood in the last of the light and let the burn climb.

It always began in the wrists. The skin went tight and hot, and then her spine remembered it was a rope. She dropped to her hands. Her ribs bent. The ground came up, and with it the smell of the whole hill, a hundred things at once, one of them Devan. He had not looked away. He never had.

A warm hand rested between her ears for the space of one breath.

Then she was gone down the road, low and quick, with the dusk in her mouth.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The coast road ran downhill all the way, and the dark ran with it.

She kept to the verge. A leopard on a cart road is a rumour, and a rumour is better than a sighting. She passed a burned byre, then a well with its rope cut, then a village that was only a smell of wet ash and a dog gone feral in the ruins. It watched her go by with its ribs showing and did not bark.

The sea wind blew inland. It carried the camp up to her long before she saw it: tar, fish, wet wool, horse dung, cook-smoke and old sweat. It carried her own scent away, up the hill. She had come at the one angle that mattered. She had not planned it. Her body knew.

Then a second smell came across the first.

It was green and rank and slightly sweet. It was crushed leaf, with a sharpness under it like a bruise. She stopped in the middle of the road with one paw lifted.

Lantana.

She had hated that smell all her life. She had hated it in the Resin House yard where it grew along the drain, and again on the east path to the kalari. She had never known why. She had put it down to being a person who hated things.

It rose from a long slope of orange-and-pink flowers on the left of the road, and it was not a smell. It was a house with the door open. She did not remember a house. She stood very still in the road and understood that her body had been remembering one for twenty-one years.

*Nellimala.*

She had the name from a page. She had never had the place.

She put her paw down and went on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The footings lay where the slope eased toward the shore. Low stone rectangles, thigh-high, dozens of them, laid out along what had once been lanes. Lantana had grown through every doorway. A cart's width of beaten earth ran down the middle, and the Thurai had made it theirs.

She went in on her belly through the flowers. She counted.

Sixty-three tents. They stood in rows, grey canvas pitched across the old hearths, some of them with a corner pegged to a threshold stone. Three horse lines ran down the far side of the lanes: twenty-two beasts, twenty-six, twenty-four. Two sentries stood at the track end with their backs to a fire-basket, talking about a woman in some other port. A third sat asleep against a barley sack. There were forty or fifty sacks stacked under a hide roof. Seed, then. The sergeant had told the truth.

At the south end of the camp a frame rose out of the dark.

It stood three men high. Its ribs were ship timber, curved and still smelling of tar, and it had been bolted to a cart bed with iron straps. Two shaped planks were nailed along one side. Adzes had worked on it by lamplight, and the chips lay pale and fresh on the ground. It was not finished. It was a siege tower, and it was being built to stand against a gate.

*Kettamala's gate.* The padippura. She counted the rungs of its ladder: nine so far, with room for ten more. She made herself note the wheel hubs, and the greased axles, and the pegs where the hide screens would go. Muthu would want the pegs.

She was working her way along the lantana toward the horse lines when the wind brought her something else.

It came from a sleeper.

He lay on his back on a blanket by the tower's foot, outside any tent, with a cloak for a pillow and one arm flung wide. He was a lean man, no older than Devan. His sleeve had ridden up to the elbow. She was close enough to hear his breath whistle.

Salt. That came first. Then clove-bark, dry and sharp, cutting through the tar like a blade through cloth. It was a resin, she knew it as she knew her own hands, and it was fresh on his skin. It was not nightbark. Nightbark smelled of scorched tamarind and pine sap. This was something else, from some other tree, on some other coast.

She came forward a hand's breadth at a time until she could see his wrists.

The skin on the inside of each was ridged. Pale lines, laid over one another, old and thick. She knew the pattern as she knew her own face. Hers looked like that. Ammu's had. Every girl in the Resin House had worn it.

*Who are you?*

She could not ask. She had no mouth for it. She could only lie in the flowers with her heart going like a fist on a door and know that if she woke him, there would be a shout, and the camp, and no more numbers. Dawn was hours off, and she could not turn back before it.

That was the rule. It had never bitten harder.

She memorised him. The long jaw, the clean-shaved chin, a ring on his left thumb. Then she backed out of the lantana, a hand's breadth at a time, and did not breathe until the horse lines had put forty paces between them.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The bank above the footings was her only shelter.

She went up it in a long loop and lay down in the lantana where it grew thickest, with her chin on her paws. The camp lay below her, a smear of low fires. The tower stood over it like a rib cage. Salt and clove still reached her, thin as a thread, though the wind had turned.

The grey was a long way off.

She could do nothing with the hours. She could not go back and she could not speak, and she could not tell the hill what she had seen. So she lay there with it, and the lantana filled her nose, and she let herself go on smelling it.

*I was here.*

She tried to find a door, and there was no door. She tried a wall, a hearth, the face of anyone. She had a hand's worth of sounds: someone knocking a net-weight against a post, and a woman's voice, low and cross. They might have been real. They might have been the tide.

Everything else had been taken at six years old. A cart. A rope. A blindfold. The Resin House had cleaned her out so thoroughly that she had never known what was missing.

*Indu.*

It was her name, and it meant nothing to her, and it was hers. Somewhere in this ruin, a woman had stood over a net in the dark and said it. It had been a good name. It had been said in a good voice.

She pressed her face into the earth.

A leopard cannot weep. She found that out lying in the dark above her village, with her whole body shaking and nothing coming out of her at all. She was mourning a place she could not picture. She was mourning people who had no faces. She thought she was a fool, and the thought did not help.

She stayed there until the sky began to grey.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cache was where she had left it. She came down the last slope with her legs shaking under her, and she barely made the crack in the boulder before the dawn took her.

It always tore. She had come to expect that. It left her on all fours on the cold earth, with every muscle cramped and her stomach folded in on itself like a fist. The hunger came behind it, hollow and sick, and her hands would not stop trembling.

"Here."

Devan stood three paces off with his back to her, one arm held out behind him. Over it lay her day cloth. He had warmed it. She could feel the heat of it on her skin before she touched it. He must have carried it inside his coat all night.

She took it and wrapped herself. He turned.

He had a leaf packet in his other hand. Rice and curd, still faintly warm, folded tight. He put it in her lap without a word, sat down on the stone across from her and spat the white pebble into his palm.

"Eat," he said, in his own voice.

She ate. It was the best meal she had ever had, which she was beginning to think was true of all of them. When the packet was empty he took it from her fingers.

"Numbers first," she said. Her voice came out cracked. "Sixty-three tents. Horse lines of twenty-two, twenty-six, twenty-four. Three sentries on the track end, one asleep. Barley sacks, forty or fifty. A tower." She described it, rung by rung. He did not write. He said the figures back to her under his breath, once, and she saw them go into him like coins into a purse. "Muthu can have those."

"He can."

"There's more." She held the cloth closed at her throat. "A man by the tower. Sleeping. He had a resin on his wrists. Not nightbark." She found the words as she said them. "Salt and clove-bark. Sharp. And his wrists were scarred. Ridged, like mine."

Devan's face went still. It was not the stillness of a man hiding something. It was the stillness of one holding a very full cup.

"The same scars."

"The same kind. I don't know." She shook her head. "I couldn't ask him. I couldn't turn back. I lay there six hours with it in my head and no mouth to put it in."

"You have a mouth now."

"I know." It came out as a laugh and broke in the middle. "That's not all of it. That's not even the main thing."

She stopped. He waited. He did not fill the quiet with a question. After a moment, he moved a little closer, which was what she needed. She had not known it until he did it.

"It smelled like lantana," she said. "The whole slope. It's the smell I've hated since I was small, and I never knew why. It's my village, Devan. I walked down the road and my body knew it before I did." She pressed the heel of her hand against her eye. "I lay there all night and tried to remember one thing. One door. I couldn't. It's gone, and I'm grieving it, and I can't even say what I'm grieving. It's stupid."

"It isn't."

"It's a smell."

"It's a place." His voice was flat and level, and it was the kindest thing she'd heard in weeks. "I've had six years of a stair. It doesn't need a picture to be a loss."

She did not answer. She could not.

The lantana grew right up to the boulder's foot, a thick orange bank of it, bruised pink where the dew had settled. Devan looked at it. He did not say a single word about the Thurai, or the tower, or the scarred man. He leaned across, took a handful of leaves and crushed them in his fist. The sharp green reek rolled out over both of them.

He held his hand open between them, and breathed it in.

Then he slid down off the stone and sat beside her in the lantana, shoulder against shoulder, and did not try to make it better. The sun came up over the hills and found the two of them there. It did not seem to mind.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They walked up the cart road when the light was full.

Her legs held, mostly. His arm stayed close enough that she could have leaned, and she did not need to. She marked that, and put it away to think about later.

The nearest pass village lay where the road bent. It was one of the Monkey Pass villages, Kesu's among them, a handful of mud-and-thatch houses with a well in the middle. Refugees from the coast had filled every doorway. They sat on cloths in the early sun with their bundles and their children, saying very little, as the women at Kettamala's well had done.

Neela slowed without meaning to. She was saying names under her breath, as she did, and gathering faces like a woman gathering fallen fruit.

At the end of the row sat an old woman with a net across her knees.

She was mending it with a bone needle, her fingers dark and quick and knotted at the joints. A tear ran along the lower edge. The thread went through the mesh, looped, and came back under itself. Looped twice. Drew tight.

Neela stopped walking.

She knew that knot. It was a fisherman's double hitch. It was the one Ammu had tied round every pepper packet, the one a net-mender behind the sheds was said to have taught them both. It was the one that had always been in her own fingers.

She looked down. Her hands were working at the end of her sash. She had not told them to. A loop, a second loop, and a pull. The same knot.

The old woman's needle stopped.

She looked up, not at Neela's face, but at her hands. A long look. The net slid down off her lap and lay forgotten in the dust, and her mouth came slowly open on a word she did not say.

# Chapter 7: Ammini's Girl

The old woman's needle did not move, and neither did Neela's hands.

Eleven people sat in the doorways of the village. She counted them without meaning to, and counted the two men by the well and the child on a hip. The knot had fallen out of her sash. Her fingers had made it again and stopped halfway, like a guilty dog.

Ten paces back, Devan stood with Kesu at the well. He had seen her stop. He did not come. He turned his head and said something to the one-eyed old man, and the two of them became very interested in a bucket.

"Again," the old woman said, in the coast tongue. She did not look up. "Slowly."

Neela undid the hitch and tied it. Loop, loop, pull.

"Who taught you that?"

"A net-mender," Neela said. "Behind the sheds, where I was raised. That's what I was told."

"Told." The old woman's mouth worked. "Sit down, girl. Sit like you're buying fish. Nobody here is watching a woman buy fish."

Neela sat on the cloth beside her with her knees drawn up. The woman picked the net off the dust and laid it across both their laps, so that two pairs of hands bent over one tear in the mesh. It was a neat trick. From the doorways they were two women haggling over thread.

"Ponni," the woman said. "Fishwife. Nellimala, till I was put out of it. Now I sleep in Kesu's cattle shed and he says I snore."

"Do you?"

"Like a boat on shingle." The needle went in and out. "You ask questions fast. Ammini did that. She'd answer a thing with a thing, and you'd walk away having told her your whole week."

Neela's throat shut. She made herself breathe through it. "Who was Ammini?"

"The best net-mender on that coast. She had hands like yours. She'd tie that hitch on a fishing line, a pepper sack, a child's shoe." Ponni glanced up at last. Her eyes were clouded at the edges, and very sharp in the middle. "She had a girl who'd copy her. Sat on the sand at four years old with a bit of cord, tying and tying. Cross as a cat when it came out wrong."

"What was she called?"

"You know what she was called."

Neela did not answer. Across the road a woman rocked a baby, and a boy fed a crust to a thin dog. The well chain clanked. She said, "Tell me anyway."

Ponni looked at her for a long moment. Then she bent over the net.

"Indu," she said.

It went through Neela like cold water. She had read it off a leaf in the dark. She had said it to Devan across a lamp. She had never heard it said by a mouth that had said it before.

"The second harvest," Ponni said, low and even. "You'll want that, I think. Twenty-one years back. We were drying fish on the racks. Pepper was in. The boats came at dusk, two of them, straight over the bar at slack water with no pilot. Nobody crosses that bar without knowing the marks. They knew the marks." Her needle stopped. "They came in like men who'd been told the way. Like men who were expected."

"Expected by whom?"

"If I knew that, I'd have said it to somebody." Ponni's lip curled. "Nobody asked. The women got the children up the goat path. The old ones stayed with the fires so the raiders would go for the fires. I had my own two boys by the collar. I ran with you in my arm for the first bend, and then Ammini took you."

*I ran with you in my arm.* Neela's hands lay dead on the net.

"And at the top?"

"That's what I've carried." Ponni's voice dropped further. "There were men at the top. Not raiders. Raiders came from the sea, in hide and bronze. These were men in grey, standing in the road, quiet as posts. They had a cart. A big flat cart with a tailboard. A little bird was painted on it, yellow and small. The paint was new."

Neela said nothing. The word *grey* sat in her chest like a swallowed stone.

"They didn't hurt anyone. That's the thing I can't set down. They took the youngest, the ones who'd stopped crying out of shock. They lifted them like sacks of rice. One of them counted. I remember he counted. And I ran, with my boys, because I was a coward." Ponni's face did not change. Her hands did, a small tremor in the left. "I never saw where the cart went."

"I know the path," Neela said.

It came out of her without leave.

"I know it. Left of the lightning boulder, up the scree, across the shoulder, down through thorn to an old well. I've walked it twice this year without a map, and I couldn't say how." Her voice thinned. "I was carried up it. On someone's back. I remember the thorns on my legs. Somebody was breathing very hard."

"Ammini," Ponni said. "She went up after you. She had her net weights still in her apron. I heard them clink."

"And she came down."

Ponni's eyes met hers. They were wet, and she let them be.

"She went up," she said. "She did not come down."

Neela sat quite still. A fly walked along the net. Somewhere a pot lid rattled. She had thought she would have to ask for a mother, a name, a face, and she had been handed all three in the same breath. They sat in her like stones in a bag.

"My name is Indu," she said.

Her voice shook, and she let it. "I was Indu. They gave me a number. I've been a number for twenty-one years. I have not said that name to anyone who knew me before."

"I know," said Ponni gently. "Your hands said it. You needn't have."

"I wanted to."

"Then it's said." The old woman slid the net aside and put her knotted hand over Neela's. "I'm not telling this to the village, girl. You've the look of a woman who has to be somebody else by evening. Go and be her. I'm here. I snore, but I'm here."

Neela turned her hand over under Ponni's and held on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The Company read its rolls by the fire at the lightning boulder, because nobody could sleep.

It had started with Thulasi. She had her page and the long strip fastened to it, and she had sat with it in her lap through the whole of supper, not reading, only holding. Then Kuttu had said, "Well? It's your village," and she had unfolded it with Pavi's help and said *Pazhayangadi* aloud as if it were a coin she feared was false.

Now forty people sat in a ring with scraps of leaf. Thilak had burned his own. He listened, one hand flat on his knee. Meenu read hers with her lips moving, a letter at a time. Neela went from one to another with the lamp, helping where the cipher stuck.

She was good at it. She could hold a stranger's loss in a lamp's yellow circle and keep her hand steady. She had got through the evening so, with Ponni's voice in her ear: *a little bird, yellow and small.*

She had told the gorge nothing. A pass village could hold its own secrets, but she could not ask it to hold hers.

"Mine's gnawed," Kuttu said. He held up the roll. A corner was missing, ragged as a torn cheek. "Rats. Look. They've had my father's name."

"Let me see."

She took it. The edge was chewed through a line, and the leaf was thin at the break. She turned it to the lamp.

And a locked door shut in her head.

She was twelve. Perhaps thirteen. Chathu's room smelled of lamp oil and jaggery. The door stood a hand's breadth open, because she had been told to wait with a tray. Inside, Kirri sat on the stool with her chin up, seventeen and proud and white about the mouth. Chathu was bent over a table. A rat-eaten page lay in front of him, brown at the edges. He uncorked a pot of resin-ink and wrote, line by line, in a hand she could have read from the passage.

"There," he'd said, so softly. "Not to worry, child. A master who saw you turn can always write you again."

Kirri had smiled up at him.

Neela blinked. Kuttu was staring. The lamp had tipped, and she righted it.

"It's mended in my head," she said, "that's all. Chathu did it once for a sister. Wrote a page out fresh, after rats."

"Kind of him," Kuttu said, without any irony at all.

"Yes." She handed the leaf back. "He was kind."

She filed it where she had always filed it. Under mercy, under the soft hands that wrote the cage. Chathu was in a cell in Kodiyur with his sweets taken away. It was an old kindness and he was a cell's worth of mercy. She did not look at it again.

When she raised her head, Devan was watching her across the fire.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He found her afterward at the edge of the light, sitting on the ledge of the boulder where the stone was still warm. The pebble was gone from his cheek. He sat a hand's width off and stayed quiet as long as it took her to realise he meant to.

"You went somewhere," he said.

"Kuttu's roll. A memory." She dragged her thumbnail along her forefinger. "Nothing. Chathu once mended a page. It's nothing."

"You counted at the well."

"Did I?"

"Lips moving." There was no smile in it. "And you sat with the old woman an hour. Kesu says she's called Ponni. I didn't come over."

"I know. Thank you."

"I want it said I could see you from where I stood."

"I know that too." She drew breath. "Her name's Ponni. She's a fishwife from Nellimala. She knew my mother. My mother's name was Ammini, and she mended nets, and she tied my knot." It came out in a rush, in order, like a list of exits. "The boats came over the bar at dusk without a pilot, as if they'd been told the marks. The women sent the children up the goat path. At the top there were men in grey, with a cart. A tailboard with a little yellow bird painted on it. They took the youngest. And my mother went up after me, and Ponni never saw her again."

She had said it all in one breath. Afterward she sat with her mouth shut, nails dug into her palm.

Devan did not move.

"Men in grey," he said.

"Yes."

"I'll say what I thought of, and then I'll say what it's worth." He was looking at the dark where the road ran down to the sea. "The man who came to Muthu the winter Asha died. He wore a grey cloak. He asked for a monthly letter *to keep the warden safe*. That's what Muthu told me, in the kalari pit, after Ittan." He turned his head. "But the Kettamala garrison wears grey. Half the wardens' men in the hills do. Half the pass villages dress their ploughmen in the same cloth. It's cheap and it takes the dye."

"So it means nothing."

"It means I thought of it, and you should know that I did. I don't know more." He said it slowly, each word set down like a stone on a wall. "I won't build on it. I won't ask you to."

She let out the breath she had held since the well. It was not a shout, or a laugh. It was something near both.

"You could have said it was the crown."

"I could have. I'd have been guessing, and you'd have had to carry my guess as well as hers."

"Twenty-one years ago," she said. "You'd have been eleven."

"I was learning to fall off a pony."

"I was learning to be Seventeen."

He took that in. After a while he said, "Tell me the rest, if there's any."

"There's the path." She looked at her hands. "I knew it the way I know my own teeth. I walked it for the first time this year and I knew where the thorns were. Somebody carried me up it and I didn't remember. My body did. The lantana, the knot, the path." She laughed, dry. "I've been tripping over my own childhood for months and calling it luck."

"It isn't luck."

"No."

The fire popped behind them. Down the bank someone was singing, low and off the note, the way Ammu used to. It might have been Pavi. It might have been the wind.

"Devan."

"Yes."

"We leave for Kodiyur at first light. Six of us. I don't know what's at the end of that road. I don't know who is at the end of it." She made herself look at him. "If I don't come back—"

"Neela."

"If I don't, I want somebody to have it. Not on a slip. Not in a ledger anyone can seize." Her voice cracked. "Someone who'll keep it as it was said. My mother's name was Ammini. She was a net-mender of Nellimala. She went up the goat path after her daughter, and she didn't come down."

It was a gift, and it cost her. A name could be written down. A name could be read aloud against you. Every leash she had known began with someone knowing a thing and holding it. She held it out anyway, on the open palm of her mouth, and did not look away.

He took it as if it had weight.

"Ammini," he said.

He said it right. He said it once, with the broken vowel at the front of it, as Ponni had said it, as if he had been listening at the well after all. Then he stopped. He did not say it again, to be sure of it. He did not make it a promise, or a ribbon, or a lamp.

"I'll keep it," he said. "You'll take it back from me yourself."

"That's not a promise."

"No." He looked at her, level, grey-eyed. "It's what I'd like. I won't swear to what I can't make true. But I'll keep this. Nobody will have it from me."

She leaned until her shoulder was against his. The stone was warm beneath them both.

Far down the road the sea wind came up through the gorge, bringing salt, and under the salt a thread of green and bruised sweetness, rank and ordinary and her own. She breathed it in. She did not hate it.

*Ammini. Indu.* A yellow bird on a tailboard.

Someone had counted the children as they were lifted. She lay awake a long time wondering who had held the tally, and for whom.

# Chapter 8: The Shelf

"Fourteen hundred," Devan said to the rock.

The rock did not argue. It sat in the cut above the lower narrows, a slab the size of a cart bed, and Thilak's pry-bar was under it. Devan put his shoulder to the bar beside the tapper's. The slab groaned, tipped, and went down the scree in a long grey roar. Dust rose from the shelf and hung there.

Raman had died under that shelf. Eleven riders in single file, a bow on the lip, and a man with a nine-year-old boy at home. Devan had ridden past it every day for a week and had not looked up. Today he looked up all the time.

He had put a lookout on the lip for that reason. Biju, a volunteer with a red headcloth, sat up there on a flat rock with a horn across his knees, facing the road and the sea. Every few breaths Devan found the red cloth against the sky, and every time it was there.

"Fourteen hundred what?" Kandan said.

The corporal stood below with a rope round his waist and a spear across his back. The scar through his brow was white with dust. Forty garrison volunteers worked the face behind him, cutting and prying and hauling. Muthu's order had said the garrison stays inside the walls. Kandan's forty had not found that order interesting.

"Thurai." Devan wiped his palms on his thighs. "Sixty hulls on the water. Twenty-odd to a hull."

"And us?"

"Forty-two odiyans. Six ride south this morning. Thirty-six who stay." He kept his voice flat, as he would for a ration count. "Thirty-six and forty. Seventy-six."

"Fourteen hundred over seventy-six," Kandan said slowly.

"Eighteen."

"Nineteen if the six don't come back."

"They're out of the sum already."

"Then nineteen once we've buried the first of us." Kandan did not smile. "Count like that, warden. It's more honest."

"I count like a man who wants the wall." Devan tipped his head at the shelf. "Look at it."

The lower narrows ran forty paces from cliff to cliff. On the east side the shelf jutted out over the cart road, a stone lip with loose scree piled behind it. Raiders could not climb it from below. But if the face came down, the scree came with it, and a bank of broken rock would choke the road chest-high across the whole gorge. Spears on top of that bank, stones thrown from the lip above, and eighteen Thurai for each defender trying to climb.

Not a wall. A bad day made of rocks. It was the only one he had.

"You want to bury the road," Kandan said.

"I want to make them climb it."

"Under spears."

"Yes."

Kandan chewed that over. He did not ask what happened when the number went past eighteen apiece. He was an honest man, and Devan was grateful for it.

Thilak set the bar again. He was a broad, quiet man, a member of the Resin House twenty-two years and a tapper before that. When he leaned, the rock moved. Vellan worked on the far side of him, hauling slabs from the cut in his arms like a man carrying sleeping children. He did not talk. He had not repeated Asha's last words since the pass, and Devan had not asked. They had the work between them, and that did for now.

Devan's spear leaned against the pry-pole at his elbow. It had been within arm's reach every hour since Raman died. He no longer noticed putting it there.

Down the road, on the verge where the gorge opened toward the sea, Neela's six stood by a pack pony. She had come as far as the shelf with them at first light and then stopped, head tilted back, looking up at the lip. *Show me what you mean to do,* she'd said. He had shown her. Then the sun had climbed, and no one had left. She had checked the pony's girth. She checked it again, one eye on him, and then a third time, though the strap had not moved.

A pebble ticked.

Devan heard it as a soldier hears a cough in a quiet hall. Scree falls down. This one had rolled sideways, then dropped, from the high side of the cut where nobody was working.

He looked for the red headcloth.

The flat rock on the lip was empty. A horn lay on it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


A man stood beside the horn.

He was thirty paces off and a body-length above, a lean shape in hide and bronze with a short bow already drawn. The arrow pointed down at the work gang. Beyond him, on the slope, two more were coming down the scree at a run. They went light-footed, stepping on the stones that did not slide.

"Down!" Devan shouted. "Scouts, the lip, left of the cut!"

Men scattered. A volunteer named Vasu was too slow, bent over a rope with his back to the slope, and the nearest runner came for him with a sword held low. Devan took the spear from the pole and went up the scree.

It was twelve steps. He had counted them that morning, because he counted everything now.

He had not struck first at a living man in eight years. He had trained the habit out of his hands. Let them come. Hold the line. Stand and be the wall. And a man had died under this shelf while he stood and was the wall, and the wall had not been enough.

The runner raised his blade in a clean, practised motion. He had not yet turned toward Vasu's back. He was still a body in the air, between one stride and the next.

Devan drove the spear into him under the ribs.

It went in the way it was taught to, and stuck, and he let go of it. The man looked down at the shaft. Then he sat down on the scree, quite gently. He was dead before the stones he'd dislodged finished rolling.

Something bright bounced from his belt and spun on the rock. A small coin. It flashed in the sun and lay flat.

The arrow from the lip sang past Devan's ear and cracked on stone. Then Vellan went up the slope on all fours with a roar that had nothing human in it, and Thilak was a half step behind him. The bowman dropped his bow. He tried to go back over the lip. He did not make it.

It was short. When it was over, four Thurai lay on the scree, and two had run up the slope toward the cut, and Kandan's men were already after them.

Devan stood over the man he had killed. The weight he'd expected did not come. Instead a coldness touched his left arm, and he looked down. A strip of skin was laid open from shoulder to elbow, thin and shallow, where the bowman's second shot must have grazed him. He had not noticed it. It began to bleed freely, and he pressed his hand over it.

*Neela.*

"You struck first," Kandan said.

The corporal had come up beside him, breathing hard, his spear dripping. There was no accusation in his face. He looked like a man who had waited a long time to see something and had now seen it.

"Yes," said Devan.

"Eight years, the garrison says. You never killed a man first."

"I did today."

"Aye." Kandan looked at the dead man. Then at Vasu, who sat on a rock with his head between his knees, alive. "Better you than him."

A shout came down from the lip. Two of Kandan's men were standing over the flat rock. Devan climbed to them with his hand clamped on his arm.

Biju lay behind the rock, on his side, with his red headcloth pulled half off. His throat was opened. There was hardly any blood on the stone. The scouts had come up the back of the cut while he watched the road, as he had been told to, and he had not made a sound.

Devan stood there. He had counted seventy-six that morning.

"Seventy-five," he said, to no one.

He knelt, straightened the headcloth, and put the horn into the boy's hands. When he stood, his hand had begun to shake against the cut. He pressed harder until it stopped.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He was kneeling beside the dead scout again when Neela reached him.

She had run the whole length of the narrows. Her left hand was clamped to her upper arm in the same place as his, and her hair had come out of its knot. Pavi was two paces behind her, with her bad paw of a hand held out as if to catch something.

"It's nothing," Devan said.

"I felt it open." She dropped beside him and peeled his fingers off the cut. When she pushed back her own sleeve, a welt ran from her shoulder to her elbow, red and weeping, mirroring his. She looked from one to the other, and her mouth went tight and level. "Shallow. Good." Then, quieter: "You killed one."

"Yes."

"Openly."

"In front of forty men."

She said nothing to that. She took the strip of cloth from her sash and bound his arm and her own, one after the other, with quick sure turns.

"Look at his wrist," Devan said.

He had pushed back the dead man's cuff before she came. He had meant to look for a tally, or a brand, or a mark of rank. What he found stopped his breath.

Pale, ridged lines ran across the inside of the wrist. They were old and thick, laid over one another. Every odiyan in the Company wore the same pattern.

"Thilak," he said.

The tapper came over, wiping his hands. He crouched. He turned the arm over and put his nose close to the skin, as Devan had done, and breathed in. Then he wiped his palm on his mundu. He looked at the wrist, and wiped it again.

"Salt," Thilak said. "Clove-bark. Dry and sharp." He straightened. "Not nightbark. There's no tamarind in it, no scorch. I've smelled nightbark on my own hands for twenty-two years." He frowned at the dead wrist. "I don't know this tree. I'd say it's been put on this man more than once, though. And a long time."

Devan looked at Neela. She was staring at the wrist.

"The one by the tower," she said. "Same smell. Same scars."

"Same *kind*," he said. "We don't know what he is."

"A Thurai who burns himself."

"Or a man who was made to." He did not know what a Thurai odiyan would mean. It might mean nothing. It might mean that every fear he had tried to count was too small. He put it down in his mind next to the grey cloak and the yellow bird on a tailboard, and he did not build on any of them. "We'll hold it. We won't guess."

Thilak went to tell Kandan. Vellan had been standing a little way off, saying nothing, with his back to them. He turned his head once, looked at the scarred wrist, and turned away again. His shoulders had gone strange.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The purse on the dead man's belt held eleven coins.

Devan poured them out on the flat of a stone. They lay in a small bright heap, with the sprig on one face and a sharp milled edge round every rim. They had not been handled. He had seen coins like them in a satchel once, under the lip of this same cut, and had taken them for evidence.

"New silver," Neela said.

"Varanad's."

"Look at the sprig." Her voice had dropped. She reached into her sash and drew out a cord with a single coin knotted on it. It was bright, too. "Parukutty's. Rema paid it to her. I traded her a worn panam for it, and I told you I was being sentimental."

"You said you wanted to weigh it."

"I weighed it. It weighed the same as the scouts' coin, to a grain." She laid it beside the heap. "Turn them over."

He turned over three. The lowest leaf of the pepper sprig was doubled on each, a small stamped stutter, as if the die had slipped when it struck. He turned over the fourth, and the fifth. Then he picked up hers and held it to the light.

The same doubled leaf.

"One die," he said.

"One die." Her voice was careful. "The scouts had it in spring. This man had it now, and the widow's gift. Rema's coin, from the crown's own chest." She breathed out. "The crown paid them."

"Once."

"What?"

"The crown paid them once," Devan said. "The coin tells us where it was struck. It doesn't tell us who put it in his hand." He set her coin down beside the others. "Rema's chest was filled from the same mint as the scouts' purse, and that's all we have. The mint is the crown's. Everything else is a guess." He looked up at her. "Who pays them *now*?"

She held his gaze. He saw her turn it over, the way she turned over a door before she crossed it.

"Someone who has the die," she said at last, "and the tally book and the cart with a yellow bird. And a man who wore a grey cloak to Muthu's door the winter Asha died."

"And grey is cheap."

"And grey is cheap." She let it go. It was not quite a smile that crossed her face, but it was kin to one. "You'd make a good clerk."

"Rema says I'd make a bad one."

He gathered the coins back into the purse and tied it. He took a long breath. Then he said what he'd carried for days, since the night at the Resin House, when he'd lain awake and listened to the wounds on his body rise up on hers.

"Neela."

"Yes."

"I killed a man today. First. I took a cut for it, and you took the same cut." He kept his eyes on her face. It was harder than the spear had been. "It's not the first wound I've put on you. I put the Company on this shelf, and the Company is on the shelf because I decided, and every fight I choose is chosen for your body as well. You never agreed to it. I let it sit because I didn't want to hear no."

She did not blink.

"So I'm asking. Straight." His voice stayed even. He didn't know how. "Do you consent? To what my war puts on you. The cuts, and worse than cuts. Because I'll hold this pass whether you say yes or no. I'm not asking so I can feel clean. I'm asking because you have the right to say it to my face, and I've been careful not to give you the chance."

A wind came down the gorge, carrying dust and the smell of crushed lantana, rank and green. Pavi had retreated twenty paces and was studying a pony's hoof with enormous attention.

Neela looked at the coins, and at the dead man's wrist, and at his bound arm and hers. She drew in a breath.

"Devan." Her voice was low and sure. "I chose that the day I—"

A horn sounded.

It came down the gorge from far above, from the gate of Kettamala, a mile and more up the cut. It rolled along the cliffs, and the langurs on the east face went silent all at once. Two long notes. A short. A long.

Not one long and two short. Not unarmed strangers.

Bhaskaran's code for the queen's riders.

Neela's mouth was still open on the end of her word. Devan was already on his feet, with the purse in his fist and the cut on his arm bleeding through the cloth. He was thinking, absurdly, of Muthu. He was thinking that Muthu held that gate.

# Chapter 9: The Unsealed Letter

"Said it," Neela panted, "the day I—"

"Climb first," Devan said. "Finish it at the top."

So she climbed. The cart road up from the lower narrows ran a mile and more, and they took it at a half run, with Pavi and the rest of the six behind them and the pony's hooves clattering. The horn had not sounded again. She counted her steps instead. Four hundred and twelve to the first bend. The wounds on their two arms pulled at each other with every stride.

She had not finished the sentence. She knew how it ended. I chose that the day I said the word to you. But there was no breath left in her for it, and she was glad. Some answers wanted to be said standing still.

At the padippura the gate stood open to its width. In the yard sat a grey horse with a green sash across its saddle and a boy holding its bridle as if it might bite. The boy was Kannan. Beside him stood Pepper, the white goat, studying the saddle with the face of a creature planning.

The rider was a thin man in a dusty green sash. He stood on the gate chamber stair with a leather case in one hand, and Muthu stood two steps above him with his arms folded.

The Company had come into the lower court from the barracks side, forty-odd people in drovers' cloth, very quiet. Garrison men filled the rest of the yard. Neela found Kandan's face, then Janu's at the kitchen door, then Bhaskaran's. Everyone was listening.

"By the crown's order," the rider said, in the voice of a man who had said it three times already that morning, "Captain Muthu, holding the seal of Kettamala, is to bring forty men of the garrison to Kodiyur for the harbour wall. Kettamala's walls to be kept by twenty. The captain to ride at the head of the forty. Departure at first light tomorrow."

He held out the case.

Muthu took it. He broke the green wax with his thumb and unrolled the leaf. He read slowly, with his lips moving on the hard words. Neela watched his hands. They did not shake.

Halfway down the leaf his thumb stopped on one line. He read it again. His eyes dropped to the brass-cornered pouch at his belt, and came back up. Neela marked it and put it down to the hard words. The clerk's hand was round and crabbed, and she would have stumbled on it too.

Then he finished, and went still. The stillness came from the chest outward, as if someone had lowered a lid.

Muthu was loud. He laughed from the belly and cursed the weather, the cook, the pass, and himself with the same relish. She had never seen him silent for longer than a breath. Now the yard waited on him, and he said nothing at all.

"He's lying," a man behind her muttered.

No, Neela thought. She had seen Muthu lie, at the feast, over a letter. That quiet had been thin and quick, like a hand drawn off a hot pan. This one was heavy. It had two weights in it.

On one side were six years of grey wax and a woman held in a household somewhere. On the other, Devan in the yard with a bound arm, and forty men at the shelf who had not found his order interesting.

He was not hiding a choice. He was standing between two. She knew exactly what that looked like from inside.

Her first impulse was knife-fast. She could drop a pot, start a quarrel, make the rider's horse bolt, and buy an hour. She dismissed it in a heartbeat. Any hour she bought him would be an hour in which he had not chosen. The crown would call it a plot, and it would stick to Muthu for good.

Don't push, she told herself. Let him stand there.

Devan had stopped at her shoulder, breathing hard from the climb. She kept her eyes ahead. She felt him decide, as a person feels a change in the wind.

He stepped out into the yard.

No one stopped him. He was not the warden, and no one called him by it. He walked to the middle of the court where the sun lay on the stones, a man in a sweat-dark shirt with a blood-stiff sleeve, and spoke to Muthu on the stair.

"I need nothing from you."

The yard went still.

"I'll tell you the cost, so that you choose with your eyes open." He did not raise his voice. He never had to. His eyes went once to the rider, and came back to Muthu. "There's a host three days off and the Company on the road between. This morning we lost Biju, who was seventeen. If you take forty men south, the road has fewer. If you keep them, it has more. I'm not asking for either. The seal is yours, and the oath on it is yours. I wanted you to know what stands on the other side of it."

He stopped. He did not say please. He did not say remember. He never glanced at the garrison to see how it landed.

Neela noted what he had left out. Not where the cut was, nor how deep, nor when it would come down. He had given the rider's ears a price and nothing a rider could carry south. She thought of the coin with the doubled leaf, and her chest eased a little. He was still counting.

He turned his back on the stair and walked to the edge of the yard, and stood, with his hands open at his sides, to wait. It was the hardest thing she had ever seen a man do with his hands.

Muthu stood motionless a moment more.

Then he said, quite quietly, "Bhaskaran. Give the rider a cup of buttermilk and a stool in the shade. He'll want an answer in writing."

"Yes, Captain."

"And find me the black wax," Muthu said. "Not the grey. The black."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The gate chamber over the padippura was small and hot. It smelled of lamp smoke and tallow. A table stood under the shuttered window with a stone inkwell on it, and above the table, on a peg, hung the iron seal of Kettamala. The disc was cut with a hill and a gate and had been worn by eleven wardens. Muthu had hung it there to write; it had ridden on his cord since Devan put it in his hand in the hall.

She had followed because no one had stopped her. Devan stood at the back, against the wall. Bhaskaran watched the stair. Muthu sat down and drew the ink toward him.

He wrote for a long time. Neela did not stand where she could read it. She watched the pen instead. It did not stall once.

When he finished, he sanded the page and folded it in half, and no further. He took a stick of black wax from Bhaskaran, warmed it at the lamp, and let one dark bead fall at the corner. He lifted the iron disc down from its peg. His hand shook as he brought it to the page, and he waited until it stopped. He pressed. Hill and gate came up clean in the wax, plain as a coin.

Beside the inkwell lay the grey stick he had carried for six years. He did not look at it. It lay where it was.

"Come down," he said.

They went down together. The rider was on his stool in the sun with the buttermilk in his lap. Muthu held out the letter open, with the fold lying flat across his palm.

"Read it," he said. "Here, in front of the yard. I'd rather you didn't carry it blind."

The rider glanced at him, then read. It took him half a minute. Neela read over his shoulder, because she was close enough, and because Muthu had asked.

From Captain Muthu, holding the seal of Kettamala in trust, to the crown's household. I have your order of this day. I keep my men at the pass. I swore to hold this seal for the border and the villages, and that is the oath I answer to. A host is on the coast road and this garrison stands between it and the hill farms. When the road is clear, I will bring the forty to Kodiyur myself and lay the harbour wall brick by brick. Not before. This letter is open. Let any man who wishes read it. —Muthu.

Black wax. Hill and gate. No grey.

She let out a slow breath. Around her the yard had begun to murmur, a low and ragged sound like a field of barley turning.

"The household will not like this," the rider said.

"No," said Muthu. "Tell them I said so."

He sounded like himself. The loudness was back in it. It was not quite a laugh, but it lived in the same house.

The rider folded the letter along its crease. He went to his horse and gathered the reins, and then he stopped and swore. Pepper stood at the saddle with her neck stretched. The green ribbon that had tied the order case lay in the dust, and the goat was eating it, steadily, in long silk strands that hung from her mouth like a fisherman's moustache.

"Get away, you filthy—"

Pepper bit him on the hand.

The yard broke. A gasp came first, then a snort, and then Kandan, at the back, let out a bark that set off Vasu, and the whole garrison went up together. Kannan clapped both hands to his face. The rider swore again, in a thin high voice. The goat went on chewing, with an expression of perfect virtue.

Neela laughed until her side hurt. Beside her, an arm's length off, Devan laughed too. She had not heard it in days. It was low and short and a little hoarse, as if it needed oiling.

The rider mounted with a dignity he did not have. He rode out under the padippura with Muthu's open letter in his fist and one hand wrapped in his sash.

"Thirty to one that goat is stew by evening," Bhaskaran said.

"Take the wager," Janu said from the kitchen door. "I'll hold the stakes."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Supper was rice and a hot green curry in the long hall, with the Company along one wall and the garrison along the other and the middle left empty by a kind of courtesy. Janu moved between the tables with the pot braced against her hip.

She set a bowl before Devan, then Muthu, then Thilak. When she came to Neela she put the bowl down on the board without looking at it. Then she put down a second bowl beside it.

"For whoever comes after," Janu said, to the air. "There's always someone."

Her eyes stayed on the pot. She did not speak to Neela. She went on down the board, her bandaged hand tucked in against her ribs as if she were holding something small.

Neela sat looking at the second bowl. In all her weeks in this house she had never seen Janu set out a bowl for someone who might come after. She understood it. Forgiveness would have come with words. This was a door left on the latch.

She ate slowly, with both bowls in front of her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Muthu found her afterward on the west landing of the barracks, where the light from the kitchen made a yellow square on the boards. He had a cup of toddy in one hand and had not drunk from it.

"A word," he said. "Not here. Come along."

He took her to the lee of the water jars. The cistern chain clanked the hour above them and they let it finish.

"You watched me in the yard," Muthu said.

"I did."

"You didn't say a thing." He rubbed his jaw. "You could have. A word to Devan, a nudge. I've watched you move people by a hair, my lady. You stood still."

"You weren't lying."

"No." He said it heavily. "I wish I had been. Lying's easy." His eyes slid to the toddy and back. "That wax is dry by now. Somewhere a woman is kept on my silence, and I've just spoken. I don't know what they'll do with that. I'll say the rest of this while I can still say it. You're going to Kodiyur."

"Yes."

"Into the palace, if the door Usha spoke of opens."

She said nothing. He nodded as if she had.

"Then listen. The escort were three days in the south range, and every night they took a cup at my table. I counted their cuffs, because that's what I do with men I don't trust. Look." He set the cup on a jar and pulled his own sleeve to the wrist. "Buttoned to the knuckle. Two pegs, never one. A stiff cotton band, pale green. They don't push the sleeve up to drink or to wash. They turn the wrist in toward the body when they take a cup." He showed her. The motion was neat and soft, a habit worn smooth. "If a cuff slips they don't snatch at it. They close the hand and let it lie."

She watched. She had seen all of it before, in the First Guard's hands, and had not read it.

"There's a surgeon at the palace," Muthu said. "I don't know her name. The escort's second was drunk on the last night, and he said it as a joke. She pares the guards' wrists each season. Takes the ridges down with a thin blade, until there's one flat seam and no more. She keeps their throats pasted with lime and ochre." He looked at her sleeve. "She'll know your wrists before she knows your face. Whatever else you are, you won't pass at an arm's length."

Neela opened her hand in the lamplight. The ridges stood thick and layered, the way a tree shows its years. Eleven years of nights, burned and scarred again. She had never been ashamed of them.

"Thank you," she said.

"Don't." He picked up the cup at last and drank. "I wrote that letter today with the whole yard looking. I'd like one thing in this fort that I did on purpose." He wiped his mouth. "That's all. Go on. He's waiting at the cistern, and he's been waiting an hour, and he'd sooner swallow his spear than say so."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Devan sat on the rim of the cistern housing with his sleeve rolled and the bandage off. The cut on his arm had closed to a long dry line. Hers had closed with it, a pink thread from shoulder to elbow. They had both stopped bleeding at the same time, without saying a word about it.

"The yard didn't want you to apologise for the plan," she said.

"I didn't."

"I know. I was there." She sat beside him, a hand's width off. "I'm finishing something."

He turned his head.

"On the road. 'I chose that the day I said it to you.' That's the end of the sentence. I chose it, and I choose it again, and I'll tell you if that changes." She breathed in. "But that isn't what I came to say."

"Say it."

She had rehearsed nothing. She picked at the edge of her bandage. "When I came here, I thought you didn't touch me because you were afraid. Of me, of what I'd do. Of yourself. I counted your restraint as fear, because mine was." The chain clanked above them, one, two. "I watched you stand in that yard today with your hands open and ask for nothing, and I knew I'd had it wrong. You hold your hand back because the thing it would take belongs to someone else. You ask, and then you wait. You asked me about the cuts. You asked Muthu for nothing."

"Neela—"

"The kindi is full every night with the spout turned left, because I reach left-handed. The knife under my mattress, you've known since the second week, and you never moved it. I read both as a hunter's patience. They were you keeping your hand off what was mine." She shook her head. "Nobody did that for me. They had a number, and they had a leash." She made herself lift her eyes. "I'd like you to hear it said. That's all."

He didn't answer.

He looked at his hands. He turned the scarred right one over on his knee and looked at that too. A long time went by. The langurs in the east cliff started up again, one by one.

"I haven't anything ready for that," he said at last.

"I know."

"I thought I'd have something."

"I know," she said, and found that she was smiling. "That's why I said it."

He gave a sound, short and rough, halfway to the laugh he had given the goat. He did not reach for her. He moved his knee until it touched hers, and left it there, and let her decide.

She leaned.

It was not enough. It would do for tonight. She would be gone by first light, up a hill road, down a coast, into a palace where a surgeon with a thin blade was waiting for wrists like hers.

How, she thought, and held her hand up in the dusk, and looked at it for a long time.

# Chapter 10: Before the Road

Rain came onto the south range after dark, thin at first and then steady, drumming the tiles over the guest room like a hundred fingers counting.

Neela sat on the edge of the cot with the leaf of resin open on her knee. Six pinches, black and glossy, folded in a square of banana leaf. Six for the road. The Company had voted them, hand by hand, and she had been the last to raise hers.

She did not fold the leaf shut. She said it aloud, because said things were harder to cheat.

"One pinch saved if I stay human tonight. One bare night on the count." She touched the first fold. "The ache comes a moon after the last turning, and Nellimala was the last. So tonight costs nothing yet."

"Yet," Devan said from the door.

"Yet." She tipped her head at the leaf. "Kodiyur is roofs. Palace roofs, sea wind, a cliff. Every roof is a night, and every night is a pinch. If I turn tonight, I'm one roof short in the palace." She folded the leaf. "So I stay a woman. It's a cheap night. The expensive part comes later, and I'm writing it down now so I can't call it a surprise."

He came in and shut the door. The lamp on the sill threw his shadow long across the floor. He had washed. His hair was wet, and the grey in it showed at the temple.

"You've never spent a pinch for me before," he said.

"I haven't spent one. I've saved one." She laid the leaf under the pillow. "It's an old guild habit. Name the debt before it names you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He sat on the stool, not beside her. She marked it and filed it, then laughed at herself, because she had stopped filing his distances weeks ago. Twenty-seven years, and she had never once chosen a night slowly. She meant to learn it before the road.

"Say it plainly," she said. "What it does. Once, before we go."

"The share." He rubbed his thumb along the old white line at the base of his other hand. "You'll be in the palace, climbing. Walls, drains, the cliff stair. Anything that cuts you opens on me as a welt. At the shelf I'll be fighting, and you'll carry my cuts. They'll come at the worst moment. You'll be hanging from a ledge with a guard below and feel a spear go in my thigh."

"A welt," she said. "Not the spear."

"A welt, if it's a cut. If it's worse—" He stopped. "You know the rest. You felt the poison."

She did. She remembered the tearing along her own side in the Resin House yard, when his ribs had gone. She remembered not understanding it and understanding it all at once.

"Then we say what we do when it comes," she said. "Before it comes. So neither of us has to decide in the dark."

"You first."

"If I bleed, you don't come." She said it flat, like a count. "You've a pass to hold and thirty-six who'll die if you leave it. A welt on your arm means I scraped a wall. It does not mean I'm dying. If it's dying, you'll know, because it'll be your own breath failing, and you still don't come, because by then it's four days and you'd arrive for the burying."

His jaw worked. "And you?"

"You bleed, I don't come. I stay in the palace roof until the work's done." Her voice stayed level, and she was proud of it. "Whatever opens on my arm, I finish the job."

"Unless the vote sends me."

She looked up.

"The Company's hands put me at the gorge," Devan said. "If the hands send me somewhere else, I go. That's the only door I leave open." He met her eyes. "I won't promise you more than I can keep. I told you that once."

"You did." She nodded slowly. It stung, and it was right, and she would not have trusted him if he had said anything else. "Then say it again, so I know it's one bargain and not two."

"If I bleed, don't come," he said. "If you bleed, I won't. Unless the vote sends me."

"Unless the vote sends you."

They each said it twice, as they had with the ledger. It was becoming a ritual, which frightened her more than a knife would have. A ritual meant you expected to need it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"We'll want a signal," she said.

He had already thought of it. She could tell, because he looked at her, then at the lamp on the sill, and did not speak. He was waiting to see if she would get there by herself.

"Noon," she said. "Every noon. A prick, so small it's nothing, so nobody could mistake it for a fight." She dug in the pouch at her hip. "I've thorns. Lime thorns, from the bush by the kitchen well. Three each."

She laid them out on the blanket, dark green, as long as her little finger and sharp as stitching needles.

"Three pricks on my thumb at noon," she said. "Three welts on yours."

"And I've the tooth." He reached inside his shirt and drew out the leopard canine on its cord. It was yellowed, no longer than a thumb joint. She had moved it from her waistband to his neck herself, the last night at Kettamala, and he had not taken it off since. The guild had meant it for his wound. He wore it against his chest.

"Three small welts means alive," Neela said.

"Three means alive."

"Fewer means—"

"Fewer means I'm busy. Not that you're dead." He frowned. "None is the thing I won't think about."

"Then think about three." She picked up a thorn and held it out. "Practice. Now. So neither of us panics at the first one."

He took the thorn. He did not prick himself. He held it between finger and thumb and looked at her, the rain loud on the roof.

"Do it," she said.

He pressed it to the pad of his thumb, once, twice, three times. Three beads of red. She did not look at his hand. She watched her own, laid open on her knee. Three small stinging dots rose on the ball of her thumb, bright and hot.

She breathed out.

"That's what alive feels like," she said, and was surprised how thin her voice came.

He put the thorn down. He crossed the floor, knelt on one knee in front of the cot, and took her hand in both of his. He put his mouth to the three red dots, one after the other. She felt it on his thumb too, a warmth on a wound he did not own.

"Still goes both ways," she said.

"Still."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She pushed him back onto the blanket and followed him down. He let her. His hands stayed open on the cloth, as in the yard, until she put them where she wanted them, and then they were not careful at all. She kissed the corner of his mouth, the hinge of his jaw, the grey at his temple. She went slowly. She had decided to be slow, in this one room, before the road. She was good at fast. She was done with fast.

His shirt came off over his head and she sat back to look.

Eight years of drilling alone had made him lean and hard, and he wore it the way another man wore a coat. But the rest held her eye. The white seam at his ribs from the ford, puckered and shiny. The scar through his brow. Seven flat pale marks across the knuckles of his right hand. The ropeburn on his palms from the goat-path rope, healing in stripes.

"Let me," she said.

He didn't ask what. She laid her fingers on the seam and followed it from the ribs to the hip. Then the brow, the knuckles, each mark, one at a time, the way she had once learned every servant in a strange house. She named them in her head. Ford. Stick. Wood. Stone. A man who had stopped hitting anything that hit back.

"You're counting," he said, very low.

"I'm learning." She bent and put her mouth on the seam. His breath caught in the hitch she had listened for all those nights, a third of the way through the long count. "Ford," she said into his skin.

"Neela."

"I know where they are now. All of them. If anyone opens one, I'll know it's yours."

He made a sound she had not heard from him. It broke off in the middle. She felt it in her own chest, as if she had made it herself. When his hand slid up her ribs and found her, warmth ran from his palm to hers and doubled back. She gasped. She could not tell whose skin was whose.

"It still does that," she said, unsteadily.

"It does." His voice had gone rough. "I don't think I'll get used to it."

"Good."

She laughed against his shoulder, helplessly, and then she stopped laughing.

She led for a long time. She took him in and held still and let it be slow, and watched his face, which he did not hide. He looked at her the way he looked at the whole fort from the wall-walk, as if it were a thing he had decided to keep. When his hands tightened on her hips, she bent and whispered against his ear, "Now you."

He turned them over with great care. He did not rush. He asked with his eyes first, and she answered with hers, and then he moved, and the room narrowed to the creak of the cot and the rain and the heat of him. Whatever he felt, she felt too, an echo rolling down her spine and out into the dark. She told him so, in broken pieces, and heard him say her name like a word he was learning.

At some point she set her teeth in his shoulder. She did not break the skin this time. She pressed hard enough to leave a pink crescent, and a matching crescent bloomed on her own shoulder, hot and sweet.

They both stopped. They looked at each other.

"Last time that was play," she said.

"Last time you drew blood."

"Tonight it's practice." She grinned up at him, wild and breathless. "Bite me. Then don't move."

He bent his head and bit the curve of her neck, gently. Pain flared on his own, and he lifted his head, startled, and she saw him wait for the old reflex to rise. The one that said hurt meant help, that his body should already be gone, up the stair, out the door. His hands braced either side of her. His whole back shook with holding still. He stayed.

"Not a summons," she said.

"Not a summons." It came out like a vow.

"Again."

He laughed, shaken, and did.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Afterward the rain was still going. She lay with her head on his arm, the blanket kicked to the foot of the cot, a pinch of cool air from the window across her legs. His heart beat under her ear, slower now. The lamp had burned down a thumb's width.

He spoke into the dark. He did not usually begin things. She waited.

"I'm not afraid of dying," Devan said.

"I know."

"I thought you might think so. It isn't that." His chest rose and fell. "I'm afraid I'll stand at the shelf with the line bending, and a welt will open on my arm that is yours, and it'll be the kind that means you're on a ledge with nobody below you. I'll know it. I'll feel it as a fact. And I'll have to pick." He did not say between what. "Your welt, or the line."

She lay quite still.

She had expected a lot of things from him. A comfort, an apology, a vow. Not this. It had cost him to say it, and she could tell he had said it in the plainest words he owned.

"Then choose the line," she said.

His arm tightened.

"That's what I married," Neela said. She lifted her head so she could see his face in the lamplight. "A man who holds. Not a man who runs after every bleeding thing. I know exactly what it costs. I've counted." She laid her palm flat on his chest, over the tooth on its cord. "I'll do the same. If your welt opens, I'll stay on my ledge and hate you, and finish, and come home. And you'll be at the door."

He said nothing for a time.

"You'll hate me," he said.

"For about a day."

"I can bear a day."

"I know you can." She put her head down again. "That's why I picked you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She woke at the middle watch with the rain gone. The room had that scoured quiet it gets after a downpour, when the tiles tick as they cool. Devan was asleep on his back, the hitch in his breath coming steady as a clock.

She slid free of him and went to the door for no reason she could name. She wanted to count it. She always counted the door.

She opened it a hand's width.

The corridor lamp outside was lit.

She stood and looked at it. It was a plain clay lamp, the kind that hung from a hook by the stair, and it burned with a low, even flame. Someone had trimmed the wick. Someone had filled the cup to the brim, so it would last until dawn without a hand near it. A smell of sesame oil hung in the draught. At the bottom of the stair, under the door of the north range where Muthu slept now, a line of yellow showed.

In the first weeks of the marriage the lamp outside the warden's door had been kept dark, and a boy posted at the stair. The boy was Raiu. Nobody had said why. A dark corridor had been Devan's kindness, once, so that nothing could see.

Now Raiu was ash, and a lamp burned in a corridor, and nobody had said a word.

She knew whose hand. She had watched that hand set down a second bowl at supper and look at the air instead of her. There would be no word about it in the morning, and no thanks possible. It was a door left on the latch.

Neela touched the cool clay of the lamp with one fingertip.

"Thank you," she said, to the corridor.

She went back in and shut the door gently, and left the flame burning.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They rode out at first grey.

Five waited in the lower court with the horses, and the pack pony stood among them, looking put upon. Pavi had a spear across her saddle and her curled forepaw of a hand tucked in her sleeve. Thilak sat his brown cob like a rock set on a pillar. Madhavi, Kuttu and Meenu were pale and quiet, with the look of people who had said goodbyes the night before and did not mean to say more.

Janu stood at the kitchen door, her hands wrapped in her apron. She did not come across the court. She nodded once, short, and went back inside.

Devan stood by the mare. He had not slept much. He held out a leaf packet of cold rice and curd, and a folded cloth, still warm from the brazier.

"For the first night," he said. "In case."

"I've a cache plan for the road."

"In case." He put the bundle in her hands and kept his own on it a breath longer than he needed to. Three small dots showed on the pad of his thumb, healed to pink. Hers had faded the same.

"Noon," she said.

"Noon."

She did not kiss him in the yard. She would have fallen apart. She mounted instead, gathered the reins, and rode out under the padippura with her back straight, counting the horses behind her until she had five.

The hill road dropped away in loops, wet and shining. Langurs barked on the cliff and then, as they passed, fell silent, and she put it down to the rain-sodden morning. A mile down the cut she heard the dull far boom of the gorge below, water running over the broken rock. The sun came up red behind the ridge.

An hour down the hill road, her left forearm began to burn.

It came slowly. First a prickle at the inside of the elbow, then a line of heat, thin as a thread, running from wrist toward the crook. She pushed back her sleeve with one hand and looked.

A welt was rising. Narrow, red, shallow, about the length of her hand. As she watched, it beaded at one end.

She knew where it came from. She knew it the way she knew her own number.

Far behind and above her, in the cut he had spent a week making into a bad day of stone, he was already fighting.

Her horse walked on. The mare's ears flicked back. Pavi, three lengths behind, said sharply, "Neela?"

"It's a graze," Neela said. Her voice came out level. "His. Shallow."

"Do we—"

"No."

She did not turn her head. She did not slow. Her fingers closed on the rein, and the welt pulled with the movement, and she let it. If I bleed, don't come. If you bleed, I won't. She said it to herself in the rhythm of the hoofbeats, once, twice. She counted the bends in the road instead of the welts, until the cut was gone behind her and the sea showed, a grey seam at the foot of the hills.

Another line rose, lower on the arm. Then nothing.

She rode down toward the coast with his blood hot on her wrist, and she kept the bargain.

# Chapter 11: Ask Me Again

The first ladder came out of the grey at the hour Neela's horse would be reaching the third bend of the hill road.

Devan saw it from the lip. It rose out of the scree below the shelf, lashed ship-timber with a hook of black iron at the head, and it swayed there until the hook bit the stacked stone of the wall. Behind it came another, and another. Along the whole slope men bent under timber, and the grey showed their bronze caps and the white of their eyes.

Three hundred, by the count he had made through the night. Seventy-five on the lip, now that Biju was gone.

"Hold," he said. He said it once, flat, as he had said it to eleven riders in single file under this same shelf in the spring. He did not shout. The men near him heard it and passed it on.

Kandan's volunteers held the right of the wall with their spears grounded and their faces set. The Company held the left, the odiyans in daylight cloth with spears in their hands. They were thirty-six now, all of them human at this hour, and none of them looked like what the night made of them. Thulasi had a salt-boiler's wrists and a stance she had learned from Kandan two days ago. Vellan stood at the far end where the wall bent, dragging his left leg a half step behind him, in a grip that had nothing of the kalari in it. It had been learned somewhere harder.

The hook bit. A man's head rose over the stone.

Devan took the spear in both hands and put the point through the gap under the man's arm. It was a clean thrust, and the man went back off the ladder with the press of the whole line behind him. He did not look to see where he fell.

The spear was a wall's weapon. It gave him a length of ash between himself and everything that wanted him. His right hand stayed on the shaft, the two fingers that could kill a man in a breath folded into the grip like a pair of knives he had decided not to draw. He had not drawn them in eight years. Not since the captain at the ford. Not now.

The second thrust took a shoulder. The third was parried, and the parrying blade came sliding down the ash and over his left forearm. It was a light stroke, almost polite. Heat ran along the skin, from wrist toward the elbow, and beaded.

He knew exactly where she felt it.

An hour out, he thought. The loops of the road, wet and shining. She would have pushed back her sleeve. She would not turn her head. She would count the bends.

He drove the butt of the spear into the man's face and the blade fell away.

"Left!" Kandan's voice, hoarse. "Left, they're over at the bend!"

Devan did not look. He took the next ladder on the point of the spear, set his shoulder to the haft and pushed. The ladder went over backward with six men on it, slowly, like a tree.

Somewhere under all of it he was saying: If you bleed, I won't.

He kept the line.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At midday the line bent.

He felt it before he saw it, through the soles of his feet and the drag in the men on either side of him. The Thurai had stopped trying the whole wall. They had found the elbow where it turned at the east end, the place Vellan held, and they were stacking men against it as water finds the low place in a bank.

Two hundred and sixty down, perhaps. Devan had stopped counting the dead and counted the living. Sixty-one on the lip that could still lift a spear. Of the Company, three had come off the wall with arrows in them and been carried back to the sheds. Kamalakshi was among those who had not. Her white face turned his way and then turned back. She was not forgiving anyone yet. She was fighting.

He did not think the line would break. He did not think it would hold. Ask him for a number and he would have said a quarter hour, and he would have been wrong either way, and the men would have known he had said it.

"Warden!" Thulasi called it from close by. She was bleeding at the temple and did not seem to know it. "They're through at the corner."

"I see them."

He went. He did not run. He put the spear between two men who were not ready for it, and for a long time there was nothing but the push and the grunt and the hot red breath of someone's face an arm's length off.

Then, behind and above, the horn.

It was Kettamala's horn. He would have known it with his eyes shut: a long plain note, no code in it, the rally call that Bhaskaran blew at festival gates and at funerals alike. It came down the cut from the fort, a mile of rock throwing it back, and it did not stop. It went on and on, until a bad horn-player might simply have been leaning on the thing out of spite.

The Thurai heard it. A ladder-man at the corner raised his head like a dog.

Down the cart road came thirty men in grey. They came at a jog in good order, spears level, and at their head ran Captain Muthu with his sword out and his mouth open, bellowing.

"Make way! Make way, you lot, escort coming through! Refugees!"

He ran straight into the gap at the elbow, where Vellan stood. He put his shoulder against a Thurai spearman and shoved. Behind him the thirty went into the line like a wedge into a log.

Devan said afterward that he did not remember how the next quarter hour went. He remembered Muthu's voice above it, bawling the same word over and over, with perfect joy.

"Refugees! Refugees on the shelf road! Mind the refugees!"

By the time the sun stood two hands past noon, the ladders had gone down to the scree and stayed there. Bronze caps went away along the slope, many of them dragging one another. Nobody on the lip cheered. They sat down where they were.

Muthu came over wiping his sword on a dead man's sash. His grey tunic was black from collar to belt. He had a cut across his chin and seemed delighted by it.

"There are no refugees," Devan said.

"It's in the post book." Muthu squatted beside him and looked at the line of living and dead with a face that had lost some of its roundness. "Black wax, plain ink, my hand: 'Captain Muthu sends thirty men down the shelf road to escort refugees from the pass villages.' The crown said the garrison stays inside the walls. It said nothing about what comes out of them to walk a woman and her goat home." His eyes were on the dead. "A pen is a good shield, my friend. Refugees are the reason I can write it down. If I'd written 'to fight,' I'd have had to hide the page."

"You'd have been hanged for the page."

"I'm still the one holding the seal." He grinned, a short mean grin. It faded. "I counted thirty of our men on the road up. I'd like to count them again later and not like the number."

"Bhaskaran?"

"Holds the wall with every cook who can lift a pole. Janu's got the pot on. I told her to feed anyone who looks like a refugee." Muthu got up with a grunt and held out his hand. Devan took it and was hauled to his feet. "And you owe me a seventh-day round."

"It isn't the seventh day."

"It's the seventh day somewhere."

Devan nearly laughed. His face had forgotten how, and all he did was breathe out through his nose.

"After the pass," he said.

"I'll hold you to it," said Muthu. "Out loud. In front of the cook."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At the evening count, three volunteers were dead.

Kandan brought the names on a broken roof tile, chalked in his own square hand, because the tile was what Neela had left in the barracks and the men had taken to it. Devan read them standing on the shelf with the last light on the stone.

Sankaran. Chami, who had been a drover. A boy from Kalladi called Rajan who had arrived with a spear taller than himself.

"Sankaran took one at the corner," Kandan said. "Before the horn. He was in front of Vasu. I don't think he saw it come."

"His mother's at Neerattu."

"She came back to the village after the husband died." Kandan did not look up from the tile. He still had not forgiven the warden for the order he'd defied, nor for being right about it. But he said, in a low even voice, "You'll write her. I'd write her myself, but my hand's a sergeant's. It goes to the point and stops."

"I'll write it."

He wrote it that night in the stone sentry hut at the back of the shelf, with a lamp and a board across his knees. He had Asha's trick of the board in his head and none of her hand. She had written his letters for him from the age of fourteen. He had said what he meant, in six words or less, and she had dressed it in a clerk's cloth and sent it off, and nobody had known it was Asha speaking from behind him.

Now there was only the stick of charcoal and a clean leaf.

*To the mother of Sankaran, at Neerattu.*

He looked at that for a long time.

*Your son held the corner of the wall on the second day. He was in front of the man behind him. He did not suffer long. I did not know him as well as I should have, and I will say what I do know. He carried water for the others without being told. He laughed at the goat. He wrote his name large.*
