He scratched out *large* and put *plainly*. He scratched out the next line, which began *He was a brave*, because Sankaran would have laughed at that too.

*I am sorry. I cannot give him back. The wall held because he stood where he stood. That is the whole of what I have.* He signed it with his name and no title, since he had none now. He sat with the leaf on his knee and thought that Asha would have fixed every line and made it worse.

He folded it small and gave it to Kandan for the next rider.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At noon the next day he was on the lip, and the sun stood straight above the shelf.

A small bright sting caught the ball of his left thumb. Then another, and another.

He stopped in the middle of an order, with the words half out. The men near him went on looking at the scree, because a warden stopping was not worth a look. He sat down on the nearest rock, a low flat boulder lichened over, and turned his hand up on his knee.

Three red beads, one beside another. Small, neat, a hand-careful row. They were thorn-sized, from a bush by a kitchen well.

Yesterday's noon had come and gone inside the line bending. He had felt a sting somewhere under his thumb with a spear in both hands and the wall giving way, and he had told himself two or three. He had told himself busy. All through the evening count he had not let himself ask which.

Three. Alive.

He sat there with the sun on the back of his neck and watched the beads fade from red to pink, and from pink to nothing. It took less time than he'd thought. It seemed a good deal longer.

He did not say a word, and nobody asked. When the last of the pink was gone he stood up, shouldered the spear, and went back to the wall.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


That night Kandan found him in the pit they had cleared behind the shelf, a patch of packed scree and a post set in stone. Devan had been drilling alone, because there was nothing else to do with his hands.

"Warden." Kandan stood at the edge in the dark, with a practice staff on his shoulder and a gourd in his fist. "You'll teach me the guard you used at the corner? Left foot back, shaft low?"

Devan looked at him. Months ago Kandan had called Neela a witch at the barracks door.

"Yes."

They worked in the dark by the light of one shuttered lamp. The staff cracked on the staff. Kandan had a drover's patience and a sergeant's anger, and both made him good. After an hour he was nearly as fast as Devan on the low guard, and Devan said so. Kandan looked as if he'd been handed something heavy.

"Rest," Devan said. He walked to the post at the end of the pit.

He had no reason to. It was the end of the drill, as he had ended every drill since the winter after the ford. Open hand, slow, from the hip. He took the form through the pivot and the turn and set the last stroke at the post, two fingers extended, aimed at a place on the wood that was not marked and did not need to be.

He stopped a hair short.

He held it there, the fingertips a thread off the grain, until his shoulder burned.

When he turned, Kandan was watching with the gourd halfway to his mouth.

"That's the one," Kandan said slowly. "The one from the ford. Muthu told us. Everyone knows."

"Yes."

"You used the spear at the corner. Three days, you've never used it. You had that in your hand every time, and a man came at you with a blade." He didn't sound accusing. He sounded like a man who has found a rock in his rice. "Why don't you use it?"

Devan lowered his hand. He looked at the two fingers, as though they might answer.

"Because it works," he said.

Kandan said nothing. The lamp ticked as the oil settled.

After a while he drank from the gourd and held it out. Devan took it. It was sour toddy, and it was the first time anyone in that fort had passed him a cup without being told to.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The days ran together after that.

The Thurai tried the ladders twice more and found the corner closed. They tried the back of the cut at night, with six men and a rope, and found Koman's old eyes waiting on the lip, and Thulasi's spear, and something that moved through the dark on four feet and was not looked at afterward. Devan kept to his orders. He did not watch the dusk on the shelf. He let the night belong to those who owned it, and in the grey he took the news from Vasu in a low voice with his back to the slope: three men taken, two ropes cut, a boat-sized timber dragged away.

Each noon he sat on the boulder. Three. Three. Three. On the fourth he found his hand shaking and put it under his arm until it stopped.

The burn on his forearm closed. The count of the lip went to seventy, then sixty-eight. A dead man's cloak became a bandage. Muthu's thirty settled into the line as if they had always stood there, and went home each night with wet faces to a fort that sent down hot rice in a cart. Once a goat was seen on the cart road, ahead of the rice, and Kandan swore it had been sent as an auditor.

On the fifth evening the Thurai herald came up the narrows.

He came alone, on foot, under a white rag on a pole, and he stopped a long bowshot below the shelf, on a flat of stone where the cart road bent. He was a small man with a big voice. He called up in a hill accent, so that no one could say they had not understood.

"Warden of Kettamala!"

Devan came to the lip. He stood in the red light, spear in his hand, and the whole wall behind him went still.

"The admiral sends this. He is a patient man. He has sat five days and lost his ladders, and he is tired of it." The herald paused for the echo to finish. "There are villages on this road. Seven are in his reach. At dawn he will burn the first. At the next dawn, the second. He will go on burning one a morning until the pass is open."

Nobody moved.

"The first is Kesu's. The one-eyed man's, at the foot of the goat path. The admiral hopes the warden remembers the way."

He lowered the rag. He turned and went down the narrows in no hurry.

Devan stood there while the last of the red went out of the stone. Kesu's was a half day below the shelf, behind the Thurai lines. There was a well in it, and a cattle shed, and an old man who had said *I'll hold the well* and meant it. It was thirteen hours to dawn.

Behind him, Kandan said very quietly, "Warden."

Devan did not turn round. He looked down at his left thumb, where nothing showed, and thought of the next noon, nineteen hours away.

"I know," he said.

# Chapter 12: The Noon Gun

The noon gun fired while the thorn was in her thumb.

Half the quay ducked. Neela, who had been waiting for it, only pressed. One. Two. Three small stings, bright as pinpricks of lamplight, and she dropped the thorn into the gutter and curled her hand shut. Four days behind her, in a cut full of broken ladders, a man would be sitting down on a rock.

*Three. Alive.*

The echo rolled off the harbour tower and out across the water. Kodiyur went back to its shouting.

Four days of road had gone by in a page of dust. The sea road was dry, and the horses were good, and she had counted the milestones because counting was what her mind did when the rest of her wanted to turn round. Pavi rode close and said little. Meenu snored under the cart. Every noon Neela got down in some roadside field, pricked her thumb three times, and climbed back up without a word. Nobody asked. By the second day Kuttu had started to look away at noon, the way you look away from a person praying.

Now the road had run out at salt water.

Kodiyur stank gloriously. Split pepper sacks, tarred rope, drying fish racked on poles to the horizon. A clove-and-wet-sack smell drifted from the north warehouses, where a Kunjan Chirayil had once kept a ward who never existed. Cranes of rope and timber leaned over the quays. Across the mouth of the harbour, a chain as thick as a man's thigh hung out of the water on its floats, with grey sails waiting beyond it. She counted eleven before she stopped.

Thurai, sitting off the mouth like crows on a fence.

Nobody on the quay looked at them for long. A porter spat. A fishwife crossed two fingers over her basket.

The five had gone in at the Salt Gate that morning behind a pepper cart, with Thilak driving and the rest in the sacks. They would lie in a salt store near the tanneries until she sent word. Neela had taken the Hill Gate alone. At first grey she had knelt over a pool in a gully and done her face and wrists.

Lime from the pass village, ochre from a potter's cutbank, mixed with water to a grey-gold mud. She had smeared it thick across her throat, over the ridged scars, and let it dry. It went chalky and tight. It cracked when she swallowed. Over her wrists she had buttoned two stiff cuffs of green cotton, starched with rice water, pegged at the knuckle the way Usha's were. The wedding bangle sat under the left one, a hard gold line against the bone.

*Don't touch it,* Usha had said, at the shelter. *Not once. A hand at the throat is a tell.*

Neela kept her hands down and walked.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The woman who sold dried mackerel at the third stall from the weighing house was called Rukmini, and her scale-boy was Pappu. Neela knew it before she reached them. She had stood at the next stall for a quarter hour, buying nothing, listening to the boy being scolded. The guild had never broken her of the habit. A woman who needed to stay unseen should know no one's name. She knew all of them.

"You're early," said a voice at her shoulder.

Usha wore her green sash at the left hip and a plain brown wrap over it, like any palace servant on an errand. Her sleeves were pulled to the knuckle. She didn't look at Neela. She looked at the mackerel.

"The roads were dry," Neela said.

"The chain's been up eleven days. Nobody's coming in by sea, and nobody's going out." Usha took up a fish by the tail, sniffed it, and set it down. "Don't turn your head. Two men by the salt-weigher. Port watch. They're counting who walks in from the hill."

"I counted them at the gate."

"Good." Usha almost smiled. "Walk with me. You carry yourself like a woman with a sword. Lose that."

They went up through lanes so narrow the washing hung across the sky. Neela dropped her shoulders. She let her stride go short and pinched. After forty paces Usha grunted, which she took for approval.

"The others call me First," Usha said. "I'll bring you to three of them. They'll want to see you before they decide anything. They won't use names."

"I will."

"You'll have to ask."

"I'll ask," said Neela.

A drying shed stood at the end of a lane behind the fish market, long and low, hung with split fish gone amber in the dark. Salt and smoke lay on the air like a second roof. Off-duty guards used it, Usha said. It was the one place in Kodiyur the palace did not listen. Nobody who valued their dinner went near it.

Neela went in and counted the doors. Two. A third in the back wall behind the nets.

Three people waited among the racks.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


A man sat on an upturned basket with his sleeves down to his knuckles and a face like a closed shutter. A woman a little older leaned on a post and looked at Neela's throat. The third, younger than both, sat apart on the floor with his arms round his knees. He had the sulky, scrubbed look of a boy who had been told to wait outside.

"Second," said Usha. "Third. Fourth. This is Seventeen."

"Neela," said Neela. "And you are?"

The man stared. "We don't—"

"I know. Tell me anyway."

A silence. Fish swung overhead on their strings.

"Sajan," the man said at last, as though handing over a coin.

"Lata," said the woman.

The youngest drew a breath. "Sanu."

"Sanu." Neela went down on her heels in front of him. "Usha says you've something to tell me."

He looked at the floor. His hands pulled at his cuffs. "I carried notes," he said, in a small cramped voice. "For a year. Not to the palace. Out. A man in a grey cloak gave them to me in the lower court. I sealed each one in a pot of salve, and I gave the pots to the ninth-day post rider bound for Kettamala. They were always for the physician at the fort. Eravi." He swallowed. "I only went up myself once, with the escort the queen's clerk sent. I had a blistered heel. I left a pot at his dispensary, as thanks, and I never read a note. I swear I didn't. I thought they were medicine orders. The Keeper's wife was sick."

"Which pot?"

"Green clay. Bark salve. It smelled of..."

"Scorched tamarind and pine sap," Neela said. "I kept the pot."

He flinched. Sajan looked at her sharply.

"I was glad of the salve," she said. "I'd have used it either way."

It did not seem to help. Sanu pressed his forehead to his knees. "A man died at Kettamala," he said, muffled. "On the stair, in the dark. I've done sums on it. The lamps. If I'd read one note..."

"His name was Raiu," Neela said. "And you'd have read one note. They'd have found another boy." She had said it often in her head, over Ammu. It was as flat and true as a floor. "Eravi's held. He's told us what he sent. You're the first of them to say it to my face. That's worth something."

He lifted his head. His eyes were red.

"What did the man in grey say?" she asked. "When he gave you the notes."

"Nothing. He never said anything." Sanu shook his head. "He just counted me. Every time. Like I was a coin."

*Counted.* She put it away with the rest.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The front door banged and a woman came in with a leather satchel. She was fifty or so, brisk and broad, grey hair scraped under a cloth, and she carried herself like one who has put her hand in a great many wounds and thought nothing of any of them. She looked at Neela once and past her.

"Thankam," Usha said, with no warmth and no coldness. "Palace surgeon. She sees us here. The infirmary has ears."

"Everything with a roof has ears." Thankam set the satchel on the basket Sanu had left. "Lata, your arm."

Lata rolled back a green cuff. The forearm beneath was a mess of angry red, split along the muscle by a bad scrape. Thankam washed it, humming, then opened the satchel and took out a knife.

It was small. A hand's length, the blade thin as a leaf and curved at the point like a claw. The kind of knife used for peeling fruit or paring a hoof. Neela watched it come out of the leather, and her whole body went still.

Thankam did not use it on Lata's arm. She laid it on the basket beside her gently, as one might lay a spoon. While she bound the wound she talked of the tide, and the price of linen, and the bad temper of the Keeper's widow, who still kept his window open in the rain. Her bare forearms showed under the rolled sleeves. There was no seam on them.

But Lata's other wrist had slipped from her cuff as she shifted. Neela saw it. A flat pale line, a finger long, no ridge to it, no layers. Smooth as a stitched silk edge. Somebody had cut away twenty years of burned skin and sewn the rest back neat.

Her own scars lay thick on the inner wrist, ridge on ridge, the first laid down at sixteen. She felt every one now, under green cotton.

"Looser at the wrist," Thankam said, without turning. Her eyes stayed on the bandage. "That cuff will chafe. It'll chafe worse on the next fortnight, on a hard wall."

It was said to Lata, and it was not. Neela kept her face still. After a moment, Thankam picked up her knife and wiped it, clean and unhurried, on a cloth.

"You'll want a fortnight to heal after," she said, to the room. "The palace is not generous with that." She shut the satchel and went out, humming, and the door banged behind her.

Nobody spoke for the length of a long breath.

"She's kind," said Lata, rewrapping her cuff. "People forget that. She is."

"She is," Sajan said. "She cut it off me with a clean hand and told me I'd feel better." His voice had gone flat. "I did feel better. That's the worst of it."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They sat on baskets. Usha poured water from a clay jar and gave Neela the first cup. Lata drank hers standing.

The thirty, Neela learned, were not one mind. She had hoped for it on the road without quite saying so. Within the first quarter hour she heard the shape of it: the thirty were close to evenly split, and not about courage.

"Some want their pages," Usha said. "They've heard what you did at the Resin House. Some have heard it as a rumour, some as a hymn. They'd go to you at a run if they thought the gate was open."

"And some want to stay," Neela said.

"Some love her," Lata said.

Sajan made a sound. Lata turned on him.

"I'm not a fool. I know what she is. I know what I am." She spoke quickly now, like one who had said it in her head a hundred times. "But she answers every widow's petition herself. In her own hand. Every one, from the border, from the pepper villages. When my brother's wife wrote about the death-gift, it came back in a week. A letter, Neela. Not a clerk's. I know her hand." She stopped. "That is the woman who kept me out of a kennel. That is the woman I'd be sending to a gallows."

"She also signs the writs," Sajan said.

"I know that too."

"She held the lamp," said Sajan. "Ask them that. Ask them what she held."

Neela looked at Usha.

Usha was quiet a moment, turning her cup. "She watches each of us turn," she said. "The first time. In her own chamber, with the shutters closed. She holds the lamp herself. She won't let a servant do it. She holds it high so she can see every moment, and then she sits the night through in a chair and says nothing at all. Not a word. At first light, when you're a woman again on her floor, she kneels down and tells you your shape."

"To you," said Lata. "Gently."

"So you know what you are," said Sajan. "And so she does. And so you're caged by the only person alive who's ever held a light to you."

"She's never said it at night," Lata said.

"She'd never have to start. She only has to open her mouth after dusk."

Nobody answered that. Neela felt her own scars drag under her cuffs. She thought of Chathu, a long time ago, sitting a girl down at a table. A kindness first. Then the lamp.

"Tell me about the sea stair," she said, because the room needed a different floor.

"It leads from the lower cells to the water gate," said Usha. "There's a landing halfway where the cliff bends. Two guards stand it. They change at the sixth bell." She answered at once, like a woman reading it off a wall. "The change takes sixty breaths. The new pair comes down from the top while the old pair goes up by the chain stair, and for the last twelve breaths there's a gap where no one stands the landing. It's twelve if the rain's off and nine if it's blowing."

Sixty breaths. Twelve. Nine. Neela noted it. It was an uncommonly exact thing to know, and Usha had said it with no pause to count. She turned it over once, the way you turn a coin to see which side the maker pressed hardest.

She did not ask.

She had a number for every door in this city already. If Usha wanted her to have this one, Usha could say why. In the guild, a woman who asked too early was a woman who got told lies.

*Later,* she thought. She put it at the back of the shelf, beside the green-sealed quire and the man in grey who counted children.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It came as she lifted the cup.

A line of fire opened across her back, shoulder blade to hip. It wasn't a prick or a scrape. It was long and hot, as if somebody had drawn an iron down her spine and then down it again.

She did not drop the cup. She set it on her knee, very carefully, with both hands. She breathed through her teeth.

*Devan.*

Not a graze. A blade, or a ladder-hook, laid across his back in a long clean pull. The welt rose under her shirt, stinging, pushing at the cloth. It went on being cut, a slow count of five, then six.

Then it stopped.

Nothing followed. No second line, no tearing. No pull toward the dark. Only the welt, swelling, and her pulse beating in it. Whatever it was, it had not been the kind that kills. It might still be the kind that does.

"Seventeen." Usha had put down her cup. "You've gone grey."

"It's nothing."

"Is it him?"

Neela gave no answer. Sajan's shuttered face turned toward her with a new look in it. Lata glanced from one to the other.

If you bleed, I won't. She had said it in a warm room with rain on the roof. She had said it twice, so that it would stay said. Now she sat on a basket in a shed full of fish, with her fingers dug into the starched cotton of her cuffs, and for a long, awful moment her whole body leaned toward the door. A hundred miles of road. Four days of it. She would arrive for the burying.

She did not stand.

"He's living," she said. Her voice came out odd and low. "If he weren't, I'd be on the floor. It's a cut. It's long. It isn't deep." She looked at her hands. "I can't send him anything. There's nothing to send but the thorns, and the thorns are for noon. Three, tomorrow. That's all he'll get."

"That's all?" said Sanu, in a frightened whisper.

"That's the bargain." She lifted her head. "It's a good one. It's holding."

Usha was watching her. For a moment Neela thought that watchful face went very soft and very sorry, and then it was gone, as if a hand had passed a cloth over it.

"There is another thing," Usha said slowly. "I wasn't going to say it till tonight."

"Say it now."

Usha looked at Sajan, who looked at the floor. She drew a long breath through her nose, like a woman about to lift something she knew was heavy.

"Chathu has asked for Seventeen," she said. "In his cell. Three days now, to anyone who'll carry it. He won't say why." She turned her cup a quarter turn. "He says you'd come."

# Chapter 13: A Gift Is the Start of a Bill

Usha went down the cell stair three steps ahead and did not look back.

Neela counted anyway. One hundred and four steps cut into the cliff, wet at every turn, and the sea working at the rock somewhere below like a slow tongue. The green cuffs rubbed her wrists. The lime paste on her throat had dried tight enough to pull when she swallowed. She wore a guard's boots from Usha's locker, a size large, and she put each foot down flat so they would not slap.

At the bottom a lantern hung from a hook. Under it sat a turnkey with a bowl of rice and a face the colour of tallow.

"Mani," Usha said. "The new girl. She carries his supper tonight."

Mani looked at the sash on Usha's hip, then at the paste on Neela's throat. He had seen both on a hundred palace guards. He went back to his rice.

"Eighth cell. The lamp's lit. He won't give you trouble." He wiped his fingers on his knee. "He gives nobody trouble. It's the worst of him."

"I'll stand the turn above," Usha said, low. "Two knocks if anyone comes down. Don't be long."

"How long is long?"

"Sixty breaths longer than you think." Usha's mouth twitched. Then she went back up into the dark.

Neela took the bowl and walked the passage. Eight doors, plank and iron, five of them dark and open on nothing. Seaweed smell. Lamp oil. Under it, faint, a thing she knew in her sleep: warm sesame and burnt sugar.

Her stomach turned over once, like a fish.

She stopped at the eighth door. It stood ajar. She counted her own breath to four, and went in.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cell was a cut in the rock, three paces by four. A cot, a bucket, a slit window with the sea behind it. A lamp on a ledge. And on the cot, with a grey blanket drawn to his chest, sat a man who had lost the softness he'd had at the Resin House.

Chathu had been round in the shoulder, and his hands had always moved as if smoothing a cloth. They still did. But the cloth had gone slack on him. His wrist bones stood out. Someone who did not care for it had shaved his hair close, and it showed the shape of his skull.

He looked up and smiled with his whole face.

"Seventeen," he said. "Come where I can see you. My eyes are not what they were."

She stayed by the door with the bowl in both hands. "Neela."

"Neela." He tried it as if tasting. "It suits you. I did not know you would keep it."

"You'd have known if you'd asked."

"Yes, child. That is the oldest of my faults." He nodded at the bowl. "Put it down. Mani will listen for the dish, not for us."

She set it on the floor. Her foot found the cell's one dry spot and stayed there.

"You asked for me," she said. "Three days."

"And you came." He looked pleased and a little ashamed of looking it. "I said you would. I said it to the steward, I said it to the guard. I began to wonder whether I was only talking to myself."

"I didn't come because you said."

"No. You came because you want something." He spread his hands, palm up, in the old gesture of a man laying out his accounts. "I would not insult you by pretending otherwise. Ask."

She had planned this on the road and in the shed and on every step of the stair. A bargain had two sides. She would not let him set the other.

"There's a leaf," she said. "The queen's. Thirty pages. I want to know where it's kept."

"Ah." Not surprise. Something nearer a sigh. "And how to reach it, I imagine."

"The way. Nothing else."

"Nothing else," he said gently. "Very well."

He told her, without hurry and without being asked twice, in the soft, patient voice she had heard all her childhood across a long table.

"This passage ends at the water gate. The sea stair starts there and climbs the cliff, a hundred and ten steps, roofed against the spray. Halfway up, a landing where the rock bends. Your friend's guards stand it."

*Usha's landing.* Usha had called it the way from the lower cells to the water gate. It was one stair, named from its other end. Neela filed it as true, and filed beside it that Chathu knew where Usha's pair stood.

"At the top, a door of black wood in a passage lined with screens. The queen's room is the third door beyond. I sat in it four nights. She had me write at her table, by the window. Beneath the window, a low chest. Red lacquer, a bird picked out in gold. A farm lock, of all things. She keeps the leaf in it. You opened worse than that at fourteen, I recall."

Her skin crawled. *Of course he recalls.* Then the rest of it caught.

"Four nights. You've been in the cells longer."

"These four nights past, she has had me brought up each evening, and sent me down again before the lamps burned low."

"By the stair you've just described."

"With a guard before me and a guard behind." He tilted his head. "You are thinking it is a strange road for a prisoner."

She was. A caged man did not walk to the queen's table by the queen's back stair unless the queen's own hand had opened every door on it. The way in was her gift too. Neela turned that over and did not like either side.

"And the queen?"

"Sits audience in the long hall at noon. From the gun till the water-clock turns." A thin smile. "The gun is a mercy. It shakes the whole rock, and for the length of it a footstep on that stair is the least sound in Kodiyur. A patient woman might walk the last flight inside one report."

Noon.

It landed in her like a stone in a well. Three small stings in her thumb, and a man four days away sitting down on a rock to count them. If she climbed under the gun, she would be on a roofed stair with her hands full of wall. There would be no thorns. Devan would sit on his rock and wait for a word that did not come.

He would not know if it was a day lost or a death.

"You've picked the one hour I can't spend," she said.

"Have I?" Chathu's eyes were mild. "I only said when the queen sits."

"You know what I do at noon. Usha's friends carried it to you, or you guessed it. You always guessed."

"I guess a great deal, child. I am not always right." He drew the blanket higher. "But you will want the gun. You will want it badly, when the time comes. I only mention it."

She said nothing. He did not need her to.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The tin sat on the cot beside his thigh.

She had known it was there since the door. A round flat thing of dull brass, its lid dented at one edge where it had once been dropped on a flagstone. She knew the dent. She had run her thumb along it eleven times, in eleven rooms, the night before eleven deaths.

He saw her looking. He did not hurry. He took the tin on his knee and thumbed the lid, and it opened with the small flat sound she remembered, and the smell came up. Warm. Still warm.

Somebody had warmed it. In a cell with no fire. She did not ask who.

"You always pay your debts, child," Chathu said. "It is the best thing about you. I wish I had taught you nothing else."

"What do you want for it?"

"Take a sweet."

That was all. He held out the tin in both hands, the way a man offers water. Nothing in his face asked for anything.

She had learned around the fifth tin what it was. A gift is the start of a bill. Not a trap, which has teeth you can see. A gift has a soft mouth. It is warm in your hand and sweet in your teeth, and a year later it is a ledger line in a good hand: *one kindness, given.* You never remember agreeing. At the Resin House they had never needed to say *you owe us.* They gave you a sweet, and you did the arithmetic yourself.

In the kennels she had refused to touch the tin. She had thought the refusing was a victory. It had been a way of saying, *I am still afraid of you.*

She could walk out now with the route in her head and her hands empty. Chathu would let her. He would smile and say, *Of course, child,* and the debt would sit on her anyway, because he had given first. A refused gift was only an unpaid one.

If she took it, she would at least know the size.

*Let it be a thing I picked up,* she thought. *Not a thing sewn on me.*

She crossed the dry spot, put two fingers into the tin and took one.

It was a small round sweet, the size of a thumbnail. Jaggery, sesame, a bit of grated coconut. Her fingers came away shining.

His hand was close to hers as she lifted it, and she looked at his fingertips because she looked at everyone's hands. They were stained. Grey-brown, dry as dust, flaking in the creases. Not black. Not tacky.

She knew resin-ink. It was black as a beetle's back and it clung to skin for days. She had never seen Chathu's hands without it. This was only ink. Common ink, the sort a clerk uses for a petition.

She put the sweet in her mouth.

It was perfect. That was the thing she could not forgive. Sugar breaking, sesame going to oil, the warmth of someone's hands still in it. For an instant she was ten years old on a bench with her feet not reaching the floor, and a big gentle man was telling her she had done well.

She swallowed. It went down like a coin.

"There," Chathu said softly. "That is all."

"It isn't."

"No." His eyes were wet. He did not hide it. "But it is all I will ask tonight. Paper is the least of it, child. You will see. But you have asked for the paper."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She put the tin back on the cot with the lid shut, so that her hands had something to do.

"Your fingers," she said. "No resin-ink."

"No." He turned his hands over, regarding them with mild interest. "The cells are poor in everything but quiet. They allow me a pot of common ink, and a reed, and a plank to lean on. The queen asked me for the forty again."

Neela went still.

"She sent the steward with a list on the second day, in a clerk's hand. The forty names, and beside each a blank." He said it in the tone he used for sums. "She would like them written fresh. A master who saw a girl turn can always write her again. I said that to you once, without thinking what it was."

*A master who saw you turn can always write you again.* She had stood at his door at twelve, and he had mended Kirri's rat-eaten page in a quarter hour.

"And you said?"

"I have not answered her."

"Not yet?"

"Not yet." He folded his stained hands in his lap. "I find that I am not in a hurry. A very odd thing, at my age."

She waited for the rest. A man who is angling for pity says more. Chathu said nothing. He sat in the lamplight with his shaved head and his uneven left foot drawn up on the cot, bare and grey against the blanket, and he was so small that she could have put her hand round his throat.

"You could write them," she said. "You've done it before."

"I could." He considered it. "The queen is not unkind. She asks and does not command. It lets one go on thinking oneself a free man." He lifted his eyes. "I held every key at the Resin House, Neela. I held every cage in this world that mattered to me. And now I sit here and understand that I am the last cage I hold."

It went into her like a splinter.

She had come down the stair expecting hatred, and she had it, it was all there. She remembered the tin in the kennel. She remembered the paddle coming down, and the resin burning on her throat. That man was real.

So was this one. The old man with his knuckles gone to string, who still folded his sweets in a tin.

Her hand had moved before she knew it. It had gone to the edge of the grey blanket, to draw it down over that cold bare foot. She stopped with the wool between her fingers. Her teeth hurt. She opened her hand and let the blanket fall.

*No,* she thought. *You do not get that.*

His eyes had followed her hand. He said nothing. That was almost worse.

"I wept for Ammu," he said quietly. "You should know it. I wept in this cell, the first night. I do not say it to be forgiven."

"Good," Neela said. "Then you know what it's worth."

He nodded as if she had paid him a compliment.

She turned to the door. Behind her the thing she had been avoiding was waiting, and she faced it with her hand on the cold iron.

She had taken a debt. For the first time since a stone and a pebble and a vow in a warm room, she had taken one alone, and she had told no one. It was a small debt, a sweet and a stair. She knew exactly how it would grow.

"The gun," she said, without turning. "You set the stair at noon on purpose."

"I set nothing, child."

"Then hear this, because you'll try to collect on it." Her voice came out flat. "If I climb under that gun, a man sits on a rock four days from here and gets no word. That's his cost, and it's yours to carry too. I'll say it to his face before I pay you anything. Not you. Me."

A pause behind her. When he spoke it was almost tender.

"Then you will have to say it soon," he said. "The queen sits every day."

She had her hand on the latch when she heard the boots.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


No knock. That was the first wrong thing.

Usha had said two knocks. Neela listened, and the stair above sent down nothing but the slow beat of the sea and, under it, one set of boots coming down. Unhurried. Measured. A good leather sole.

And a light. A lamp swinging on a hook; she saw its yellow tongue lick the turn of the passage.

She moved before she thought: back across the cell, behind the open door, into the angle between the plank and the wall. The lamp ledge threw its glow in a half circle, and the dark behind the door was thick and bare. She put her shoulders to the rock. She stopped breathing in the way the guild teaches, by counting.

One. Two.

Chathu did not look at her. His hands lay folded on the blanket, and he looked at the open door with the mild, attentive face of a man waiting for his supper.

The boots came to a stop outside.

The steward stood in the passage with a lamp held at the height of his shoulder. Through the crack at the door hinge Neela saw a slice of him: a grey cloak, a clerk's round shoulders, a hand that held the lamp so steadily it might have been nailed there. He did not come in. He stood where the light reached the cot and no farther.

*Grey.* A man in grey who counted children onto a cart. A man in grey who counted a boy like a coin.

"Master Chathu," he said. A dry, careful voice, round at the edges. "I did not wish to wake you."

"I am awake."

"So I see." The lamp shifted a finger's width. The glow slid across the floor and touched the edge of the bowl of rice. "Has she come?"

Neela did not move. Her pulse knocked in her ears.

Chathu looked at the steward for a long moment. His face was kind. It was the face of a man who had told her that the kennel was a mercy.

"Not yet," he said.

The lamp did not move. The sea breathed in the rock.

"The queen would wish to know when," said the steward.

"When she comes," Chathu said gently, "you will know it."

A pause. Neela counted it. Four. Five. In the dark behind the door she could see one hand of the steward's, and the lamp, and the stained fingers of an old man laid in his lap, quite still.

She did not know.

He might have looked past her, and kept her. A man who sells a debtor does it with exactly that face. Or he might have said *not yet* because he had taken a sweet's worth of her side, and meant to see the bargain through. Both were Chathu. Both always had been.

The steward's lamp stayed where it was.

# Chapter 14: The First Village

Vasu came up the cut at a run and did not lower his voice.

"Torches," he said. "On the lower road. A mile below the mill ford, coming up."

Devan was already on his feet. He had not been asleep. He had been sitting against the shelf wall in the black before grey, with yesterday's ladder-hook cut pulling across his back every time he breathed, watching the east for a colour it did not yet have.

"How many?"

"I stopped counting at sixty." Vasu bent over his knees. His mother kept ducks at Kalladi, and Kalladi had stopped answering. "Warden, the village. I got to the well. Thirty of them won't leave. Old Kesu sat on the curb and said he'd held that well sixty years and wouldn't be told by a boy with a spear. The rest won't go without him." He wiped his mouth. "And the fishwife. Ponni. She sat down in the dust beside him. Said if he stayed, she stayed. She'd done her running."

"Did you tell them what the herald said?"

"I told them. Kesu said a herald was a man who had never held a well."

Devan looked down the dark of the cut. The herald had named that village first to burn at dawn. Torches a mile below the ford meant the Thurai would reach the thatch at first grey. The goat path was half a day up and a hard two hours down in the dark, for a man who did not mind his knees.

He had thirty-six odiyans. He did not have one to spare.

"Wake the Company," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They came to the dead fire in ones and twos, hair flat on one side, blankets round their shoulders. Kandan's volunteers stood off to the edge. They were not part of the vote and knew it. Nobody asked them to be.

Devan said it plainly, because he had no other way to say anything.

"Two choices. We hold the shelf as we are, and the people in that village burn with their well. Or fifteen go down in the dark, in shape, and break the Thurai horse lines at the ford while the rest of us go round by the goat path and bring them out."

Nobody spoke. Somewhere a pony shifted.

"I want to go," he said. "I'll say that first, so you can weigh it. Now the cost."

He counted it on his fingers because he trusted his fingers more than his voice.

"One. Fifteen pinches of resin. We've no spare in this week. Tomorrow night's wall is fifteen shapes thinner for it." He folded a second finger. "Two. Those fifteen are the best of you. They turn back at first light below the village, human and bare, with a half-day climb ahead of them and the morning hunger on top. They'll be no use on a wall until dusk." A third. "Three. There are seventy horses in those lines and a hundred men by the torches. I'd put it at one chance in three that we all climb back whole. Someone pays in blood, and it may not be a life."

"Lower," said Vellan.

He sat by the embers with a half-carved bird in his hands. He did not look up.

"Lower, then," Devan said.

"I'd go anyway," said Vellan. "I'm good at lanes."

A dry sound went round the Company that was nearly a laugh.

"Not my order," Devan said. "Yours. Hands. Sally, or hold."

Kandan counted, since nobody could accuse Kandan of favouring anyone. It took a long time. Hands came up slowly and some came up and went down again. Thulasi's went up high and stayed there, and she met no one's eye. Kamalakshi kept hers in her lap.

"Twenty," said Kandan. "Sixteen."

Four voices.

Kamalakshi looked at Devan across the embers. "I voted hold," she said. "I'll take the wall. If any of you come back, you can thank me for the dull part."

"I will," Devan said, and meant it.

He turned to Kandan's men. They had gone quiet in the way soldiers did when they knew they would be asked something.

"Corporal, you and your ten come with me by the path. We go in behind the shapes and take the people out. This is my order, under the Company's vote, and I'll answer for it." He said it to all of them. "If it goes wrong, the name that goes first on your tile is mine."

Kandan's scarred brow moved. "It goes on the tile either way, warden. Question is whose goes under it."

"Then keep the tile dry."

Fifteen odiyans went down the slope to a flat of rock out of the wind. Devan turned his back on them. He had a reason and a better one: what they were was theirs to show, and he had not been asked. Behind him he heard the cork, the small wet sound of resin on skin, a sharp hiss through teeth. Then the bone noises, which he did not watch.

When he turned round the rock was empty. Cold air smelled of musk and of scorched tamarind.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Something went past him in the dark on the goat path, low and quick and warm as a hearth. Then another. He felt them as weather on his face. Kandan's men pressed against the cliff and said nothing, and the shapes went down and were gone.

Devan saw nothing of the horse lines. He heard them.

The first scream came from the ford, high and wrong, a horse that had met a thing it had no word for. Then the whole line took it up. Picket ropes cracked. A torch swung and fell and a second bloomed beside it, and in that jumping light he saw seventy horses come up the lane in one dark wave with their eyes rolled white. Behind them something huge and slow put a shoulder to a loaded wagon. It went over like a dropped plate. A raider's shout turned into something else.

Vellan's bear went through the picket like a man through a bead curtain. Where it passed there was a lane.

"Now," Devan said, and ran.

Spears slapped behind him. The lane stank of dung and crushed barley and fear. A raider rose in front of him with his sword half out and Kandan's spear took him under the arm. Devan did not stop. He struck once, a haft to a knee, because a knee on the ground was a man who stayed down. He kept his blade for when it was needed. It was not yet.

The village lay above the ford on a hump of red earth, a dozen roofs of thatch, a cattle shed, a well with a stone curb. Raiders already stood among the lower huts with torches. Two thatches were burning.

Grey had come into the east, thin as a knife. Behind him in the thorn the shapes had gone quiet, drawing off to wait out the light. He could not feel them go. He knew they had by the way the noise changed. In a little while there would be fifteen people down there in the brush, naked and shaking, and nothing he could do about it yet.

Kesu was sitting on the well curb with a billhook across his knees.

He was a small man with one eye gone to a seam. He looked at Devan with the other and said, "Late."

"Come down off that."

"I hold this well."

"It's held." Devan crouched in front of him, because shouting would not do it. "It'll hold if you're dead. It'll hold better if you aren't. You can't draw from it from the pyre."

The old man's mouth worked. Behind him, in a ring of women and grandfathers and a boy with a bad foot, the rest of the village watched the thatch burn. Ponni sat on the ground with a blanket over her head. She pushed it back.

"Told him," she said. "I told him the warden had a wife who ties knots, and she'd skin him for sitting on a well against an army." Her eyes went over Devan's face, hunting for someone else's. "Where is she?"

"Four days off."

"Hm." She got a hand on his sleeve and hauled herself up. "Then you'll do."

Kandan picked Kesu up bodily, billhook and all, and slung him over a shoulder like a bag of grain. The old man called him four kinds of fool. He did not struggle. Devan thought he had known all along that he would be carried.

The women pulled blankets off their own beds and left them in a heap at the thorn's edge for whoever came out of it. Then they went up the path in a long ragged line, Kesu and Ponni and thirty more, and eleven soldiers, the boy riding on a volunteer's back. Devan took the end. Halfway up the first bend he looked back.

The thatch went all at once. A roof caught and the fire ran along the ridge-poles and the whole village stood up in a column. Raiders were running between the huts with torches in both hands, not even needing to hurry. Beside him Kesu said nothing at all. Ponni took the old man's hand and kept it.

The well curb stood in the middle of it, grey stone, untouched.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They climbed all morning. The sun came up into a hard white sky and the path turned to oven. Women carried children and old men carried each other. Devan walked the rear, and every time he looked behind he counted, because counting was what he did when he was afraid. Thirty-two. Thirty-two.

Near noon, in the lower narrows, the Thurai horse found them.

Not many. A dozen riders, the ones who had not bolted, with a grudge. They came up the cart road under the shelf where Raman had died, and the first Devan knew of them was the sound a rope makes in the air.

He turned. That was the mistake: he turned toward the sound and offered his side.

The hook was a boarding hook, iron, three-pronged, the kind a ship's crew throws at a rail. It took him across the ribs under the right arm and went on dragging. He felt cloth part, then skin, then something deeper that he decided not to name. The rope snapped taut and he went over on his face in the dust.

Everything went white and ordinary. The sun was straight overhead. His shadow lay under him no bigger than a hand.

He tried to push up and his arm would not hold.

Boots. Then a spear came over him, very close, and a man planted himself astride him with his feet wide. Kandan. The Corporal who had called Neela a witch at a barracks door. He did not look down. He said to the riders, in a voice with nothing in it, "Come on, then."

Devan lay with his cheek on the path and let him.

It was not a small thing. For six years he had held every back he stood behind and none had held his. He could have said *I've got it*. He had said it to Muthu a thousand times in a hundred ways. He lay under another man's spear and did not say it, and the silence was louder than the hook.

The riders had seen enough spears for one morning. They went down the road without any hurry.

"Up, warden," said Kandan, still facing the empty road.

"One breath."

"Take two. I'm not going anywhere."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The shelf at last. The wall, the ladders stacked and broken, Kamalakshi on the lip with her sleeves rolled, giving him the long look of a woman who has been right and is too polite to say it. She did not say it. She said something better.

"Fifteen went down," she said. "Fifteen came up the cut at mid-morning, in what blankets they'd found. Human, bare, shaking so hard they could hardly hold the rock, climbing the back of the cut one behind the other. I counted every one in. They're in the cistern shed eating everything Janu's girls left us." She jerked her chin. "Thulasi took a hoof on the foreleg. It's cracked, not broken through. She walked, but she can't stand a spear human, not this week. And Vellan won't leave her side."

Devan did the sum before she could. Fifteen hollow and flat on their mats until dusk. One more with a leg, and one more to sit by it.

"That's sixteen hands off tomorrow's wall," Kamalakshi said. She did not say *I told you*. She did not need to.

Everyone alive, and the shelf thinner for it. He had said *whole*, and this was the part of the word he had meant. He found he had to look at the horizon for a moment.

"Thank you for the dull part," he said.

"Sit down before you fall down."

He sent the villagers to the cistern shed beside the fifteen and ate nothing. Then he sat on a fallen ladder rung with his shirt off and Kandan's curved needle in his teeth, a hank of waxed silk in his lap, and sewed himself shut.

It was not clean work. The hook had gone in at the side and out toward the back and left a ragged lip of skin that he had to pull with two fingers. He kept his eyes on the horizon so as not to look at it. Kandan stood a polite pace away holding a lamp he did not need, in broad daylight, because no one had told him what to hold.

"It'll scar," Kandan said.

"It'll join the others."

Third stitch. Fourth. He counted them. He counted everything.

He counted the hour, too. It was well past noon by the sun, and he had not pricked his thumb. Hers had come early, three small stings on the climb, a half hour short of noon, and he had not known what early meant.

Three small stings, every noon, from her or from him. It was the whole of their bargain across four days of road. If one of them could not send, the other waited, and if the waiting went long, the waiting itself told you nothing good. He had missed it. He had been on his face in the dust at the exact hour.

And Kodiyur had a gun.

He knew nothing of its stairs. He knew only that, the night before she left, she had told him she would go where the city was loudest, because in a loud place no one hears a quiet thing. In Kodiyur the loud thing was the noon gun.

*If she was on a stair at noon.* He pulled the fifth stitch through. *If it came while she was on a stair.*

The wound-share carried hurt, not words. It would not say *I'm hit across the ribs, it isn't deep, I'm well*. It would raise a welt along her ribs, hot and weeping, in exactly the place and at exactly the hour, and the pain would feel to her as if something had torn her open. She was a woman in a palace who had to stay silent. He had no way to reach her. He could not even say sorry.

Eight stitches. Nine. He bit the silk and knotted it.

His hands had begun to shake, so he did the last thing with care. He took the leopard tooth from the cord at his chest. It was yellow, old, curved like a hook of its own. He set the point against the pad of his left thumb and pressed.

One.

Two.

Three.

Blood came up in three small beads. He watched them and thought of a girl's thumb somewhere with three welts rising on it, late, a whole hour late. *Alive.* It was the only word he had. It wasn't enough, and it was all there was to send.

"Warden?" Kandan was looking at the thumb.

"Nothing." Devan closed his hand over the beads. "Count the people in the shed again."

"I've counted."

"Count them again."

Kandan went. Devan sat in the white heat with a wet needle in his lap and his side stitched like a torn sail, and listened for a pain that was not his own.

The ribs throbbed. His thumb burned. Somewhere south, a woman who had walked into the palace with a throat full of lime had a welt rising hot along her ribs in the wrong place at the wrong hour, and had already decided whether to scream.

# Chapter 15: The Sea Stair

By day there was no shape to hide in.

Neela had never minded that before. At night the other one hid her. At noon she was a tall woman in borrowed boots, hanging off a cliff by her hands.

The rope was stiff with salt and it burned through the rags she had wound round her palms. Forty feet of rock went up in front of her nose, black and weeded at the foot, dry and brown higher. Below, a mackerel skiff rode the swell under the overhang. Rukmini had lent it for a day and four panam and asked nothing. Pavi held it against the cliff with one oar. Her left hand, the old-broken one, lay curled on the gunwale like a sleeping crab.

"The slot's a body wide," Pavi had said. "Usha swears it."

"Usha put the odds at three in four."

"And you?"

"I think Usha is loyal to a building."

Pavi had laughed once, short, and then not again.

Neela had pricked her thumb before she started. Three lime thorns from the bush by the Kettamala kitchen well, a full half hour before the gun. She had sat in the skiff with her sleeve pushed back and watched three beads come up. It was not noon. It was not nothing. He would read it for what it was, a word said early, because she would have no hand free at the hour. She had told Chathu the cost was Devan's to hear from her. This was the first instalment.

She climbed. Fifty-one breaths. The slot was a stone-framed gap in the stair roof where spray had eaten a beam, and she went through it on her stomach and came down on a wet step with her heart going.

The rope slithered up after her. Pavi hauled it off the face before the quay could see a washing line on a cliff. Then the skiff was gone from the edge of the slot, sculling for the point.

The sea stair took her.

It was roofed in dark stone and loud with water. She stood still and counted. Below her, down a long bend, two men talked at the landing. Usha's pair. They faced the water gate and had never once looked up. She went up on the outsides of her boots, step by step, and did not hear them stop.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At the top was a door of black wood, and it was not barred.

She didn't like that either, but she had a list of things she didn't like and a stair behind her. The passage beyond was screened in carved teak. Light fell through the fretwork in lozenges. She slid into the niche behind the last screen and held her breath with the count.

Voices. Women's, and a clink of keys. Then another, warm and unhurried, with a trace of laughter in it.

"No, leave the shutters. I like to hear it."

A courtesy to an attendant. No raised voice. Neela had spent a month learning to fear a woman she had never heard, and the voice was kind. That was the worst thing it could have been.

Skirts went along the passage, away from her. A door closed far off. Forty breaths. Fifty. She counted every one and the passage stayed empty.

*Noon sits audience from the gun,* Chathu had said. *Which means she leaves before it.*

She went along the screens to the third door and into the queen's room.

It was plain. She stood a moment and let the plainness settle. Whitewashed walls. A narrow bed with a grey cover. A table, a stool, a brass lamp, a shelf with eleven books and a gap for a twelfth. A window of shutters left open on the sea, and the sea, huge and flat and bright, filling it. She had expected silk. She had expected a room that told her what the woman was.

On the table lay a stack of petitions as high as her hand. She moved close, because she looked at everything, and read the top one.

*Cheru's widow, of Kalladi. Seed grain for the second sowing, and her husband's last pay.* Under it, in a quick slanting hand, wedged between the lines: *Seed goes tomorrow. Pay is slow; I have paid it from my own purse, and you are not to thank the roll. Hold the well. Nirmala.* The whole name, as if a widow deserved all of it.

She turned over the next. And the next. Every one was answered. Every answer was in that same hand, no clerk's copy, no stamp. Forty petitions, perhaps. Forty people who now believed someone had heard them.

*She signs death writs in the same ink,* Neela thought. The thought did not fit anywhere, so she set the paper back square on the stack.

Under the window, on the floor, stood a chest of red lacquer with a bird picked out in gold.

She knelt. She would wait for the gun. The lid would sing on its hinge and the gun would drown it. She laid her palm flat on the lacquer, which was warm from the sun, and heard the harbour tower take its breath.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The gun went off, and the room jumped.

Something took her under the right arm and dragged.

It came from nowhere. A line of fire from ribs to spine, ragged where a blade would be clean, catching and pulling as it went. She knew the shape of it the way she knew a rotten stair. Not a cut. A hook. Something with prongs had gone in and kept going.

She bit down. Her tongue split against her teeth and the copper taste filled her mouth. She did not make a sound. The gun was still rolling off the rock and would have covered a scream, and she kept it in anyway, because the guild had spent twenty years teaching her what to do with a scream.

*Dragging,* she thought. *If it's the start of the drag, I'll know in a minute.*

The welt burned. It did not widen. It did not pull her down. It was hot and weeping along her ribs, soaking the guard's coat, and it held.

*He's alive. He's alive, or he was.* The thought had no floor. She put it aside by force and lifted the lid.

The chest was not locked.

The farm lock hung from the hasp with its shackle up, as if someone had meant to come back and turn it. Her skin went cold. *Opened worse at fourteen,* Chathu had said. She had come with the picks in her cuff and the plan in her head, and the lock had not waited for either.

Inside, on a bed of folded blue cloth, lay a stack of pages.

She counted them with the tip of one finger. Thirty. Each was a stiff pale leaf with an entry in resin-ink black as a beetle's back and still tacky after years. Each had a thin strip of another paper looped to one corner by a twist of green thread. An intake roll. A name and a village, for every one of them. A person.

She did not read the top entry. Her eye caught a number in the corner, a date, and the third line, which was the one that named the shape, and she dropped her eyes before it could settle. A name read in a hurry was a name you carried.

But in the margin, beside the entry, was a small brown bird.

Wings folded. Head turned. Drawn in a fine thin line, like a tick in a ledger.

She had seen it before. She knew where, and the knowing came in pieces. A tally book in her wrap, with the same little bird inked beside the lines marked *by leave*. Thurai boats at Nellimala crossing a bar at slack water. And the back half of a ledger, thirty cut stubs, a folded bird by each.

*By leave. Whose leave?*

The itch was enormous. She had no hand to scratch it with. Voices in the passage. Not near. A door, a laugh, somebody calling for water. She turned the leaves one by one. Every margin, the same bird. Thirty out of thirty.

She slid the stack into a sheet of oilcloth, folded it flat, and bound it under the guard's coat against her ribs with the long strip she had brought. It sat against the welt like a hand pressing on a bruise. She shut the lid. She left the lock exactly as she found it, shackle up.

At the door she looked back once. The sea filled the window. The petitions lay in their square stack.

She went down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The stair was empty all the way to the landing, and at the landing the sixth bell began.

It was a thin bright sound off the harbour tower, and it was the sound Usha had promised. The pair at the post straightened, said something to each other, and walked off down the lower flight to be relieved, each with one shoulder a little higher than the other from a long standing. Neela pressed into the shadow of the bend and counted.

Sixty breaths for the change. The last twelve with the landing bare.

She reached ten in the dark and the post was empty. She reached twelve with her feet on the stones of the landing and the sea flat as a plate beyond the arch. No wind. If it had been blowing it would have been nine, Usha said.

She crossed in eleven. At the twelfth she was below the post, and the relief came up the stair behind her in a clatter of spears, and no one looked down.

It had fallen to the breath.

A palace guard of eleven years who had measured that landing till she dreamed it could do that. Neela told herself so down the last flight and out through the fish wicket, where porters in wet wraps hauled in the palace's afternoon catch and did not look at her. Nothing in a palace fell to the breath. She had been in houses for a living. She knew which locks were left open and by whom.

Pavi was at the sea steps with the skiff.

"Go round to the salt store," Neela said. "I'll walk."

"You're white."

"I climbed a cliff."

Pavi looked at the front of her coat and said nothing. Then she shoved off.

The cliff path went up from the steps between gull-white rocks and wind-bent thorn. Neela made twenty paces and sat on a stone. Her hands were shaking. She held them out in front of her and watched them do it. They had done everything she asked for an hour and they had saved the shaking for now.

The sun had gone an hour past noon when her left thumb stung.

One.

Two.

Three.

Three small welts, hot and neat, one beside another on the pad of her thumb. An hour late. Late, and not a word, and the oldest word they had.

She put the thumb against her mouth, where the split tongue was, and for a while she did nothing at all.

*Alive.* He had been on the ground, or something had been in him, and now he had a hand free and a tooth to prick with. The bargain had held. He had waited out the hour, and she had waited out the hour, and the not-knowing had cost them each the same. She had told Chathu it was Devan's to carry too. She would say so to his face.

The pages pressed her ribs. She sat on the cliff path in the white afternoon with thirty people against her skin and a thumb that said *I live,* and the success of it was as hollow as a dropped shell.

She stood. She went up to the lane.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


By dusk the drying shed smelled of salt and old fish and the lamp-oil Usha had brought. Racks of split mackerel hung in rows like the washing of the dead. The side door was bolted. The front door was bolted. The door behind the nets was open a hand's width, and Thilak sat on a crate beside it with his boar's patience and a broken cup of tea.

They came in ones and twos, in plain cloth, with their sashes left in lockers. Neela had learned the names by the second lamp. Sajan, bitter about the queen. Lata, whose brother's wife had had a reply to a petition. Sanu, the youngest, who had carried notes for a year and never read one. Twenty-seven others whose hands she took one at a time, whose mothers' villages she would not know till morning.

Thirty. She counted twice.

She laid the oilcloth on a barrel and did not unfold it.

"I have your pages," she said. "All thirty, and the rolls that go with them. I haven't read them. I won't tonight."

No one moved. Somewhere in the racks a drop of brine fell and was loud.

"Tomorrow at dawn, in this shed, in daylight, I read each one aloud to the one it belongs to, and put it in her hand. After that she holds her own leash, and no one else can read it against her. That's the rule, and it works. I've done it eighty-two times."

"And the room hears our shapes," said Sajan.

"Everyone in it. You'd have new witnesses. I'll ask the room to be only people you chose." She made herself say the rest. "That's the cost. I won't dress it."

"What happens to us after?" said Lata. "When she finds the chest empty."

"She'll know it was me."

"She'll know it was one of us."

"She'll know it was me," Neela said again. "I'll be the one she hunts."

Lata looked at her a long time. Then she looked at her own hands and said, "I'll take my page from the queen's hand, or I'll not take it."

"That's yours to say."

"I'm saying it." She stood. She did not seem angry. She seemed a woman who had finished a sum. "I'm sorry. She's never hurt me."

"I know," Neela said. "That's the trouble."

Hands, then. Kandan wasn't there to count, so Neela asked and counted herself, slow. Usha's went up first and her face did not change at all. Sanu's followed, shaking. Sajan's last, hard, as if it hurt him. Twenty-two. She counted them twice.

Eight stayed down. Lata and seven who gave no reason. They went out one at a time by the net door, and Thilak held it for each, and did not look at their cuffs.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It was full dark when the shed emptied. Neela stood in the net door with the cool coming off the harbour and the oilcloth against her ribs, and she did not feel like a woman who had won.

*Too easy.* She had said it on the stair and she said it again. The chest left open. The landing bare to the breath. A voice that kept the shutters open to hear the sea.

"Neela," said Thilak, very quietly. "Look up."

The palace stood on its cliff above the roofs, a black shape cut out of a darker one, studded with a few late lamps. She had stood below it all her childhood and seen nothing but a wall. She knew its windows now by the count: the third door beyond the black one.

One lamp was moving along the outer gallery.

It came out of the long hall's end and went along the cliff wall at a walking pace, steady, held at the height of a shoulder. It passed one window and the next. It turned the corner where the passage began.

It was going toward the queen's room. Toward the red chest, and the lock hanging open with its shackle up.

It did not hurry.

# Chapter 16: Safe, Unseen, and Mine

The first name Neela read aloud belonged to a boy who had stopped answering to it at seven.

"Intake," she said. She held the leaf nearer the shed's one grey window. Dawn came through the slats in stripes and lay across the mackerel racks. "Chandrika. A tailor's son. Of Pallikara, by the river. Sold at seven."

Sanu stood very still in front of her. He was the youngest of the twenty-two, and he had carried notes for a year without reading one.

"That's me," he said, as if someone else had said it.

"Yes."

"I thought it was a girl's name."

"It was your mother's mother's. It says so, two lines down."

She read the rest. The number. The date, six years back, in a witness's hand. Then the line that named the shape, which she said aloud in the shed's cold light because the rule said she must, in full, before the one it belonged to. Twenty-one people heard it. Sanu closed his eyes. When she folded the leaf into his palm his fingers shut on it like a trap.

"It's yours," she said. "Nobody else can read it against you. Burn it, keep it, eat it."

"Keep it," he said, thickly. "Can I keep it?"

"Anything but give it away."

That was the rule of the work. A page read and handed over, and the room left holding what it had heard. She had done it eighty-two times at the Resin House with the sun on her back. She had not known it would be this hard with a roof on.

She did them in ledger order. Each came forward. Each heard a village that had not been said aloud in fifteen years, a trade, a mother's name. A woman learned she had a sister. A man of thirty with a soldier's walk was a fisherman's son from a place that had since gone under the tide, and he laughed once and sat down on a crate.

Usha came nineteenth.

She came up with her hands open and her sleeves pulled to the knuckle, as she had come up the hill to the Company. Neela read the leaf. Birth name, a hill village, a potter's daughter. Usha's real name was Ushakumari and she had been called Usha the whole time. It was the only name that had survived being sold.

"That's it," Usha said, when Neela finished. Her voice gave nothing.

She took the page. She folded it twice, small, and put it inside her cuff against the pared-flat seam on her wrist. She offered not one word of thanks, and went back to the wall.

Neela noted it the way she noted everything. A woman who had asked no question. A woman who had stood nearest the door since first light.

Twenty-two. When the last had gone out into the lane, blinking, the shed was empty but for Thilak at the net door and the smell of brine.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Noon came up the harbour in a long white glare.

Neela sat on a crate with her back to the racks and ate cold rice, because Thilak had put it in her hands and not moved until she did. Pavi had gone to the salt store to sit on Madhavi. Kuttu and Meenu had the front. The pages were gone, every one, into twenty-two cuffs and sashes and hems. Against her ribs there was only the fading ache where a hook had not been.

*Alive.* She had said it to herself for a day. He would be at the cistern shed now, or on a wall. Noon was minutes away. She would prick three times with the thorns in her sash, the right hand this time, so he would feel it clean.

She had the first thorn in her fingers when Thilak lifted his head.

He made no sound. He only turned the broken cup in his hands, once, the way he did when he scented a thing before it came into view. The net door moved. Not a push. A hand, laid flat and patient, and the door swung back a handspan, and then wide.

A woman stood in it with a writing case.

She was not tall. She wore plain grey cotton, a plain wrap, and her hair was greying under a cloth. She was fifty-four. Neela knew the number as she knew the sum of a lock's pins. She knew the voice before the woman opened her mouth.

"May I come in? I would knock, but there is nothing to knock on."

Behind her, a step back, with her sleeves pulled down, stood Usha.

There were no soldiers. Neela looked past both of them, down the alley, to the roofs and the blank wall opposite. No green sash. No spear. Thilak's eyes found hers and asked a question with his whole boar's body. She gave him the smallest shake of her head, and he moved his bulk aside from the door.

The queen came in alone, and every door in the shed was open behind her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"No," she said, when Neela stood. "Sit. I've been walking since first light and I would like to do the same."

She took the second crate. It was low, and she settled on it without fuss, her case across her knees. She looked at the racks of split fish and at the stripes of light. Her face was lined and kind, and tired in a way that had been earned a petition at a time.

"You climbed in by the spray slot," Nirmala said. "My grandfather's masons left it. I've been meaning to ask them to close it for thirty years. I'm glad I never did."

"You left the lock open."

"I did." She did not look away. "I wished to see what you'd take, and what you'd leave. You took the thirty and left the petitions square. That told me more than a good many reports." She unlatched the case. "Usha, would you shut the net door? Thank you."

Usha shut it. She did not look at Neela.

"You told her," Neela said.

"Last night," Usha said. "After the lamp."

"Why?"

"Because you'd run thirty of us into rogue lives before the week was out." Her flat voice cracked at one corner. "Half of us hunted by crown and guild. I wanted you to see the rest before you read the first. And she asked me. She asked, Neela. Nobody has asked me a thing in eleven years."

"That's what a leash does," Neela said. "It asks."

"Yes," said the queen. "And I would like to ask you something, too."

From the case she drew a leaf and laid it on the barrel between them.

It was a fresh page. The paper was still the pale, stiff kind. The ink was black and tacky, and a fly had landed on one corner and could not get free. The hand was a quick slanting hand Neela had read twenty times over in a petition stack.

*Ushakumari, called Usha. Ledger number Nineteen, crown leaf. Night-shape —*

The word was there. Neela did not read past it.

"I wrote it last night," Nirmala said. "In my own hand, after I had fed her tea and heard what she had done. It will never dry; resin-ink doesn't. It has taken, as you see." She laid a finger beside the line, not touching. "You carried away paper. You can't carry away what I saw."

Neela looked at it. The shed stopped being a shed.

*A master who saw you turn can always write you again.* She had been twelve or thirteen. She had stood at the door of Chathu's room and watched him bend over a gnawed page, one that rats had been into, resin-ink blooming wet under his reed. *Kirri's,* he had said, not looking up. He had written it whole inside a quarter hour. Neela had walked away believing she had seen a kindness.

And at the shelter, Kamalakshi had asked it straight, with Vellan's bird in her fingers: *Can anyone else ever hold me?* Neela had given her the rule word for word and heard it ring hollow. She had not liked how fast she said it.

Burn a page and the witness remained. A page was only the memory of a witness, set down in a form that could be read. Anyone who had stood and seen could write it out afresh.

"The pages in the chest were only leaves," she said. Her mouth had gone dry. "You are the page."

"I am the witness." Nirmala said it gently. "I saw every one of the thirty turn, the first time, in a room of mine. I held each hand. That cannot be stolen." She looked at the fly on the corner of the leaf. "I did not tell you in order to frighten you. I told you so that you would not walk out of here believing something that wasn't true."

From the case, once more, she took a sheaf of leaves and set it on the barrel beside Usha's.

They were forty. Neela knew them by their thickness. Black ink, a master's hand.

"Chathu's," the queen said. "I had him write at my table, four evenings. Your forty, from memory. He has a very good memory. He wept for the ones who died. He said he'd never had cause to forget a girl." She did not smile. "These are true pages, and true pages hold. Your forty are mine too, whenever I choose."

Something snagged. Neela saw a cell under the palace rock, and a man's fingertips stained dry grey-brown from common ink. She saw a steward's list of forty names with blanks, and a man who had not answered it. The pictures would not lie flat against the black leaves in front of her. She reached for the thought and the queen's calm voice went on over it, patient, and it slid away.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Neela did not move.

*Whenever I choose.* The words went into her like the hook, and caught, and she breathed through her nose.

"You'll say it at night," she said. "To cage them."

"I will not." The queen's mouth tightened, and for a moment she looked her age. "I have not caged a single one of my own in thirty years, and I will tell you what it cost me not to. But I would be lying if I said I could not. You'd know I was lying, and I don't wish to start that way."

"Why does it matter, what I think?"

"Because I want something from you." Nirmala sat back. "I'll say it plainly, and then you will want to know why I have sat in a fish shed to say it."

Neela waited. Her hands were cold.

"The folded bird," the queen said. "You've seen it. You have a tally book in your wrap. You've been trying for a month to learn whose leave."

"Yours."

"Mine." No shame in it, and no pride either. "Spread for what the realm sees. Folded for what it needn't." She raised one hand, wings flat. Folded it. "I keep a private hand. Everyone who rules does. You'd have found it sooner or later, and I would rather you heard it from me than from a clerk with a reason."

*Whose leave. By leave.* A boat crossing a bar at slack water without a pilot. A man in grey counting children. Neela waited for the rest to come, and the queen watched it not come, and did not offer it.

"Not all of it today," Nirmala said. "I'll not lie and not tell. I'll tell you what you need to answer me. Then you'll go and think."

She folded her hands on the case.

"I want the night-guard. All thirty, as one company, under your command. Not under mine. I will write no pages. Not one. They'll be vouched under the crown, so no hunter in Varanad can lay a finger on them, and you will answer to nobody but the throne, and not often to it." She paused. "And I give Devan back Kettamala. The seal, the warden's office, the garrison's grey. No one would call him harbourer again. Safe, unseen, and mine."

It was the way she said the last of it. *Mine.* It came out of a warm, tired mouth like a coin laid on a table.

"It's a good offer," Neela said.

"It's the best I have."

"Why would I take a leash from a hand that's kind?"

"You wouldn't have one. That's what I am offering." Nirmala did not look away. "I'd not even hold the pages. The thirty could burn theirs tonight and I'd stand over the fire. You'd have the knives, and I would have a night-guard that obeyed the woman who freed it, and Varanad would not go dark when the Thurai land."

"They've landed."

"They have." The queen's eyes did not change. "And I have kept a promise to my own people, which I may not break. You will want to ask about it. Not today."

Neela turned the words over, and one stuck.

"There's a better way," she said. "I know it. I've done it. An odiyan says her own shape, aloud, in her own voice, to one person she chooses, at the dusk turning. It voids every other witness. Every one. You. Chathu. The room. After that nobody can cage her without her. It's the only way to be free of all of you at once."

"I know." The queen's voice was very quiet. "I have known it for nine days. Usha told me what a ledger page does when it burns by itself."

"Then you know what it costs."

"Do you?"

It was not a taunt. It was a question put, patiently, to a woman who had been paying the cost since a night at the Resin House with a bleeding wrist.

"You'll have thirty pairs," Nirmala said. "Thirty odiyans each bound to a chosen person, and each pair feeling every wound the other takes. A spear in a night-guard's thigh is a spear in her husband's thigh, her sister's. They bleed for each other for life. And every one of them marked rogue, because the guild cannot vouch for a page that has burned. Your Company will be the freest knives in Varanad, and also the most precious, because every blade has a heart in the shape of a wound. You'll have made them precious, and precious knives don't win wars."

Neela didn't answer.

"I'm not saying it to wound you," Nirmala said. "I'm saying it because I've watched what a wall does to a man whose wife stands on the other side of it. I would not like to watch it thirty times."

*A thumb. Three welts, an hour late.* Neela had sat on a cliff path with her split tongue against the pad of it, and for some time she had not been a woman who needed anything.

She had told herself it was freedom. Freedom was belonging to no one. She had said it to Ammu at fourteen, and to Chathu in a locked room, and to a man on a rampart in the dusk with resin on her wrists. She had meant it as armour.

And the only time in twenty-seven years she had not been owned, she had been tied to a man by a wound that opened when he cut his chin shaving.

*The only freedom I have ever had,* she thought, *is the one that bleeds when he does.*

She would not know how to say it. She did not say it.

"You've not yet asked about him," Nirmala said, "and you have asked about nothing else with your eyes. So I will say it. A rider leaves the Salt Gate for Kettamala at dusk with the same offer in my hand. Three hundred crown spears for the pass, and the fort given back. If his wife takes command. If she does not, the rider turns round."

Neela's hands, in her lap, closed.

"He will have it before you do," the queen said. "I thought you would rather know."

"You've priced him."

"I've priced his old office. Which is not the same as pricing him, and you know it. But I'll own it's a lever, and I won't pretend otherwise." The queen's tired face was gentle. "It is a hard thing, to hold a lever on a person one respects. I would not have done it to a stranger."

*He'll get a letter that says his house for my answer. And I can't answer before the rider does.* She had no right to decide his life, and no right to let someone else decide it either. His fort. His father's seal on a nail. Raiu's pyre. A garrison who had saluted him fist by fist at the gate.
