If she said yes, she bought it for him. If she said no, she kept it from him, and he would never have asked her to.

"I want time," she said.

"You'll have it. Until the pass." Nirmala stood, and the crate scraped, and she was a plain woman with a case in her hand. "You'll be going north to him, and I will be sending what I send. Answer me there. Not before, if you'd rather. I won't ask twice."

She took up the leaf with Usha's name on it, and blew once across the tacky ink. Then she laid it on top of the forty on the barrel and left them both there.

"Keep them," she said. "A witness who tells you what she holds is one you can bargain with. The other kind does not tell."

At the net door she stopped, one hand on the post.

"Thirty is a great many people to have against your ribs," she said. "I know the weight. Don't carry them alone."

She went out. Usha went after her without looking back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Thilak shut the door.

Neela sat very still. The light on the barrel was white now, high, falling straight down through the slats. Two stacks of pages lay before her, and the fly in the corner of one leaf had stopped moving.

*Noon.*

Counting was the one habit that never left her. She went back over the last quarter hour and found where the count had broken: the gun had gone, a dull blow through the rock and the racks, and she had not felt it. Somewhere between *May I come in* and *a lever, and I won't pretend otherwise*, the harbour tower had fired and she had been sitting with a thorn in her fingers.

Her hand opened on it. The pad of her thumb was smooth.

The gun had come and gone without her.

A day behind, she had pricked early. Now late, the gun a quarter hour gone, and no word. And he would sit on a wall or a ladder rung with a stitched side and listen for a pain that was not his own, and hear nothing, and know what nothing could mean.

She took the thorns out of her sash. Three, in a row. She put the point to her thumb and pressed.

One. Two. Three.

Blood came up in three small beads. She watched them, and knew, as she knew a rotten stair, that she would carry the offer north to a man who would already have answered.

"Thilak," she said. "How far to the Hill Gate, on foot?"

"Eleven hundred paces." He studied her. "Neela. What did she want?"

"Everything," Neela said. "She offered it all."

She wiped the thumb on her coat. Then she took up the pages, Usha's on top, the fly still stuck to its corner, and began to count how many hours there were between this barrel and a pass she could not yet answer at.

# Chapter 17: The Price of an Office

Noon came and went and nothing happened to his thumb.

Devan stood on the shelf with a sheaf of arrows in the crook of his arm, careful of the stitches at his ribs, and counted them for the third time. Forty. There was no gun here to mark the hour. There was the sun climbing over the east lip of the cut, and the shadow of the shelf wall shortening to nothing at his feet. He had watched it shorten with the whole of his attention while he told off arrows.

He held his left thumb in his right hand beneath the sheaf, where nobody could see him do it.

*She's on the road.* Two hands on the reins, a horse under her, a lime thorn in a sash she couldn't reach. That was one reason. There were two more and he had built all three with care. The fourth he had not built, and it stood outside them like a man at a door.

A welt meant a wound. No welt meant no wound. That was all the silence could tell him, and he made himself take only that and put the rest down.

"Thirty-nine," said Muthu.

"Forty."

"That's the third time you've counted that sheaf, and the second time you've got forty." Muthu sat on an upturned bucket by the handcart he had dragged down the cut at midmorning. Eleven sheaves, bound in wet cloth. He had brought a flask of the gate-house tea too, and his big laugh, which the shelf had needed more than the arrows. "I've known you twenty years. You don't miscount. You hold the number and let it go."

"The sheaf's short."

"The sheaf's fine." Muthu grinned at him. Then the grin went out of his face, because down the cut, thin and clear, a horn had started. Two long notes. A short. A long.

The crown's riders. Bhaskaran's code, carried a mile down the stone.

Devan set the arrows in the cart and let go of his thumb.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The rider came up the cut leading a horse that had stopped being a horse some miles back and become a lather on four legs. He wore a green sash and the grey of the road. He had changed mounts at four posts, and he said so the way another man would show a warrant.

"For the Warden of Kettamala," he said, and stopped. His eyes went to the plain brown cloth Devan wore, and to the captain with the iron disc at his belt. "Captain. Sir. I was told—"

"Devan."

"Devan." The rider drew a case from his saddle. "Her own hand. I was to put it in yours and no one's."

Green wax. The sunbird, spread. Devan thumbed it up and read standing.

The body was in a clerk's round hand, tidy as a row of stitches. It offered what he had known it would offer, because he had been waiting for it since the day Rema left the fort. Kettamala restored. The seal, the office, the garrison's grey. The charge of harbouring struck off the roll. And three hundred crown spears sent up the cart road to hold the pass. They would climb on the day his wife took command of the night-guard, as one company under the throne's protection. *The same is put to her in Kodiyur.*

Below it, a few lines in a quick slanting hand.

*You will think it a lever. It is. I would sooner you thought so than that I pretended. N.*

He read it twice. Then he read the middle again, slowly, as if the words might be a different size the third time.

The trouble was not that it was clever. The trouble was that he wanted it.

He wanted it the way a man wants water. The iron seal on its nail in the hall. Janu's kitchen his again. The grey coats at the padippura, and the west stair, and the cistern chain that clanked the hours so that he slept through them. Raiu's pyre had been lit in that court. Every night for five years he had told himself he did not mind being warden, and he had not known until a sheet of paper laid it out in black ink how much he minded losing it.

And the arithmetic wasn't pride. He did it, because arithmetic was what he had. Fourteen hundred on the road. A shelf wall held by thirty-six odiyans, each one spending a night in shape and a day in shaking hunger, and by garrison men who had never been asked to be anyone's wall. The chalked tile in Kandan's hand already ran to four names, and it had been a week. Three hundred spears at the narrows would be a different war. They would be a different kind of morning for Kesu's people, and the hill villages. Too late for a boy from Kalladi who had carried a spear taller than himself, but not too late for the next one.

He knew the shape of the thing anyway. Vouched, unhunted, and a knife in a drawer. The old cage with a better roof.

"Well?" said Muthu, quietly.

"Read it."

Muthu read it. He read it with his lips still, the way he did when he was working a hard line. Halfway down he looked up and said nothing, and it was not the quiet of a man lying. It was the other quiet, the one Devan had learned to tell apart. A man holding his breath between two good things.

"Devan," he said at last. "That's three hundred spears."

"I can count."

"Seventy-odd of you against that host. There's Kesu's people behind the line. There's a goat-boy at the fort and a woman at the well with a baby. If it's a yes, they live." Muthu touched the iron disc at his belt. "And this is mine now, and a yes takes it off me. I'd hand you the disc tonight and be glad of it. So hear me as a man with nothing to win." His big hands opened. "Nobody's asking you to like what you'd say yes to. It's the pass."

"I know what it is."

"Then say yes and let her sort the rest."

"That's the part I can't do."

He said it flatly, because it was the only way he had of saying anything. The rider had gone very interested in his horse's girth.

*Let her sort the rest.* Her name in the queen's mouth, and thirty lives in the Company riding on how she answered it, and the first thing the queen had done was price his fort against her yes. If he wrote *yes* to buy the pass, the answer would be his before it was hers. If he waited for her to come up the cut with the fort lying on the table between them, she would answer with it on her back. Whichever way she went, she would carry what it cost him.

"I'll answer now," he said. "Here, in front of you, and the rider. Muthu, you'll witness it."

"As seal-holder?"

"As a man who watched."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He wrote it on the lid of the arrow cart, in plain ink, with the rider's pen. He read each line aloud as he made it. It was not for the rider's benefit. A thing said where others can hear it cannot later be made smaller.

*To Nirmala, queen of Varanad. I have your letter. It is not mine to answer. My wife's answer is hers. I will not buy the pass with it, nor sell her to buy back a fort. I will hold the pass with whoever chooses to stand. If three hundred spears come with no price on her, I will thank you for them. If they come with one, I will hold without them. Devan, son of the warden of Kettamala.*

Muthu read it over his shoulder and said, "You've put a hole in your own wall."

"Yes."

"You could say *with conditions*. Take the spears and let her decide the command after."

"A condition is a yes with her name on the price."

Muthu breathed out through his nose. "She's two days up the road, if she rides as she climbs."

"The rider goes at first light."

"Then wait one night. Ask her."

"I would." Devan heard how it sounded and said it anyway. "We made a term, she and I. Say it before you do it. It was a good term. I'm doing it first."

The words hung over the cart lid between them. He had made that term in a tack room with rain coming through two roof leaks, and he had been proud of keeping it. It had held through a ledger, a raid, a hearing, a road. Now he would put his thumb to a letter she hadn't seen. She would hear it from the queen's mouth, or a clerk's, or his, and the order of it would matter more to her than the words.

*I owe her this.* He added it to the list he did not like.

He had no seal. The disc was Muthu's. He melted a bead of plain black wax over the rider's lamp and pressed his left thumb into it, the thumb she pricked. The print came up whorled and plain.

"Witnessed," Muthu said. He signed under it with the pen held like a tool. He didn't laugh when he gave it back.

Devan took a second leaf from the rider's case and wrote shorter.

*Justice Rema, Kodiyur. A roll is wanted at the Monkey Pass, north side, within the week. I am without office and the crown gives me no leave to call you. Come as recorder. Whatever is done here, held or lost, should be written by one who writes what is. Devan.*

"Rema?" said Muthu. "You'd call her here?"

"She entered Asha by name. She wrote down an execution she couldn't stop." He folded the leaf. "She records against the crown. That's the only kind of recorder I'd trust with this."

The rider took both, tucked them into his sash, and went down the cut leading his spent horse. He walked like a man who had just carried something heavy and did not know what.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The light went amber in the cut. Down the shelf, the wall pairs changed. The Company had its human night by rotation, and half were out of their shapes and hungry, hunched round a pot of rice that smelled of very little.

Vellan sat in the lee of the wall, on a flat stone near Thulasi's blanket. The big man had a knife and a block of pale wood, and the beginning of a bird in his hands. Thulasi slept with her cracked leg propped on a saddle. Vellan did not look up when Devan sat down.

"They're asking each other," Vellan said, after a while.

"Who?"

"The Company. Who they'd name, if they named. It's the sally. All that talk of shared wounds, and Neela's welts on your thumb, and who you'd trust to carry yours." The knife took a curl of wood. "Kuttu asked me."

"What did you say?"

"That I'll not bind to anyone." Another curl. "I've handed enough people my wounds."

It lay there between them. Devan knew the sum of that. Everyone on the shelf who had heard that sentence would hear it differently, and he, who had stood at the bottom of a stair at twenty-six with Janu holding a lamp, heard it the one way he could. He turned a pebble over in his pocket and found nothing to do with his hands.

He had not asked for Asha's last words. They would come when Vellan decided they were earned, and not a day before. He had let a man walk away from a reading with his back turned, and counted forty-one steps. He had not forgiven him. He had never said so, and the man had never asked.

"Then I'll mind your back," Devan said.

Vellan's hands stopped.

Slowly he turned his head. His face did what Devan had not seen it do in all the weeks: it slackened, and then it broke. A low sound came out of him, rusty, like a gate that had not opened in years. It took a moment to know it for a laugh. Thulasi stirred in her blanket and muttered. Vellan put a hand over his mouth, and the sound went on in his chest.

"You," he said.

"Me."

"I know whose back you'd be minding."

"I know whose it is." Devan kept his voice level. "I'm not forgiving you. I'm standing behind you. Those are different."

Vellan considered that for the length of one slow breath. The laugh had gone out of his face and something steadier had come in.

"They're different," he said. "That's what I'll have."

He went back to the bird. Devan stayed on the stone while the light drained out of the cut and the first oil lamp was lit down the line.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk the old watcher called him to the lip.

Koman lay on his belly on the rock above the shelf, his nose lifted into the wind, the way he had done in the pass village for eleven years. Devan lay down beside him. Below them the cut dropped to the river, and beyond the narrows the cart road climbed in a long grey switchback.

Something was coming up it, and the noise of it reached them before the sight. Oxen. A groaning of axles, and a rhythm of men on ropes, sixty at least, chanting low in a language he did not have.

It rounded the bend like a house climbing a hill.

Ship-timber, black with tar. Three men high on a cart bed, lashed hides hung wet from its flanks against fire, a roofed platform at the top and slits cut in its face. A tower. It rolled a hand's breadth at a time. Behind it came a flat supply cart with a pale square on its tailboard, and painted there in fresh bright yellow, a small bird. Clean paint. He looked at it a moment longer than he needed to.

*Three men high.* He measured the shelf wall by eye. An archer on that platform would look down on every man behind it.

He opened his mouth to say so to Koman, and the old man's hand closed on his sleeve.

"There," Koman said. "In the river."

The river ran below the narrows, brown and quick, past the shingle where the cut met the road. Something broke its surface. A long dark back, low and slick, with no head to speak of. Then a tail, thick at the root, trailing like a rope. It crossed against the current with no hurry, the way a thing swims that has done it all its life. It was longer than an otter and heavier than any river-beast in the hills.

It came up on the near bank, our side, onto wet stones, and stood in the last light. The water streamed off it. Then it went into the thorn below the shelf, and the thorn closed.

"Is it ours?" Devan said.

"None of ours is that. None of ours would be out." Koman's nose was working. Eleven years he had walked the hill edge reading what the House's shapes left behind, a civet from a hare, a jackal from a fox, a bear's old musk three days cold. His voice had gone very small. "I've a name for every scent the Resin House ever made, warden. I've no name for that."

Devan let him have the word. He had given up the office that morning and the old man hadn't noticed.

Above them the tower groaned another hand's breadth up the road, and the sun went off the lip of the cut.

# Chapter 18: Twenty-Two Riders

"Safety isn't a small thing," Lata said. "Say what you like about it."

She said it to the road ahead, not to anyone. The road was red dust, a day north of the last post, and twenty-eight horses were raising it in a long tail behind them. Neela rode at the front and heard every word.

"Nobody's saying small," said Sanu.

"You are. Every time you open your mouth." Lata rode with her back straight and her cuffs buttoned to the knuckle. She was Third Guard, the one who had refused her page in the fish shed and would take it only from the queen's hand. She had come anyway. She had been at the Salt Gate at dusk with a horse and no explanation. "A vouched name. A roof with a lamp in it. No hunter on the road who may lay a hand on you. Thirty years she hasn't caged one of us. Tell me what the hill has given you that matches that."

"It gave me a brother," Sanu said.

Nobody answered. The hooves went on. Neela knew the rest of it, because she had read it aloud at dawn four days back, in a shed that smelled of brine. Sajan's leaf had come ninth. *Pallikara, a tailor's son, sold at nine, one brother younger, sold at seven.* Sanu had been standing by the door. Both men had gone the colour of ash, and neither had spoken until the shed emptied.

"He hasn't said yes," Sanu said now, lower. "I know he hasn't. I only want to be asked what I'd do with the yes."

"Then ask him," said Lata.

"I've asked him. He says he'd bleed for me and he'd rather not." Sanu laughed, a short wrecked sound. "That's the nicest thing he's said to me in twenty years. I think he meant it kindly."

Sajan, riding twenty paces back, kept his eyes on his horse's ears. Neela did not turn her head.

Usha rode at the rear with her sleeves pulled down. At the Hill Gate the queen had put a strip of paper in her hand, folded small and sealed in green wax with the sunbird. She had not let Usha break it. She had said the line aloud, so that Neela heard it too, in that warm unhurried voice: *Ride with her. Press nothing.* Usha had carried it unbroken ever since. At the third post, when the stable-master saw twenty-eight riders and began to ask questions, she held the strip up whole, sunbird to the lamp. He fetched fresh horses and asked nothing more. She tucked it back in her sash, where it rode all day behind Neela's shoulder like a hand not quite laid on her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The offer was honest. That was what made it hard.

Neela turned it over for the hundredth time while the road dropped into a dry riverbed and climbed out again. Thirty knives under her hand. No page on any of them, vouched under the crown, no hunter allowed within a day's ride. No one would need to name themselves to anyone, and so no one would need to bleed. A spear in a night-guard's thigh would be a spear in a night-guard's thigh. Nobody's husband. Nobody's sister.

She would be their protector. She'd be no one's master, and everyone's. And she would never have to stand in front of a living person and ask them to bleed for her.

That was the part she kept coming back to. Not the fort, not the lever. She had asked it of Ammu without knowing she was asking, and Ammu had paid. She would not ask it of thirty more.

*Safe, unseen, and mine.* Warm in the mouth. A coin laid on a table.

Against her saddle, under the flap, lay Chathu's tin. She had taken it from the cell because her hand did it before she thought. She had eaten one, in his cell, standing, and it had been perfect, and that was the worst of it. She had not touched another. It rode against her thigh all day, warm still from some fire she had not seen, and she did not open it.

*A gift is the start of a bill.* The queen had given her no sweets. She had given her a fly stuck on a page and a hand held out flat.

"Neela," said Thilak, riding at her stirrup. "You've stopped breathing again."

"I haven't."

"You hold it on the climbs." He didn't look at her. "It's all right. I only count."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They made camp in a dry grove off the road on the third night, under teak trees with the leaves gone leathery and brown. Nobody smeared. It had been Devan's last order before she left, and the Company had carried it out of the shelter on its back. The air went blue and the stars came on over the hill road. Twenty-eight odiyans and guards sat round a small fire in their own skins.

Neela walked to the stream with her cloth and her kit and a bar of the brown soap Pavi had bought from a tinker.

For five days she had worn a green starched cuff and a throat paste of lime and ochre. It had dried chalky and cracked when she swallowed. She knelt by the water and began to wash it off. The paste came away in grey ropes, and the water took it, and under it lay the scars.

She heard them come up behind her. She had known they would.

She did not turn. She scrubbed until the skin stung. She undid the cuffs and rolled them back, the green cotton, the wedding bangle under the left one, and laid her wrists on her knees with the palms up. Throat. Wrists. The pale thick ridges, layered over each other like a tree's rings. Eleven years' worth.

Then she stood and walked back to the fire and sat down in the light with every inch of it showing.

Nobody spoke. The queen's guards were pared smooth at the wrist and painted at the neck. A surgeon had taken their scars once a season, and they'd learned to think of it as care. Sanu looked at his own cuff, and then at hers.

"Does it hurt?" he said. "The real kind. Still."

"The burn's the first night. After that it's the morning." She turned her wrist to the fire. "I don't hide mine. I'm not telling you not to. I just want you to see what it looks like when you leave it."

"A question for a question?" Kuttu said.

He had come up on the other side with a pot of rice. He was Kumaran, a ferryman's son, not yet thirty, and it had been his idea in the first place to call the forty a Company. He sat down hard and began to ladle.

"That's how they did it in the Resin House," he said. "Nobody told us. You get a question if you give one."

"We did it at the long table," Neela said. "I'd forgotten."

"I haven't," said Madhavi, and sat.

So it went, as the rice went round. Meenu asked Lata what the queen had given her that nobody could take back, and Lata said, after a long moment, "A name that was always mine. She called me by it before anyone else." Thilak asked Sajan why he'd served the queen so long, and Sajan said, "Because I thought if I was useful enough, she'd tell me where my brother was." That was a harder answer. The fire cracked. Sanu put his head down.

Lata turned to Neela. She did it the way she did everything, straight on. "What does it cost, then? The name. I want the cost, not the poem."

Neela told her. She gave the cut that opened on someone else, the fever that dragged another body toward the ground, the three welts on a thumb an hour late. She said she'd had the ache, and the forced resin, and a man's wound opening across her side in the Resin House yard. Then she said the rest, because it was the question.

"I've never felt so tied in my life. And I've never been less owned."

"Both?" said Lata.

"Both." She held her wrist to the light. "It took me a month to learn those are different."

Lata looked at her a long time. "I don't know that I'd pay it," she said.

"No. You don't have to. That's why I won't take the offer by myself."

It was out before she had looked at it. She sat there while it went round the fire, face by face. Usha, beyond the light, had lifted her head.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Later, when the others slept, Kuttu sat with her on the stream bank. He didn't ask. He turned a stick in the coals and it threw sparks.

She did not know she was humming until he went quiet to listen.

It was the old tune. Ammu's, the fishing song she'd hummed at nine and twelve and twenty-four, when her hands were busy and her head was not. It had no tidy words. Neela had made up the half she didn't know.

"Pull and pull," she sang, under her breath. "The tide is coming in."

"I know that one," Kuttu said. "My father's ferry crew sang it. Different tune." He hummed his version, flat, slower. "Where'd you get it?"

"From someone who heard it at a net-mender's knee." She stopped. *A net-mender behind the sheds,* Ammu had always said, *taught us both the hitch.* There had been no net-mender behind the sheds. There had been Ammini, a net-mender of Nellimala, and her hands, which Neela's fingers had carried up the goat path without her. Maybe a girl of seven had tied it for a smaller girl, and Ammu had never had the heart to say whose knot it was.

She sat with that, and then with the other thing it knocked loose.

Three short yips. A pause. One long rising note. *Tomorrow, I will find you.* She had heard it a hundred times as a child, from a girl with a gap in her teeth. She had heard it a last time in the Resin House yard, from a jackal's throat, the dusk Ammu drew Vellan and the hunters off. Neela had stood there in shape and could not answer.

She had always taken it for a goodbye. A promise Ammu had broken because she died.

But Ammu had given it on the nights Neela went off alone to settle something that was not hers alone. The stone empty, the cup bare, and that thin cry from the spur. It had never meant *I'll arrive.* It had meant *don't decide without me.*

"What?" said Kuttu.

"Nothing." Her throat hurt. "I only just heard what she was saying."

He didn't push. He threw the stick on the fire.

She lay awake after, on her back, with the stars. She tried the queen's offer one more way. If she took it, nobody would bleed for her. She would give the Company safety, and she would also hold the door shut on the choice she had made at a dusk turning, with a man looking at her. The only freedom she had ever had was the one she had been given, and had given back.

She could not decide that alone. Ammu had said so across a season and a grave. It wasn't hers to decide. It belonged to the forty-two with a page in a cuff, and a plank of names, and a man on a shelf who held the same offer in his own hands and would call it a lever, if he had any sense.

The Company voted on resin, on a sally, on a hill. It would vote on this.

And Devan would have a voice among theirs. He had said so himself, in the shelter, with no office: *If the hands say no, I sit by this fire and hate it.*

She was afraid of it.

She had watched him do it the morning the hands sent her south. He had put a river pebble in his cheek before the count, so that whatever he felt would stay behind his teeth until the last hand was up. Then he had said *yes* to the result. One flat word, level and careful, for the pass, for Kesu's people, for a boy in a red headcloth. He had said it the way a man shuts a gate on his own fingers and keeps his face still. She had not been able to tell what it cost him. She had only seen that he could pay it.

That voice would say yes to the queen. Or a no said for her sake, so that she'd never know whether she had wanted him to say yes. Either way he would say it gently, and she would have to stand there and hear it.

She fell asleep counting the stars she could name, and she knew only four.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They climbed the last day without stopping. The road bent west, steepened, and went to stones. By afternoon the langurs had gone silent on the cliff faces, and she did not need Thilak to say what that meant. Strangers on the road. The Thurai, or the hunger of them.

Nobody sang. Even Lata rode without a word. They went up through thorn and scree, up past the lightning-split boulder where the goat path left the cart road, and Neela knew the place without a map, and for once did not wonder why. She did not slow.

The sun was a hand off the west rim when they crested the shoulder above the pass.

Below them, past a long fall of rock, the cut lay in its own blue shadow. A thread of river. A shelf of stone beneath the cliff, small as a hearth-ledge at this height. There was smoke over it. Not the cooking smoke of a camp. A thick black column leaning with the wind.

She gathered the reins.

The first sting came in her left palm. It was like a bee, small and bright. She ignored it. Then the right went, and the ones that followed were not stings. Heat rose under her skin, across the pad of each hand, along the base of each finger, slow and then fast. She looked down. The leather of the reins had not changed. The skin under it was lifting, white, in two bubbles the size of a thumbprint, and then in four.

She did not cry out. She made a sound in her throat and her horse tossed its head.

"Neela?" That was Thilak.

She held both hands up. The blisters shone in the low light. She turned them over, back and front, the way a woman looks for a splinter. The skin wasn't broken. It was only burned.

*Fire.* Not hers. She had touched nothing hot. Somewhere below, under that column of smoke, a man had taken hold of something that was alight, with his bare hands, and had not let go.

"Ride," she said. "All of you. Now."

And she put her heels in, blistered palms on the reins, and went down toward the smoke.

# Chapter 19: Fire on the Shelf

The tower was burning against the shelf wall, and Devan was at the lip with a ladder on fire in his hands.

Neela saw it from the last bend of the cut, over her horse's ears. Black timber, three men high, wearing flame like a coat. The wet hides on its flanks had gone to hissing rags. A Thurai ladder hung from the platform to the wall, its iron hooks bitten into the stone, and it was alight from the rungs to the lashings. A man stood at its foot with his bare hands on the burning wood. He heaved. The ladder swung out, hung, and fell away into the dark with its climbers screaming.

Her palms screamed with him.

"Down," she said. "Walk them. Don't gallop on the scree."

Nobody argued. Twenty-eight horses came down the cut in a line, and the Company's wall pairs turned to stare. Someone shouted *Riders!* in a cracked voice. She did not stop to learn whose. She counted the steps from the stirrup to the stone, fourteen, and Thilak was beside her at nine, holding out his hand.

"The pot," he said. "Noon trees. Even pinches, the way we voted."

He had a clay bowl on a flat rock behind the wall, black and shining. She tipped her banana-leaf fold into it, all six pinches she had carried to Kodiyur and back. Then she took one from the bowl with a fingertip, the same as anyone. Behind her, Usha cracked the green wax off a flat tin she had carried from the palace store, and the queen's guards crowded round it. Crown resin. It smelled the same. The hands that took it were pared smooth at the wrist.

The sun went off the rim. The cut turned blue.

She ran along the wall with the pinch in her fist. Devan was on his knees by the lip, his back to her, his hands held out from his body like a man carrying hot bowls. The skin of both palms was gone to a raw red shine. He was breathing through his teeth.

"Don't touch anything," she said.

He turned his head. His face was black with smoke, and he looked at her as though she were a sentence in a language he was relearning.

"You're early," he said.

"I'm late."

"You're here." He glanced at her hands, which she was holding exactly as he held his. "It's the same."

"It's the same."

There was no time for more. Over his shoulder a second ladder was swinging up out of the dark, and the tower groaned as its platform leaned. She crouched where she was and rubbed the resin hard across her throat and wrists. It burned as it always did, like a hot coal pressed to the old ridges. She kept her eyes on him.

Devan kept his on her. But he lifted his voice down the wall, hoarse and flat. "Pairs! Face the road. Backs to the shelf. Count sixty and don't turn round."

Nobody asked why. They had heard that voice in the dark before. Thirty heads swung outward, and thirty backs went square to her.

He had no pebble in his cheek. He had no time to find one. He watched her hands, and then her face, for the whole minute.

A minute is a long time. It was long enough for the hook of a ladder to bite the stone beside his knee. He planted one boot on the shank and kicked it loose without looking away, and the ladder went down the wall behind him. Her spine bent. Her hands went. The cut turned from blue to the bright, hard grey of the other one's eyes, and every smell on the shelf stood up and spoke at once: tar, scorched hide, wet iron, sweat, a man's blood.

His blood. Under the smoke, close, a thin line of it.

*Behind you,* she thought, and could not say it, and went past him to the lip.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The other one did not think about sides.

She thought about edges. The wall had one, the platform had one, and between them was a gap of air a man could cross in a leap. Six Thurai came over it with bronze caps and short swords, half-blind in their own smoke. She met the first before his feet touched stone.

Behind her the shelf was changing.

She felt it more than saw it. The queen's riders were turning along the wall: Usha, Sanu, Sajan, Lata, and the eighteen who had come with them. Gold eyes in the dusk, and low long backs, and the sound a body makes when it comes out of itself. A fox. A heron, struck on one leg and coming down like a dropped knife. A dark creature with a snout and a thick, patient gait. She had never seen them in shape. They had been palace knives for years, kept in cuffs and paste, and they had never fought for a thing they chose.

They fought as if they had been waiting.

No page. No hand on a lead. Nobody at their backs tonight who could say a word and cage them. But the queen had watched every one of them turn, and she was alive, and the road behind them led straight to her. It was not far enough. The guards fought as though they knew it, and as though it made the night worth more. They went along that wall in a loose line and nobody told them where. She watched the fox take a man at the hamstring and be gone before the sword turned. She watched the heron strike a hook-rope and cut it with her beak.

*Unbound,* she thought, and was glad past all reasoning.

Then the second wave came over the gap, and she stopped watching.

Her left flank was open. She knew it as she knew a bad stair. A Thurai spear came at her ribs, low, and she twisted from it, and a weight she knew landed at her back.

Devan.

Spear in his scorched hands. He should not have been able to hold it. She smelled the blood from his palms as he gripped the shaft. He stood with his shoulders against her hip, not touching, then touching, then pressing the way a man leans on a wall he trusts.

"Left," he said, over her head. "Four. The big one."

She went left.

He did not call her name, or the other word, or any word that mattered to the dark. He called targets. They had learned that from the sally. She went where he told her, and where he did not, and each time she moved he moved with her so that her back was never open for longer than a breath. The spear came round once and took a man's blade on its haft just as her jaws closed on another's arm.

A sword caught his forearm. She felt it before he did. A bright line of heat ran down her own foreleg, shallow, a welt and no more. She did not miss her step. He grunted, and the grunt told her it was nothing, and he drove the butt of the spear into the swordsman's face.

Across the shelf a heavy shape had stopped. Kandan had turned round at last, sixty counts gone, with a sword in one hand and a chalk tile stuck in his belt. He was staring. Not at the Thurai. At the two of them.

Beyond him, the fox.

Usha had a man by the wrist and had forgotten to bite. She looked at the pair with her pale muzzle lifted, one breath, two. Then she let the man go to the wall's edge, where he fell on his own, and trotted along the stones to where Sajan stood bleeding at the foot of a ladder. She turned and set her back against his. He flinched. Then he shifted his weight to take her, and neither of them looked round.

The tower gave way at the middle watch.

It went all at once, with a long cracking groan, as the burned beam under the platform let go. The top of it folded outward and came down across the gap in a rain of sparks. Men fell with it. A roar went up from the shelf, and under it, from the river road, a ragged horn called the Thurai back.

She stood on the wall with her ribs heaving, and Devan stood at her back, and neither moved for a long time.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The first grey came up the cut like water into a footprint.

Neela crouched behind the wall, back in her own skin, teeth chattering. Hunger hit her as it always did, hollow and shaking, as if her bones had been scraped. She reached for the cloth. It was already in front of her, held out behind a man's back, warm.

She wrapped it round her. It had been inside his coat. The warmth was his.

"How many dawns?" she said. Her voice came out cracked. "Since I left."

"I lost count after nine," said Devan, to the wall. "I've warmed that cloth every one."

"You couldn't have known I'd be here."

"No." He turned, then, and offered her a leaf packet of cold rice and curd and a cup. The water in the cup steamed. "I did it anyway. You said cold water hurts your teeth."

She had said that in the dark, a long time ago, on a night when neither of them had meant it to matter. She took the cup in both hands, and the blisters cried out against the clay, and she drank anyway.

They sat with their backs against the wall and watched the tower. It was ash. A tall black stump of beam stood in the middle of a wide grey scar, smoke feathering off the top. Garrison men were poking through it with spears. The Thurai had left a ladder-hook in the stone, still bright. Someone had chalked a tile, and Kandan was standing over it, not writing.

"How many?" she said.

"Two of ours. Chami's cousin, and a woman from Kalladi I didn't know." He said the names carefully. "Seven hurt. The wall held. That's all I can count to."

"The guards?"

"All twenty-two. Three limping." His mouth moved. "Lata took a man off the wall by the belt. In shape. I saw her. She'll tell you she didn't."

She almost laughed. She held out her hands instead.

Her palms were white and puffy, the skin lifted over the heel of each hand. His lay beside them, on his knees, and they matched down to the line across the base of the thumb. He saw her looking. He turned his over, and she turned hers, and for a moment neither of them could tell which of them had been burned.

"Yours were first," she said.

"Yours came while I was holding it."

"Both, then."

"Both." He found a pot in his coat, Pavi's green salve, smelling of crushed leaf and smoke, and began, with his teeth, to unstop it. She took it out of his hand and did it herself. She laid the salve on his palms with the flat of her fingers, light, and the answering coolness came in her own, a half-breath behind. He shut his eyes.

"The noon signal," she said. "I know you waited."

His eyes opened. "I'd have waited longer."

"I couldn't. The queen was at my elbow." She kept her voice flat, because it was the only way to say it. "Noon, the day after the gun. She walked into the drying shed, and she sat beside me the whole hour. She's gentle. She doesn't need to be anything else. I had three thorns in my sash and I couldn't put my hand to them in front of her. If I'd flinched, she'd have wanted to know what I was reaching for."

"You did right."

"I know. I hated it." She wiped the salve from her fingers on the cloth. "I pricked a quarter hour late, once she'd gone. Two noons on, I was on a post-horse at a gallop and couldn't loose a hand from the rein. You'd have stood on the shelf with a sheaf of arrows and a thumb that did nothing. I knew that, and I rode on."

"It did nothing," Devan said, "and I counted the arrows three times." His mouth twitched. "Muthu says I'm the worst liar he's ever known when I'm worried."

"You don't lie."

"I do arithmetic."

It was the closest to a joke he'd made in days, and it was bad, and she took it like bread. She leaned her shoulder against his. He did not move away. The sun was a thin line on the east lip, and the wall beside them was warm already where the stone had held the night's fire.

"Neela," he said. "I have to say something before the others come. I should have said it first, and I've been working out how."

"Is it about the queen's offer?"

"Three hundred spears, and Kettamala. The same she's put to you." He swallowed. "The queen sent me a letter. I answered it before I—"

A horn went off behind them.

It was not Bhaskaran's code. It was the great ram's horn the Company kept at the cairn fire, one long bellow that rolled down the cut and came back off the cliff. Every head on the shelf came up.

It blew again. Then Thilak was running along the wall, a hooked tapping knife in one hand and his grey wrap flying, and he shouted before he reached them.

"Council! Everyone to the fire!"

"Why now?" Devan said. He was already on his feet.

"The vote. The queen's offer." Thilak stopped short, hauling air. His eyes went from one blistered pair of hands to the other, and he looked away. "Koman read the road from the lip at grey. A column behind Usha's riders. Crown banners, and Usha says they're the queen's own. A day and a half back, no more. If we're going to say yes or no, we'll say it before it's at the gate."

Neela got up slowly. Her legs shook with the hunger, and her palms had not stopped burning, and the rest of the sentence was still in Devan's mouth.

*The queen sent me a letter. I answered it before I.*

Before he what?

He looked at her. Whatever it was, it was written plainly across his scorched face, and he did not say it. The horn blew a third time, and she took the hand he held out to her and went to the fire, with half a sentence in her ear and forty-two voices ahead of her.

# Chapter 20: One Name Each

Devan had a mile of cart road to say it in, and he spent all of it not saying it.

Neela walked beside him with her hands bound to the wrist in strips of her own hem. Behind them the Company came up the cut in a long dusty line, the queen's riders mixed among them, two wounded to a horse. The ram's horn had called a council. Muthu had the gate open before the third blast died. He met them under the padippura with his hands open and the iron disc swinging at his belt.

"The lower court's yours," he said. "As long as it takes. The crown's order says no rogues inside my walls. It says nothing about drovers who held the shelf for me."

Kandan, at the back, made a noise through his nose. Muthu did not look at Devan. He looked at the ground, and he was quiet. It was not his lying quiet. Devan had learned the difference late and was glad of it now.

So they sat in the lower court of Kettamala at midmorning, under the tamarind, on the stones where he had learned to ride. The garrison stood along the steps. Janu sent a pail of rice water across the court with a kitchen girl, and a ladle, and no scolding at all, which frightened him more than scolding would. Chellam hushed her baby by the well.

Neela stood in the middle with an upturned grain measure in front of her. She laid things on it one at a time, like a woman paying a debt.

First, a single stiff leaf in a slanting hand. "Usha's page. The queen wrote it fresh the night before I left, and gave it to me." Then a thick sheaf in black ink, tied with green thread. "Forty pages. Chathu wrote them at the queen's table. One for each of you."

"Ours?" said Madhavi.

"Yours. I'll read each one aloud at noon, in daylight, to its owner alone or with whoever they choose. After that it's yours to burn or keep, and nobody can read it against you." Neela's voice was flat and careful. "But everyone in the room hears your shape when I read. That's new mouths. And a master who saw you turn can write you again. Chathu told me that when I was twelve, over Kirri's rat-eaten page. The queen saw every one of her guards turn. Chathu saw every one of you. Both of them are alive. A page is a leaf. A witness is a mouth."

She gave them the offer next. Command of thirty knives with no pages on them, vouched under the crown, no hunter within a day's ride. Three hundred spears for the pass. Kettamala given back to its warden. Safe, unseen, and the queen's.

"It would work," she said. "Nobody would bleed for anybody. I want you to hear that, because it's true."

Lata's chin came up a fraction. She had braced for a lie and not been handed one.

"There is one thing that frees a person from every witness at once. Only one. It's the only way to be rid of the new mouths as well as the old." Neela set her bound hands flat on the measure. "You say your shape aloud, in your own human voice, in the minute you turn at dusk, to one person who is looking at you. Once in your life. It voids every other witness. The queen. Chathu. After that nobody can cage you without your voice joined to theirs."

"And the price," Sanu said.

"The witness page burns where it lies. You're rogue, and the crown may hunt you. And you and that person feel each other's wounds for life." She did not look at Devan. "A mortal one pulls the other toward the ground."

She stopped there. She did not say *so you should* or *so you shouldn't.* He watched her not say it, her jaw set, and thought it was harder than anything she had done on that shelf.

"I won't ask anyone to do it," she said. "I'm asking you to know what it is."

The court stayed quiet. Then Kuttu, from the stones: "She's told us her half. Somebody ought to tell us his."

Every head turned.

Devan's hand had gone to his belt pouch, for the pebble. He stopped it. A pebble kept words behind the teeth, and he needed these out.

"I've been on the other end of it since the night she named herself to me," he said. "I'll say what I have."

His voice came out rough from the smoke. He stood, because sitting felt like hiding.

"A hook opened my ribs in the narrows at noon. The same minute, it opened hers, in the queen's house, among people who would have used the sight of it. I sat on a broken ladder rung and put nine stitches in myself with Kandan's needle, and I could not tell which of us I was sewing." On the steps Kandan looked at his boots. "For four mornings I counted whether she lived by whether I woke to a welt. I couldn't ask a rider. I read my own thumb."

Nobody moved. He kept going, because stopping now would be worse.

"Mine go to her. Last night at dusk I took a burning ladder in my hands to keep it off your wall. She came down the cut with blisters to the wrist. She'd touched nothing. I did that, and I did not ask her, because there's no asking. When I swing a blade I send the blow to the one person I would never choose to hurt."

He made himself look at Neela. She was looking at the measure.

"There's the other side. I know she's breathing. It's the only reason I slept at all. I can't tell you it's worth it for you. I can tell you what I paid and what I got. Don't do it for the wall. Don't do it because I stood here and said it was bearable. If you do it, do it because there's one person you would rather bleed with than be safe without."

"And if there isn't anyone?" Lata said.

"Then you stay vouched, with a roof and a lamp, and I would say that to the queen's face. It isn't nothing."

Lata studied him. "No," she said. "It isn't."

"So we vote on it," Madhavi said. "On who goes with who."

"No." Neela lifted her head. "No vote can choose whom you bind to. You ask one person. They have to be looking at you at dusk, in the minute. Nobody can look for you, and nobody can say yes for you."

For a time nobody did anything. A hen walked across the court.

Pavi stood first. She was small and quick, twenty-four, with a white scar on her chin and an old-broken wrist she held a little crooked. "Thilak," she said.

Thilak was turning a cup in his hands. "I'm forty-one," he said. "I bleed slow and mend slower."

"I know."

"You'll have my knees."

"And you'll have this." Pavi lifted the wrist. "You've been bringing me tea over it for a month."

He laughed once, an ugly, surprised sound. "Yes," he said, and set the cup down as if it might spill.

Kuttu didn't stand. He crossed the court to the gate arch, where Bhaskaran stood with his horn on his belt and his arms folded. "Sergeant," Kuttu said.

"I have four daughters," said Bhaskaran.

"I know."

"Their mother will have words." He looked at the horn for a while. "Twenty years I've carried this. Never carried anyone." He unfolded his arms. "Yes. Ask me when it's dusk."

Kamalakshi was on her feet before the murmur finished. She turned in a slow circle, taking in the whole court. "No," she said. "Not you, or you, or you. I've been looked at by enough people who could do something about it. I'll look at myself." She sat down, and from somewhere near the wall came a short, cracked laugh that might have been hers.

Vellan sat in the doorway with his knife and a scrap of pale wood. He did not lift his head. "No," he said. "I told the lad once."

Sanu was watching Sajan. Sajan put one hand on his brother's shoulder and said nothing, which was not a yes, and was not a no. Sanu looked at the hand as if it were a letter.

"That's all anyone has to say today," Neela said. "The vote on the queen's offer is tonight, after we eat."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Gopan had been oiling the cistern wheel when they came up the east steps. He took one look at the two of them and found somewhere else to be.

The housing was cool and smelled of wet iron. Neela stood with her back to him and her bandaged hands pressed to the stone, as though it were a fever she was trying to draw out. Somewhere below, a ladle rang in a pail.

"How will you vote?" she said. She asked it of the wall.

"I don't need to. I've answered."

"Answered who?"

There was no more road to put it off on. "The queen. Her letter came while you were still on the road. I wrote back within the hour. I refused Kettamala. I wrote that the spears weren't mine to take or leave, and that the Company would answer for itself. Muthu witnessed it. The rider was on the road by morning."

She did not move. He counted the chain through two links.

"You tried to say it at the wall," she said. "The horn cut you off."

"It would have cut me off. I was working up to it. That's not the same."

"No." She turned. Her voice dropped and slowed, each word set down apart from the next. "In the tack room, with the buckets going. *Say it before you do it.* You held the string while I tied that."

"Yes."

"Do you know what I carried down the road?" Her voice stayed low. "Your fort. Four days. If I said no to her, I was saying no to the roof you were born under. I decided I could pay it. I'd decided before Kodiyur was out of sight. I was going to pay it with my eyes open, in front of the Company, and you'd already paid it for me, with a pen." She drew a breath. "I carried a thing that was gone. And now I can't know what you'd have said if you'd waited. Or whether it was a no said for my sake."

He had meant to start with the excuse and put the apology after it. Hearing her, he saw there was no room for the order.

"I broke it," he said. "There's a reason. The rider was ready, you were four days off, the queen wanted an answer. It's true, and it makes the breaking no smaller. I could have held the rider a night. I could have sent him back with nothing. I didn't, and I should have. I'm sorry."

"Don't say it like a report."

He tried again. "I took the price out of your hands before you'd chosen to pay it. That was yours. I'm sorry." The words were not any better, but they were truer. "I said no because the fort isn't worth the Company's vote. That's the whole reason. I won't dress it up as sparing you. You'll have to take that on trust. If I can't tell you first, I'll tell you that I can't, and why. Before."

She watched him. Her thumbnail dragged along her forefinger nail, the bandage leaving the tips bare.

"Hear the rest," she said. "I'm still angry."

"I'm listening."

"Tonight, when the Company votes, I'll say what I think of the offer. If I think you're wrong, I'll say that too, in front of all of them. I'll speak against you if I must." She kept her palms on the stone. "And tomorrow at the gate, you don't answer for me. Not a word. Not to spare me, and not to spare the fort."

It cost him something to hear it, and she watched it land. "Yes," he said.

"You said that fast."

"Then I'll say it slower. I will not answer for you at the gate." He made himself go on. "Here's what I mean to say to her tomorrow, so you have it before I do. That I refused Kettamala in writing, and that was my act alone. That the Company hasn't answered, because the Company hasn't voted. And that I'll say nothing of what you are unless you say it first."

She was silent a while. "Good," she said. "Stand a step behind me when you say it."

"A step."

She turned back to the wall and set her palms on the stone again, and he set his on it a hand's width from hers. The cold of it went into both of them together. He felt the answering ease in his skin half a breath behind her own, and knew she felt his.

"It's mending," she said, after a while. "It isn't mended. It isn't forgotten."

"I'd think less of you if it were."

She nearly smiled. It went out of her face again at once, because someone was coughing at the foot of the steps.

Koman came up with his hands clasped before him, like a man at a naming. He was old, thin, and sunburned to the colour of a roof tile. Nobody had hired him to climb a mile of road, and he had done it anyway. "Warden," he said. Devan had given up correcting him.

"Say it, Koman."

"The long shape is in the river again." He kept his eyes on the floor. "Same bank as before, below the shingle. It came in after the tower fell, at the middle watch, and lay in the shallows with only the eyes above until the stump stopped smoking. It took nothing. It didn't touch the dead on the bank, or a horse. It watched the shelf. When the smoke thinned, it went under." He shook his head. "Eleven years I've read that water. Nothing the Resin House ever made smells like that."

Neela's eyes found Devan's. Neither of them said anything. Whatever lay in that river wanted nothing from the dead. A thing that watched and did not take had been sent, or had come to count.

"Thank you," Devan said. "Go and eat."

Koman bowed, badly, and went down. At the landing he stood aside for a woman coming up with her sleeves dragged to her knuckles.

Usha stopped inside the door and did not come further. She looked at the two of them, and at the hand's width between their hands, and took the news into the room like a stone she meant to set down gently.

"The column reaches Kettamala tomorrow at noon," she said.

"Koman said a day and a half."

"Koman read the dust at grey. I went up the lip with him at midday and read the outriders." Her voice was level. "When she rides with her people, the outriders go in pairs, always, on both flanks. The banners say her household. The pairs say her."

Neela's bound hands closed on the stone.

"She's with them," Usha said. "The queen is riding in her own column. She'll be at your gate by noon."

The chain clanked the hour. A queen who sent a letter and a lever did not climb a hill road to hear no. She came up it to answer. Devan found he had already counted the steps from the gate to the pass, and did not like the number.

# Chapter 21: The Third Slat

Muthu was waiting at the foot of the north stair with a lamp in one hand and his other arm full of bedding.

"I've been told I snore," he said. "So I'm sleeping in the gate chamber tonight. Which is a kindness to everybody."

Neela looked at the bedding, then at him. He went quiet when he lied and loud when he joked, and he was neither now. He was just a big man holding a pillow like a shield.

"The warden's chamber," she said.

"It was always his. I only kept the bed warm." He held out the lamp. Sesame oil, full to the lip. "One night. Janu's changed the sheets and sworn at me while she did it. There's rice and curd on the sill and water in the kindi, spout toward the pillow." He stopped. "I didn't do that last part. She says that's how it's done."

Devan had come up behind her. She felt him there, a step back, as she'd asked.

"Muthu," he said.

"Don't." Muthu raised the pillow an inch. "The slat still creaks. I didn't fix it either."

He went down the stair, whistling something that wandered off the tune, and did not look back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The room had not changed. The teak shutters faced west, closed and latched. The long bar hung on its hooks. The bed stood where it had always stood, and on the floor beside it lay a sword in its scabbard, hilt toward her side, because Muthu had been told once and had never asked why.

She counted the doors. Two. The little one by the hearth made three.

The council had run until the lamps burned low. Nobody would answer the queen until the queen had spoken in the open, at the gate, with the Company standing where she could see them. No vote on her offer had been taken. It was a delay. It was also a door held ajar. Dusk had come with the old pull at her throat, ten counts of burn in the scars, and gone. She was human. The ache would count the night against her. She let it.

Devan set the lamp on the table. Then he went to the pack hanging on the peg and took out an oilcloth bundle tied with red thread.

"I carried it up a hill and down again," he said. "It can sleep one night where we hid it."

He knelt at the third shutter and pressed the housing of the slat. A piece of the frame swung in on a hidden pin, no wider than a hand. He laid the bundle in the hollow and closed it, and the slat gave its small dry creak under his thumb.

"Sit," she said. "Please. I'd like to say a thing before I lose it."

He sat on the edge of the bed. She stayed standing, because the telling wanted her on her feet.

"You asked me at the fire what I thought of the queen's offer. I gave the Company the reasons. I didn't give them the rest." Her bandaged hands were clasped before her. "I wanted it."

He said nothing. He watched her and waited, and the waiting let her go on.

"In the fish shed. She sat on a crate among the racks and asked if she might come in, because there was nothing to knock on. She was kind about it. She offered me thirty knives with no pages on them and a roof with no number on it, and I stood there and wanted it the way I want water after a turning." She heard her voice go flat and let it. "Command, Devan. Nobody over me. And nobody looking. That's the part I'm ashamed of. I could have been unseen again. No one with power over me who'd chosen not to use it, only a quiet house where no one could. Twenty-one years I answered to a number. It was safe. I missed it for an hour, and I hated that I missed it, and I came home and put my hand to the thorns and I still missed it."

"Is it gone?"

"No." She made herself look at him. "It's smaller. It's not gone."

He did not tell her it was nothing. He reached out and turned one of her bound hands over in his, palm up, and set his thumb on the strip of hem where the blister was. Not pressing. Just there.

"Thank you for telling me," he said. "I won't make it smaller than it is."

It was what she had needed, and nobody had ever said it to her.

"Now you," she said. "I know the answer you gave the queen. I don't know the one you gave yourself."

His jaw worked. He looked at the lamp.

"Muthu asked me on the shelf if it hurt," he said. "To refuse her. I said no. That was a lie, and he knew, and he let me have it." He drew breath. "It hurt more than I let him see. Her letter said *Kettamala restored,* and I stood on the shelf and smelled the pepper coming down the cut and I wanted it back. All of it. My father's chair. Asha's door. The shutters open for the air, the way he kept them. I wrote my refusal on the lid of an arrow cart, and my hand didn't shake, and then I went and hit a post for an hour."

"You didn't tell me."

"I told you I wouldn't dress it up as sparing you. I left out that it cost me." His thumb moved on her palm. "I wanted the fort. I still do. I would trade it again for what I got. Both are true."

She sat down beside him, close enough that the bed gave under them. "That one's easier to carry than the other."

"Which other?"

"The one where you're only noble."

He laughed. It came out short and cracked, and it startled him. She leaned into his shoulder and felt it in her own ribs.

"I won't take the command," she said. "That's settled. But I won't refuse it in a corner either, the way I'd refuse a tin of sweets. She hears my reasons in the open, with the Company standing there. If she's wrong, she can be wrong where people hear."

"And if she's right?"

"Then I'll say that too."

He nodded slowly. It was a smaller thing than a vow, and it settled in her like one.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She did not remember who moved first. She remembered undoing the strips of hem from her own hands, one wrap at a time, and his hands taking over when her fingers fumbled. The raw skin of her palms shone in the lamp. His matched it. He turned them side by side and studied the pair for a long moment.

"I'm twenty-seven," she said, because she liked to say it plainly, and because it felt like part of the asking. "You're thirty-two. We're both grown, and nobody here is paying for either of us."

"No one." His voice had gone low. "Neela, if you want to stop, you say. Any time. No reason."

"I know." She laid her cheek against his scarred knuckles. "Leave the lamp."

He went still.

"Keep it lit," she said. "I don't want the dark tonight. I want you to look at me. All of it. The whole time." She swallowed. "I've been seen in a hundred rooms by people who owned the seeing. I want to choose this one."

He took a breath that shook. "I'll look."

"Then do."

His hands came to her face first, careful of the blisters, and he kissed her like a man learning a path. She pulled at his shirt. He lifted his arms so she could drag it over his head, and the lamp showed her what the shelf had cost him. The long ladder-hook cut across his back, dressed and still pulling. On his right ribs, nine stitches of waxed silk, black and fresh, the skin round them angry. Beside them ran the old white seam from the ford.

She put her mouth to the old seam first. Then, slowly, past the stitches, as lightly as she could manage. The ache came anyway, a dull hot line under her own right arm, where the same hook had torn her in a room in Kodiyur. It lay along her ribs like a held note. He made a sound she felt in her chest.

"Does it hurt?" he said.

"Both of us. Don't stop."

"I won't." His voice was rough. "Tell me if it's too much."

When he bared her throat, he stopped. The scars lay there, pale ridges under the lamplight. He looked at them the way he looked at the pass from the wall, and then he put his lips to each in turn. She had once let a man see them as a lie. She let him see them now as a fact, and she did not look away.

"Look at me," she said again.

"I am."

He was. His eyes did not leave her face as he laid her back on the bed that had held the worst night of her life and the best. His hand went under her mundu, slow, a question, and she answered it with her whole body. She reached for him, blistered palms and all, and the burn of her own hands bloomed across his skin at the same moment, and he gasped. Then warmth came through it. The wound-share carried that too, she had learned: a heat in her that she felt twice, once in herself and once in his shoulders, bright and shared.

"You feel that," he said, roughly.

"Both ways."

"Both ways."

It slowed after that. He moved above her, and the lamp showed her his face, the grey in his hair, the line between his brows, which eased and came back and eased. She watched him watch her. She did not close her eyes. When the pleasure rose in her it rose in her face, open, helpless, undefended, and he saw it. She felt him see it.

She said his name. He said hers. Not the number. Not the cover. Her own, the one she had chosen, with the other one folded behind it and still unsaid.

The bed creaked under them. The slat did not.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Afterwards she lay with her head on his good side and the lamp burning down. Her palms ached, pleasantly now. His breathing slowed, and she counted it, as she had counted it for forty nights without telling anyone why.

One, two. The hitch came a third of the way through the long count, soft and sure. She counted again to be sure, and it came again.

"You're counting," he said, into her hair.

"You're awake."

"I'm lying very still so you can." His arm tightened a little. "Is it right?"

"It's always right."

On the sill by the shutters sat a grey river pebble in the dust, exactly where he had left it. Muthu had not moved it. Janu had cleaned the sill around it. She looked at it a long time in the thin, steady light.

She slept.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She woke before the first clank of the chain. The lamp had burned out. Grey light lay under the shutters. Devan was asleep on his back, one hand open on the sheet between them, and she watched him for a while without counting anything.

Then she got up, wrapped a cloth around her, and went to the third shutter on bare feet.

The housing gave under her thumb with its dry creak. He did not stir. The hollow opened.

Inside, the oilcloth bundle lay where he had put it. She untied the red thread with fingers that no longer shook. The hide strip of the contract chit, with the warden's chamber and *death by beast* in a hand she knew. The payment writ, folded small, dated seven weeks before her wedding. And the gift seal, cut from the sandalwood box, green wax with a sunbird and a small chip at the left wing.

She laid the three side by side on the sill, next to the pebble. In the grey they looked small. A hide strip. A folded paper. A piece of wax. Raiu had died of the first. Ammu had died of what came after. She did not think the queen knew what they weighed. She thought she knew exactly.

"It's all there," Devan said behind her.

She had not heard him wake. He was propped on one elbow, hair on end, his eyes already clear.

"You can't leave a bundle in a wall and expect me not to count it."

"I'd have been disappointed." He sat up, and she saw him catch his breath at the stitches. "She comes at noon."

"With three hundred spears." She did not turn from the sill. "What do they do, Devan, when their queen is named a murderer at her own gate?"

He did not reach for comfort. He was quiet, and then he gave her the answer as he gave her everything, flat and whole.

"I don't know. Here's what I do know. Three hundred, under captains of her household who have never met me. On our side, ninety-eight. Forty-two odiyans, now the six are back from Kodiyur. Twenty-two of her own riders, who may not hold when she's named. Forty garrison volunteers. That's a hundred and four, less six in the ground: Biju, Sankaran, Chami, Rajan and two more. Fourteen hundred Thurai wait on the far side of the cut. If one captain lifts a spear when I finish reading, we fight at our own gate with the host at our backs, and we lose the pass. If none lifts, I've still named her a murderer in front of the only men who can hold the cut beside us. Some of them will not forgive me. Some will go home."

"So it might cost us the spears."

"It might cost us the spears and the pass with them. I think that's likely. I'm telling you so you don't find out at noon."

It was no comfort at all. She found, oddly, that she could stand on it.

"Then read it," she said.

"I'll read the writ aloud before her. In daylight, at the gate, with the Company and the garrison and the village standing there. The whole of it, her wording, her wax. Not hidden. Not whispered in a stair." He swung his legs over the bed's edge. "And I'll tell her first, to her face, that I'm going to."

"Why first?"

"Because the last man to keep a thing in his sleeve against me was Chathu." He met her eyes. "I said I'd say it before I do it. I mean to keep that."

She looked at the green chipped wax on the sill. For weeks that seal had ridden in his own sleeve, and he had told nobody. He saw where her eyes went.

"I know," he said. "I kept one too. I put the seal in the bundle on the hill, so it's there for you to see. I'm telling you before, and not after."

She almost laughed. It stopped short of her mouth.

"Then here's the term," she said. "You read. I refuse. My refusal, my reasons, in my own voice, to her face. You don't carry any of it."

"I'll stand a step behind you. I won't speak for you."

"Is reading the writ not answering for me?"

He took the question seriously, as he took all of hers. "The writ is my account of what she did to me and mine. That's mine to read. The Company's answer is yours."

She folded the bundle back together. Her hands knew the knot. She still wanted to ask whether he would use the writ as a rope or only as a light, and she found he had not promised her which. Neither did she know.

"Then I'll have my own words ready," she said. "Hold the pebble."

"Why?"

"Because I want to see you hold it," she said, "and not put it in your mouth."

He took the stone from the sill. It was cold, and it sat in his scarred palm like something small and honest, and he closed his hand around it and did not lift it. Down in the court the cistern chain clanked again, and the first cocks answered.

# Chapter 22: The Queen at Kettamala

Neela counted the spears from the padippura roof and got to three hundred and four before the first of them reached the shingle bank.

They came up the cart road in a double file, green pennants limp in the noon heat. Grey dust rose behind them. At the head rode a woman on a grey horse, one hand loose on the rein. No woman who knew she was going to be refused rode like that. Usha walked at her stirrup, bareheaded, sleeves pulled to her knuckles. Behind them, in a tight knot, rode seven guards in green sashes. Neela knew every one of their names from the drying shed in Kodiyur. They had been offered their pages and had said no. The eighth who had said no, Lata, was not with them. She had ridden north with Usha's riders and was already up on the shelf, and she had not looked at the road once.

"Three hundred and four," Neela said.

"Three hundred and three," said Devan, behind her. "One's a boy with a drum."

The garrison lined the court in grey. Bhaskaran's horn stayed at his belt. Nobody had told the Company to hide, so the Company did not. They stood at the back of the lower court, forty-one of them, in their own mud-coloured cloth, and let the queen look.

She looked. The gate took her through under the tamarind, and she slid from the saddle without help. She was fifty-four, small, her hair gone iron-grey under a plain white cloth. She wore no crown. Her hands were ink-stained at the second finger, like a clerk's.

The garrison began to kneel. It went along the steps in a ripple, spears grounding.

"Stand," Nirmala said, in the warm unhurried voice Neela had heard through the screens outside the sea room. "This is a warden's court, and I'm his guest. Neela. I'd rather see your face than the top of your head."

Neela stayed on her feet. She had planned no speech for it. She stood where she was, hands bound in clean strips, and said, "Majesty. Kettamala is glad you've come. It was not told you were coming."

"It wasn't. I hoped to be forgiven." The queen's eyes went past her to Devan and rested there with what looked like real warmth. "Warden."

"Majesty." Devan did not bow lower than a warden bows. His voice was level and carried to the back steps. "I hold a paper with your wax on it. I'll read it when my wife has answered you. Not before."

Spear butts shifted on the stone along the steps, then went still. Neela kept her eyes on the queen's face and did not turn, because they had agreed he would say it and she would hear it, and she heard it.

The queen's brows lifted a little. "Then I'll wait on your wife," she said. "I'm told it's the only way to get anything done in this house."

Muthu came down from the gate chamber stair with the iron disc swinging at his belt and his big hands empty. He did not bow. He touched two fingers to his brow, as a captain does to a guest, and stood straight. The queen studied the disc, and then him.

"You hold my seal, Captain."

It was a test, laid so lightly that half the court would not have felt it.

"I hold the border's, Majesty." Muthu's voice was quiet and steady. He was not lying. "It was given into my hand by the man who wore it five years, and I hold it for the villages. If the border wants it back on a different neck, the border will say so."

Somewhere behind him a spear butt scraped a step. Neela watched the queen weigh the answer, as she had watched men weigh a blade at a throat. Then Nirmala smiled, and let it stand.

"A good answer. I need a man who holds the pass more than I need one who holds my seal." She turned. "Is there a table in this house? I have a petition waiting."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The hall had eight pillars. Neela had counted them on her first morning as a bride, and counted them again now, because it kept her mind steady. Janu had put out rice and curd and plain water, no better than for any guest, and stood against the wall with a tray in both hands.

The queen sat in the warden's chair. Devan did not take the seat beside it. He came and stood behind Neela's bench, near enough that she could hear him breathe. He had said he would stand at her back and not be her wall, and he stood a step back, as she'd asked.

"Before anything else," the queen said, "I'll hear who is waiting."

Neela had expected a ceremony of speeches. Instead a thin woman in a washed grey wrap was brought up from the court, coughing into her sleeve, and held out a tally stick as if it might burn her.

"Parukutty," said Neela, before she could stop herself.

The woman flinched at her own name. The queen looked from one to the other and said nothing of it.

"Soldier's widow, lower village," Parukutty said. "My husband was Raman. Spearman. I've a girl of six and a boy of nine." Her voice shook and straightened. "Majesty, the Justice paid mine early, and I thank you. But there are three more on the wall rolls, and the lower village will burn when the Thurai come through. Their death-gifts are due at harvest. There won't be a harvest. Could they be paid now, so they can buy bread on the road? And Chandu wants to carry arrows on the wall. He's nine. I'd rather he carried rice."

"Raman," Nirmala said. She said it as one says the name of someone one has met. "Killed in the spring, in single file under the shelf. The patrol of eleven." She did not look at a clerk, because there was none. "Minnu and Chandu. Is the girl's cough better?"

Parukutty stared. "It's mine, Majesty. The girl's well."

"Then you should have a physician's willow bark, and not wait for a harvest." The queen drew a sheet of paper toward her, took the reed Janu had brought, and wrote in a quick slanting hand. She signed it *Nirmala* in full. No stamp, no seal. "Three widows on the wall rolls, paid now, from my own purse. And Chandu does not carry arrows. He carries rice to the arrow-bearers, and eats first." She blew on the ink and held it out. "If anyone tells you otherwise, show them this."

Parukutty took the paper in both hands. She did not kneel. She seemed to have forgotten how.

Neela watched the queen's ink-stained finger tap the table once, then rest. This was real, the whole of it. It was the same hand that had kept a dead spearman's name and his children's names since spring, and had reached for a cough the moment it heard one. It was also the hand that had sealed a writ in green wax and sent Vellan up the west stair, where Raiu had died.

Both are hers, Neela thought. She could not make them into one woman, though she had been trying since Kodiyur. Behind her, Devan's breath stopped for a moment, and started again.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


When the widows were gone the queen turned to the rest of the hall. "Now to the thing I came for."

A guard set a red lacquer case on the table. Neela knew it by its gold bird and its farm lock. The queen lifted the lid. Thirty stiff pale leaves lay on blue cloth, each with a clerk's copy of its intake roll looped on in green thread.

"The crown leaf," Nirmala said. "Fresh pages, in my own hand, written the night before your wife left Kodiyur. Usha's I wrote twice; your wife carries the first. I mean to burn them in front of you all, once you've heard my terms."

Neela reached into her wrap. She laid Chathu's sheaf beside the case, tied with its green thread, forty pages in black ink. "Mine," she said. "Chathu wrote these at your table. They're not burned. I told you why."

"You did." The queen did not seem hurt by it. "Look at them together, if you like. I'd be a poor queen if I minded being read."

Neela bent over the two piles. She did it for the Company and for the garrison, and because it kept her face busy. The queen's leaves gave off a bitter smell, scorched and sharp, like resin gone too long on a flame. Chathu's smelled like a lamp-black cake, dry, the smell of a clerk's ink pot.

She frowned. The two should have smelled alike. They were both pages in the same resin-ink, written by true witnesses. She turned the thought over but could not make it come out. When she straightened, it slid off like water off a leaf.

"Well?" said the queen gently.

"Nothing," said Neela. "I wanted to know them again."

"Then know them." Nirmala's gaze moved over Neela's shoulder. "Warden. I'll be plain, in your own hall, before your own people. The offer I sent still stands. Kettamala is yours. The disc, the pass, the garrison, your father's chair. Muthu may give back what he's held, with my thanks and a captaincy for it."

Every head in the hall turned. Neela kept her eyes on the pages and listened to the quiet behind her. This was the moment where a man could reach past his wife for what was his by blood, and the whole house knew it.

Devan said nothing.

Sandals stopped shifting on the stone. Janu's tray rattled once against her wrist. Devan did not step forward to take the chair or to refuse it. He did not so much as look at the disc on Muthu's belt.

The queen waited. Then she nodded, slowly, as if he had said a great deal. "Think on it," she said. "You'll have your answer when your wife has given hers."

The hall let go. It was a small noise, sandals scuffing, a cough, a spear butt set down hard in the passage. Without turning, Neela moved her bound right hand back along the bench, and Devan's fingers closed over the strip at her wrist, warm and dry. His thumb found the blister under the cloth and stayed there. Neither of them looked down.

"That was a great deal of nothing," she said, low.

"You told me not to speak for you," Devan said, just as low. "I took it as wide advice."

Her mouth twitched. His thumb pressed once on the strip, then he let go, and she faced the room with her hand in her lap and the print of his fingers still on it.

"Janu," she said.

The steward jumped. "Lady?"

"Sit down."
