"I'll not sit while the queen—"

"It's your hall," Neela said. "You fed her. You've kept it standing all these years, and you stood against the wall while she wrote a widow's bread on your table." She pulled out the bench beside her. "Sit."

Janu looked at the queen. The queen inclined her head a finger's breadth, a small permission, her mouth dry with watching amusement. Janu put the tray down on the stone with a clatter that rang off the pillars. She sat. Her hands went flat on her knees. Around the hall, a dozen kitchen women found sudden business with their sleeves and their sandals.

Neela kept her face still. She had never in her life been so sure that she had taken something and given it away in the same moment.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The west stair had forty-four steps. Neela went up them at dusk, because the queen asked it.

"Would you walk with me," Nirmala had said, after the meal, after the spears had been settled on the terraces and Usha had gone to see to the seven in green. "The warden's wife may choose the stair."

Neela had chosen this one. She had gone wide of the parrot newel from her first week, without knowing why. She knew why now. She had stood at its foot with a shuttered lamp, six years ago, and counted. She laid a hand on its broken beak and felt it smooth as a river stone.

They climbed together. Nirmala's breath was short at the tenth step. She stopped on the eleventh, and it rang under her sandal with a hollow note.

"Here," she said.

Neela's hand tightened on the rail. Pala wood, worn.

"Rema's roll took down the physician's account: the postern, the eleventh step. I came up knowing which one to stand on." The queen did not look at her. "She was Devan's sister. I met her once, at the Kolathur fair. She argued with me over the price of pepper-sacks, and was right."

Neela's fingers went white on the rail. Her mouth had gone dry, and for one breath she had the whole sentence ready: *I held the lamp.* The queen had Rema's roll. She would know it already, and it would be no gift to her to hear it. Neela shut her mouth on it. She would say it where it cost the speaker, in the open, and not on a dark stair to the one who had paid for it.

"Ittan paid for Asha," she said instead. Her voice came out very even. "Not you."

"Not I. That was Ittan's coin, and Chathu's knife, and a bear's. I'd have stopped it, had I known. I will not pretend I never would have signed a thing like it." The queen turned from the stair and faced her. In the grey dusk her face was tired and honest. "I sealed the payment writ. Usha saw the bundle in your husband's pack on the hill, and she has told me what such a bundle holds. He carries it up and down my hills. He was loyal, Neela. He was also a week from opening a box in Ittan's keeping, and what was in that box held a price I had paid for twenty years. Ships off this coast. Boats that stayed on the sea. That price is not paid now. It failed this season, when the fleet crossed the bar, and the Thurai are at the cut."

Neela heard it land, and heard what it took with it. Devan meant to read that writ at the gate and watch the crown's own spears hear it for the first time. The queen had just said it herself, in a half-lit stair, as calmly as a price at market. There would be no gasp now. Anyone who heard of this talk could say the queen had owned the thing already.

It would still have to be read. A confession made to one woman on a stair was a secret with a kind face. Said before three hundred spears, it was a fact.

"So you killed a loyal man to keep it paid."

"I tried to. I'd do it again, if the arithmetic stood the same." There was no cruelty in it, and that was worse than cruelty. "A realm is kept safe by what it never has to see. A widow in a village sleeps because she has never watched a knife go in. I've signed death writs for people who loved me and had done nothing but get in the way, and I have not slept well after any. But the villages slept. Until this season, they slept."

"Ammu is dead."

"Ammu. Twenty-five. Her name is in Rema's roll." Nirmala's voice did not change. "I'm sorry."

It was not an excuse. Neela had expected one, and could have fought an excuse. She stood on the eleventh step with a dead woman's name in her mouth and no place to put her hands.

"You'd have kept me unseen," she said. "Thirty knives, no pages. You said it kindly."

"I meant it kindly." The queen almost smiled. "It is the only gift I know how to give. I am sorry, too, that you don't want it. I think you're right not to."

A cistern chain clanked in the dark. Down in the court somebody laughed, and it was Kamalakshi, and the sound of it carried up the stair like a thrown pebble.

"My spies tell me two nights," Nirmala said, her voice shifting into a quieter, harder register. "The main assault, not the scouts. Fourteen hundred, with a new tower and ladders, up through the cut. Two nights from tonight. I'll have my captain give the order and mean it, whatever else stands between us." She drew a slow breath. "I'd have chosen a private room. He's chosen the court. I'll allow it."

"I'll answer at the dusk before the assault," Neela said. "In the open."

"In front of whom?"

"Everyone. Your captains. The garrison. The Company. Whoever else is left in this house." She heard her own voice, flat and slow. "You said the realm is kept safe by what it never sees. I'd like it to see. All of it, including what you've just told me."

The queen looked at her for a long time. Then, unexpectedly, she bowed her head, not as a queen to a subject but as one woman who had run a house to another who meant to.

"Then the realm will hear you choose," Nirmala said, "between what it never saw and what it cannot unsee. I would not envy you, if I were not fond of you." She turned and began to climb. At the landing she paused, one hand on the rail. "I did not lie to you in the fish shed, Neela. I have never needed to. It is the cruelest thing about me."

She went up into the dark passage.

Neela did not wait for her steps to fade. She went down the forty-four steps two at a time, past the newel without slowing, and across the lower court where the Company sat in knots around their cook-fires. The great ram's horn hung from the well post, where Kuttu had slung it after the council. She lifted it down. It was heavier than it looked, and the mouthpiece was cold against her lip.

She blew one long bellow. It rolled off the walls and up the terraces, and every head in the court came round.

They came to her in ones and twos, then all at once: Kuttu with his bowl still in his hand, Thilak wiping his knife, Vellan limping up from the gate arch. Bhaskaran's sentries leaned over the wall. At the edge of the firelight, Devan stopped a step behind her, and said nothing.

"The queen has given us two nights," Neela said. Her voice shook, and she let it. "I'll give her my answer at dusk, the day after tomorrow, at the gate. In the open, with all of you standing where she can see you." She looked from face to face. "Every one of you hears it before she does. Tonight, at the cairn fire, I'll tell you what I mean to say. Then you tell me if I'm wrong."

# Chapter 23: What Vellan Carries

The queen's captain would not take an order from a man with no office, and he said so before Devan had finished unrolling the map.

"You gave back the disc in front of this whole hall," Captain Haran said. He was a broad, grey-moustached man who sat his chair as if it were a saddle. "I carry the queen's word to three hundred spears. I don't carry yours."

They sat at the long table in the hall, under the eight pillars: Haran, Muthu with the iron disc at his belt, Usha with her sleeves pulled down, Thilak with his big tapper's hands flat on the board, Neela at Devan's left. The queen sat at the head of the table in the warden's chair, with a cup of water she had not touched. She had said she would listen.

Devan had drawn the cut in chalk on a plank. Forty paces wide at the gorge. Narrower at the shelf, where Raman had died. Fourteen hundred on the far side of the river.

For five years, men had done what he said because the disc was on his belt. Nobody had ever answered him, and he had never had to hear whether he was right.

"Then don't take it from me," he said. "Put it to a vote."

Haran's moustache lifted. "A vote."

"Five of you hold something on that wall. You hold the crown spears. Muthu holds the garrison and the seal. Usha has the queen's riders, Thilak the Company's hands, and Neela the Company's voice. I hold nothing." He laid the chalk down. "I'll say what I'd do, and each of you can say yes or no. If it's no, I sit down."

"Sutlers vote," Haran said. "Wardens command."

"Let him, Haran," Nirmala said from the chair. She turned the cup a quarter turn. "I'd like to see what happens."

Devan told it plain. Crown spears in three ranks on the shelf wall, behind the stakes Kandan's men had cut, because they were the only ones with the numbers to take the first ladders. Garrison archers behind them, with Muthu's eleven sheaves. The Company in the thorn below the lip and on the wall by turns, rotated by the hour so no one was spent before the tower came. Kandan's ten to the cart road, to cut the horse lines if the line bent.

"And where does my household stand when the line bends?" Haran said.

"Where it can retreat to the wall. I'd rather your men lived and ran than died and held." Devan heard how it sounded. "That's the part you'll like least."

Neela spoke for the first time. "Captain. Who is your second?"

Haran looked at her as if she had offered him a sweet. "Sethu."

"Then I'll want Sethu at the stakes with Kandan before dusk," she said. "They should know each other's faces before they have to know each other's backs."

Nobody laughed. Haran's mouth worked.

"Hands," Muthu said, in a voice that was almost his old loud one.

Muthu's went up. Usha's. Thilak's. Neela's. Devan raised his own last, and it felt strange, a hand held up like a boy's among people who could have sent him from the room.

Haran kept his on the table. "No."

"Five to one," said Muthu.

The captain studied the chalk plank for some time. "I'll hold the centre," he said. "Not under that man. Under the table." He glanced at the queen. She gave him nothing, only the dry watching look she had worn the day before.

"That will do," Devan said. To his surprise, it would.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At dusk he went down to the shelf.

The stakes stood in a double row, new-cut ash, the points still pale. Below the lip the river ran brown and loud. Beyond it, the Thurai fires made a low orange smear along the far bank. He counted a hundred and ten of them and stopped.

Vellan was at the lip, where the cut narrowed toward the top. The bear's limp showed in the man today, a drag and a step. He stood with his arms crossed, looking at the place where the walls drew in to eleven paces.

"There," he said. "I'll hold that."

Devan came and stood beside him. "The cut is the last narrow before the gate road. If they take it, nothing stands between them and the court."

"That's why."

"Alone?"

"Unbound." Vellan did not turn. "No one has named herself to me. If I'm cut there, no one else bleeds for it. I've handed enough people my wounds." He gave the cut a nod, as he might have to a bad neighbour. "Let the others bleed for the people they chose. I'll bleed for nobody."

"The Company voted the line. You'd be off it."

"The Company's voted me a hand. Mine's up." A small rough sound came out of him that might have been a laugh. "You'd have done the same."

Devan would have, once. He did not say it. The wind came down the cut, and with it, faintly, the smell of the far bank's smoke.

"She said—" Vellan began.

Devan went still.

The big man's jaw moved. His eyes were on the narrow place, not on Devan. "Asha. At the end. She said—" He stopped. "No."

"Vellan."

"After." Vellan uncrossed his arms. "If there is an after. Not on the night before a fight. You'd carry it up the cut instead of your spear."

Devan had waited six years. One more night he could do. What he felt was not forgiveness; he did not have that to give yet. It was nearer to what a man feels when a hard animal finally lets him within reach. He did not step closer.

"After, then," he said.

Footsteps scuffed the path behind them. Kamalakshi came up from the gate arch with a rag bundle held against her ribs, the way someone carries a thing she's afraid will be seen. She was perhaps nineteen, thin, with her hair cropped short, and the shelf stones rang under her sandals as if she were trying to make them.

She stopped three paces from Vellan.

"Here," she said, and held the bundle out at arm's length.

He unwrapped it. A bird, the length of his thumb, carved from pale wood. One wing was thicker than the other. The head sat too large for the body, and the beak, which should have been whole, was a blunt wedge that looked ready to bite.

"It's bad," Kamalakshi said.

"Yes."

"I haven't forgiven you."

"I know."

"Don't know it so loudly." She scrubbed her palm on her hip. "I did it with your knife. I wanted to see if it was hard."

"It's hard."

"Good." She turned on her heel and left, and did not run, which Devan thought must have cost her.

Vellan stood with the bird in his hand a long time. Then he put it inside his shirt, over the heart, and turned back to the cut.

Devan looked at the river. He gave the man the privacy of a view, as Neela had once given it to him, and the Thurai fires crawled on the far bank, thick and slow.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The pit below the east wall smelled of sesame oil and red earth. Lamps burned in their niches round the rim. Muthu was already down in it, barefoot, his shirt on the step.

"The seventh day," he said. "You owe me a round. I counted."

"I know I do."

"You've owed it since the Resin House. I'd like the interest in blood." Muthu grinned. It was his old grin, wide and a little foolish. "Don't worry. I'll be gentle with the stitches."

"Don't be."

Devan went down the north steps barefoot. The ribs pulled under the silk. He let them. Muthu knew where the old seam was and where the new stitches were, and Devan said so, and Muthu said he'd take it as a map.

They did not use marma. They went to touches. Muthu came in low and fast, as he always had, and Devan gave ground and took it back, and the packed earth slapped under their feet. It had been eight years, and then a few mornings, and the body remembered what it had been taught as a boy. His open hand stopped a hair from Muthu's ribs. He had practised that stop alone for years, in front of a post, and now there was a man at the end of it, laughing, who lifted his own hand to answer.

On the fifth pass, Muthu's palm landed on Devan's left side below the ribs. It was light. It would have been a rib broken, and a lung after, if either of them had meant it.

"There," Muthu said, breathing hard. "It drops. When you're tired. It always did."

"I know."

"You've always known. You've never done anything about it." He stepped back, rubbing his chest. "Who found it, then?"

Devan looked up. Neela sat on the north steps with her bound hands around her knees. She had not made a sound. Her lips were moving in a small count, steps or breaths, he could not tell.

"My wife," he said.

"She told you."

"She came down those steps on the eleventh day, put her fingers at the back of my neck, counted three, and told me my left dropped. I didn't turn. I held my breath." He wiped his face with his forearm. "I never did anything about it."

"Then do something," Muthu said. He went up the steps for his shirt, passed Neela, and put two fingers to his brow. It was a captain's salute, given to no one in the garrison, and she returned it with her chin.

Devan climbed out. The lamps burned steady. He stood in front of her with the sweat cooling on him, and for a breath he did not know how to ask.

"On the wall," he said. "In the line. Wherever I'm standing when the ladders come. Will you take my left side?"

She did not answer at once. She looked at the pit, at the packed earth, the marks of their feet.

"I'll be a cat by then," she said.

"Then the cat." He held her eyes. "My left drops. You know it, you found it. I'd rather it were yours to cover than anyone's. It's the weak side. I'm putting it in your hands."

"Whether it's hurt or not, I'll feel it."

"I know. That's why you."

Her mouth moved, and she did not smile. It was not that kind of thing. She reached out and put two fingers, light as a clerk's, on the cloth over his left ribs, where Muthu's palm had landed.

"Yes," she said.

He let out a breath that had nothing hidden in it.

On the way up the steps he saw that Muthu had watched the whole of it, and so had the lamps and the men on the wall, who had come to look at a warden fighting. He had used his strength in the open. Men would vote on what he said and count how badly he had stood. He had thought it would feel like having his skin off. It felt like being seen. He found he could bear it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


A hand on his shoulder in the middle watch.

He was awake before the voice. The lamp in the passage showed him Koman, the old pass-village watcher, with Unni behind him holding a torch. Koman's breath came in short pulls, and his eye had the stillness of a man who had run up a hill.

"Warden," he said, and Devan did not correct him. "I was on the lip. The far bank is moving."

Neela had sat up beside him without a sound. Her hand had found his wrist, as if it knew.

"Moving how?"

"The spies said after dark, the second night. They'll come at dusk, while the light's still in our eyes." Koman wet his lips. "They've brought the tower up in the dark to the shingle. They're not waiting for full night. I counted ladders until I couldn't." He spread his hands. "Dusk tomorrow."

Devan swung his legs to the floor. The stitches pulled. He felt Neela's other hand close on the sheet.

"The queen's spies were wrong," he said.

"The queen's spies," Koman said, with a flat old man's patience, "were not on the lip."

Unni's torch spat. Down in the court the cistern chain clanked the hour, three, and was answered by nothing.

Neela was already out of the bed and reaching for her cloth. Her face in the torchlight had the shut calm of someone doing sums she did not like.

"I set my answer for dusk tomorrow," she said. "At the gate. With the Company and the queen."

"Yes."

"That's the hour they come." She tied the cloth at her waist with a hard small pull. "My answer and the first ladder, in the same dusk. She'll want it sooner. She'll have to. They all will."

"You don't have to give it by first light."

"No." She looked at him. "But I won't be given the time to be afraid of it either. I'll go and wake the queen."

# Chapter 24: The Naming Dusk

The queen said yes to the afternoon before Neela had finished asking.

"I thought you might," Nirmala said, sitting up in the dark of the guest room off the north range, her hair in a plain white plait. "A woman who has to be brave will want it done before she can be talked out of it."

"I won't be talked out of it."

"No. But you'd like the option to be tired of the idea. I'm sorry I can't give it to you." She reached for her cloth. "The lower court. When the sun is over the well post."

So it was the lower court, in the long heat after noon, with the dust lying still on the stones and the ram's horn hanging silent on its post.

Neela stood in the middle of the court with her bound hands at her sides. At the cairn fire last night she had told the Company what she meant to say. Kuttu had tried to argue her out of half of it, and Madhavi had made him stop. Now they stood along the north wall in their mud-coloured cloth, forty-one of them, with Vellan at the end, apart, arms crossed. Opposite them stood the queen's thirty. They had come down from the terraces in their green sashes with their sleeves pulled to the knuckle. Usha was at their head. Lata was at the back, looking at the ground.

The queen sat on a plain bench under the tamarind. Haran stood at her right, Sethu at his shoulder. Muthu had the iron disc at his belt. Janu had come out with her tray and stood by the kitchen door with her chin up, not sitting this time.

Devan stood a step behind Neela's left shoulder. She knew it without looking. She could hear the small hitch in his breath.

"Majesty," she said. "You offered me thirty knives with no pages. Safe, unseen, and yours. The offer was kind. I'm going to refuse it."

No sound came from the garrison. Somewhere on the wall a sandal scraped.

"You asked me to be their master," Neela said. She did not look at the thirty. "I'll say what that means, since nobody here has ever said it plainly. A page owns a woman. You'd burn the pages, so you'd say you'd freed them. But the queen would still be the one who saw them turn. They'd be unseen to everyone but you. Unseen is owned. It is the same cage with the bars painted out."

She counted her breaths. Four. She went on.

"I was Seventeen for twenty-one years. I know what it is to be safe because no one can see you. It was never safety. It was waiting to be sold." She turned her head, then, to the green sashes. "I will not be your master. I won't hold your pages or your names. Nobody in this court will hold a leash over another person while I live. The Company fights by vote. It is bound by what its hands choose, and by nothing else. If the hands say no to me, I sit down."

"That's a lot of sitting down," Kuttu said, from the wall, not quite under his breath. A few of the Company laughed, a short, startled sound.

Neela's mouth twitched. It helped. It always helped, and she hated that.

"One more thing." Her voice went quieter, and she let it. "Before you all. When I came to Kettamala I came to kill the warden. I was paid for it. I had the night, the window and the knife." She breathed in. "I told him my name instead."

She took a step to the side. Not toward him. Only out of his way, so that the court could see.

"This is Devan. I chose him. Nobody gave me to him, and nobody paid for it. I said the one word that could have caged me to the one man who could have used it, and he never did. That is how I know what free is. It is not belonging to no one. It is choosing who you're tied to, and being able to walk away, and staying." Her hands had gone cold. "I'd like the realm to know whose wife I am, and why."

Behind her, Devan was silent. She had asked him to be. But his breath left him, slow and ragged, and a moment later his fingers touched the back of her bound hand. He did not take it. He only let her know he was there. In front of three hundred spears, that was louder than a speech.

The queen sat very still. Then she nodded, once, slowly, as she had nodded in the hall.

"Warden," she said. "You had a paper."

Devan's hand went to his coat. The whole court turned to him.

"I said I'd read it once my wife had answered," he said. "She has. Majesty, do you want it read before the ladders, or after?"

It was how he asked everything, flat and exact, with the choice left in the other person's hands. Neela did not look round.

"After," Nirmala said. Her mouth had a dry, tired line in it. "I know what it says. Read now, in front of Haran's three hundred, it would break the line tonight. Men do not hold a shelf for a queen they have just heard named. Read it once the ladders come down."

"Then after." Devan took his hand from his coat. "I won't read it unasked."

"Thank you. Now I answer the warden's wife."

She stood, and the whole court shifted.

"I could say I'd keep my pages and my guard," she said. "I could say it, and Captain Haran would be glad. But she is right about the cage, and I am too old to make a show of not seeing it." She looked past Neela, to the green sashes. "So. The night-guard stays mine until each of you frees herself. That is the price, and it is a hard one, and I won't hide it. Every guard who names herself will carry her partner's wounds, and her partner hers. Each wound counts twice, once as blood and once as a welt. A death can pull a second one after it. I'll have a night-guard that cannot be cut without hurting someone she loves. I have weighed that. I think it is worth the weight."

The garrison stood very quiet.

"I will read no page tonight. I say it in front of my captains. Every leaf I hold stays quiet, and no one will be caged by my voice. I need you whole." Her gaze rested on Usha, and then moved along the row, face by face. "Each of you chooses. At this dusk, with the Company, on the shelf, you may name yourselves to someone you've chosen and fight carefully, knowing it. Or you may stay unbound and fight as mine. I won't think the worse of either. I will think the worse of any who choose without looking at the cost."

She sat. It was done.

Neela stood where she was while the court came to pieces around her. Voices rose, boots shuffled, somebody started to weep without noise. Janu was crossing the stones toward her with the tray. The queen had named the price, Neela thought, but not one person who would pay it. She would do that herself, by name, tonight.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


After the sun went under the ridge, the shelf was the colour of old brass. The stakes stood in their rows, pale at the points. Below the lip the river ran loud, and on the far bank the Thurai fires were thick and low, with something big and square moving among them.

Neela counted what she could see. Sixty breaths between the last light and the first drum. Eleven sheaves behind the wall. The tower on the shingle, three men high.

She did not count what she could not. Thirty of the queen's, forty-one of her own. Every one of them would be a cost.

They came out onto the shelf in the going light. The queen's chair had been carried down, and Nirmala sat in it with the red lacquer case open on her lap, the thirty crown leaves laid on blue cloth. Neela sat on a flat stone at her side with Chathu's forty fresh leaves across her knees, the Company's pages written at the queen's table, held under a board against the wind. On top of them lay the members' own Resin House pages, handed to her for the dusk. Between them they would see every naming burn. Haran's spears stood behind them with their backs turned to the shelf, as Devan had ordered, the garrison facing the road, counting sixty.

"No one smears who isn't naming," Neela said. "No one turns who isn't ready. If you want to wait for the next dusk, there will be one."

"There might not," said Pavi, flatly.

"There might not," Neela agreed.

The first to go was Pavi. She walked across the shelf to Thilak and smeared her throat and wrists without a hand to steady her. Her old-broken forepaw was still a bent wrist for one more minute. Thilak stood where she had put him, with his big tapper's hands open at his sides. When the burn took her, she looked at his face and said something low and quick, in her own voice, and the otter finished on the stones in front of him.

Neela did not hear the word. It was not hers to hear.

On her knee, Pavi's own Resin House page caught where it lay on Chathu's sheaf. A thin line of flame ran along the witness entry and ate it. The intake roll fastened beneath it stayed whole, the green thread unscorched. Neela slid it free and pressed it flat under the board. A name and a village were not the guild's to burn.

Kuttu went to Bhaskaran. The sergeant of the padippura stood with his helm under his arm. At the cairn fire an hour ago he had told his wife, by name, what he meant to do, before he said yes to anyone, so that she would hear it first. Kuttu was shaking too hard to smear the resin. Bhaskaran took the pot from him and did it with his bare fingers, very slowly, because a man with four daughters could do a thing slowly. The resin bit. His fingertips went red and then raw, and he hissed through his teeth and kept going until the last wrist was done. Then he wiped his hand on his thigh and stood and held still. A second leaf curled on Neela's knee, and she saved the roll beneath it.

Sanu went to Kandan. The corporal had a scar through one brow, and a lame mother in Mathoor, and Neela had once said his name and been answered. Sanu had wanted his brother. His brother stood at the back with hands shut, Sajan, not ready, not refusing. Sanu looked once toward him and said, "I'll ask you again after." Then he turned to Kandan, who was white around the mouth and standing as if braced for a blow, and held out his hands.

"Not you," Kandan said. "I'm nobody's—"

"You're the one who said my name when you knew it," Sanu said. "That's enough."

It was true. On the cart road, when Sanu's horse went lame at the boulder, Kandan had asked his name once, from the saddle, and had used it every time since: *Left, Sanu. Water, Sanu.* No one in the green sashes had been called anything in years but a number. Kandan closed his mouth. He nodded. In the queen's lap, a leaf of the thirty caught. Nirmala watched it burn. She made no move to put it out.

They went on. Thulasi started toward Vellan's side of the shelf, remembered he would take no one, and walked to Madhavi instead. A heavy-set guard from the queen's thirty, who had said no in Kodiyur, crossed to a man in Haran's rank, a stranger, a spear-captain with a grey moustache. He took his helm off. Neela knew neither name. Company leaves curled on her knee, guards' leaves in the queen's lap. By the time the red had gone off the ridge, the lacquer case had half emptied itself into ash, and the wind off the river carried the smell of it, scorched and bitter.

Neela kept the ledger in her head, because it was easier than watching faces. Each of them rogue now, in the eyes of the crown. Each of them leaving a page that had burned where it lay. Each wound counted twice, once as blood and once as a welt, and each death able to pull a second after it. A line made of pairs, and a Company that could not afford to be hurt in two places at once.

Devan stood beside her and said nothing. He had ordered the wall. He was holding the shelf. Whatever came up the cut would find the line bent round these people, who had chosen to be bled.

Usha went last.

The light had thinned to the colour of weak tea. The Thurai drums had started, one and then three, as if tuning. Usha crossed the shelf with her sleeves still pulled down, and the paste on her throat cracked a little as she swallowed. She went to no one on the wall. She went to the queen's chair, and stopped three paces in front of it.

"Majesty," she said. "Look at me."

Nirmala's hand stayed on the case. The topmost leaf lay under it, the one with Usha's number, Nineteen, in a quick slanting hand.

Neela saw the old woman's face. She saw the queen weigh it as she had weighed everything, a realm's worth of arithmetic folded into one pause. Nirmala could have looked away. Anyone could have looked at the river, or the case, or Haran. Looking would cost her. It would give the girl she had once sealed into a green sash a share of her wounds, and a woman who had never in fifty-four years shared a cut with anyone would share them with a guard.

The queen looked.

She raised her eyes and held Usha's, steady, and did not blink. Usha smeared the resin on her own throat with two fingers and then on her wrists, and pulled back her cuffs. The flat pared seam showed. The burn took her. Her breath caught.

"I choose you," Usha said. It was not the shape. It was the word before the word. Then she said the other, very low, to Nirmala alone, and Neela turned her face away.

The last leaf of the twenty-six went up with a small dry sound. The ash rose white in the dusk. Four leaves lay on in the case, untouched, for the four who were not ready.

The queen's mouth shook once. Then she put her hand over it, and took it down.

"It stings," Nirmala said, to nobody. "It does sting."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Four of the thirty held back from the naming line and did not come forward. Lata was one. She stood by the stakes with her arms crossed over her sash and her face closed. She had refused her page in Kodiyur, and she was refusing this too, and nobody went to her. Sajan stood near her, two others behind him. The queen had said she would not think the worse. She did not turn her head.

Kamalakshi did not name. She had said in the cairn firelight that she had no one to give it to, and she would not give it to a person who didn't want it. She stood at the back with her arms folded, a thin cropped girl who had laughed once at the shelter. Her leaf lay unburned on Neela's knee. Vellan stood a long way from her, at the narrow place, a man alone against a wall. Unbound, he had said. He had a bad wooden bird inside his shirt, and he looked at no one.

Neela turned that over, too. Four unready, two unbound, and the rest tied to a person by a cord of blood. It was the most exact accounting she had ever done, and it would not balance.

"Now the dark," Devan said, low. He was looking at the far bank.

The last light went off the water. The fires on the far side blinked as something big crossed in front of them. The drums quickened.

Neela went to the thorn below the lip, where the Company by rotation was to smear and turn, and found the new pairs already learning each other. Kuttu, in shape, set a forepaw on a stake point, and Bhaskaran, ten paces off at the wall, swore and shook his hand. Pavi clipped her paw on a rock and Thilak staggered like a man pushed. Thulasi's foreleg scraped a thorn, and Madhavi hopped three steps and caught herself on the wall. Sanu put a pad down on a flint edge and Kandan gave a startled laugh and then stopped laughing.

"That's how it feels," Neela said to them all, over the drums. She was not shouting. "It's a small thing. It's meant to be small. Learn it now, with thorns. By midnight you'll be learning it with iron."

Bhaskaran had crossed the stakes. He crouched by the animal at his feet, still rubbing his raw fingertips, and looked down into its eyes. "Does it get easier?" he asked it.

The animal looked up at him and could not answer. Neela did.

"No," she said. "You get better at it."

Devan's hand closed on her shoulder, brief and dry. She did not turn. She smelled the resin on her own wrists, and the first burn began at her throat.

"Left side," he said.

"I know where it is."

She put her eyes on his face for the span of one long breath. She did not say the word. She had no need. She stepped down into the thorn, and the world went to scent.

A drum stopped.

The silence was entire. On the far bank, the great square shape on the shingle began to move, very slowly, on its cart. Torches ran ahead of it. Along the lip the garrison's backs straightened, and Haran's voice rolled down the line, flat and carrying, calling the ranks.

Then the first ladder came out of the dark, iron-hooked and slick with river water, and struck the lip of the shelf with a crack of timber on stone.

# Chapter 25: The Spear

The third ladder came over the lip while the second was still burning, and Devan put his spear through the first man who showed above the rung.

He did it without thinking. Since the scouts on the shelf, his body no longer asked leave. The man had a bronze cap and a mouth open to shout, and the point went in under the jaw and the shout never came. Devan wrenched the shaft free, braced his boot on the dead man's shoulder and shoved. Ladder, man and hooks went backward into the dark. Somewhere below, wood broke.

"Left, Sanu!" Kandan yelled from three paces down the wall. "Water, damn you, water!"

Devan did not look. He had one rule for tonight: look only at the next hand that came over.

The shelf burned in pieces. Torches on the stakes, a pitch pot someone had kicked over, the orange glow of the tower where it squatted on the shingle with its ram arm half raised. Beyond the stakes the river was loud and black. Between the stakes and the lip, the Thurai kept climbing out of it with bronze on their heads and iron hooks on their ladders, and the line held them and bent and held.

It was the first watch. The drums had been going since dusk. Devan had counted six ladders, then eleven, then stopped counting.

Neela was at his left.

He knew it by sound, not sight. A scrape of pads on stone, a low cough of breath, a warm bulk that moved when he moved. When he stepped, she stepped. When his left side dropped, as it always did when he was tired, a spotted shape was there to fill it, and a raider's thrust that should have opened his ribs found teeth instead.

He kept his eyes front. He was learning how to be guarded.

A sting lit his left forearm, sharp, then a second on the calf. Hers. A bar of fire along one flank, a scored line he could feel but not see. He gritted his teeth and kept the spear moving. Each welt was a count in a ledger he had agreed to keep. Every blow she took, he wore. Every blow he took, she wore. They had bought that at dusk in the Resin House yard, and tonight was the first time anyone would pay in full.

The Company paid beside them. He could see it, between ladders, by torchlight. An otter slid along the foot of the wall and a tall tapper in grey fought a pace behind her, spear held crosswise to shield her flank. When a raider's hook dragged across the otter's back, the tapper flinched, stumbled and kept his feet. A boar the size of a calf stood at the wall's end with Bhaskaran at its shoulder, the sergeant's raw fingers wrapped on his shaft. Sanu's long shape went flat to the stones, and Kandan, who had sworn he was nobody's, stood over it with a shield from a dead man's arm and took the hook meant for its neck.

They were fighting carefully. That was the word for it. Every animal looked for its partner before it struck, and every partner looked for the animal. They were slower than they would have been alone, and none of them was dying.

Not yet.

"Warden!" Muthu's voice, from behind, thick with smoke. The captain stood on the firing step with his garrison archers in a row, working their bows as fast as hands could draw. "Sheaf six is empty!"

"Take from the dead."

"They're on the wrong side of the stakes!"

"Then take from ours." Devan heard how it sounded, and did not take it back. "Muthu. Keep the arrows on the tower. Not the ladders. The tower."

"If the tower comes—"

"It's why I want you on it."

A crack of bows answered, ragged and then even.

Devan cleared a gap with the spear's butt, took a breath that tasted of pitch, and saw the thing he had been afraid of.

It happened where the line bent. A slight guard from Usha's riders had gone into the front rank beside a Kettamala spearman. Devan did not know her name. She had been paired with the man at dusk, and he had seen it from Neela's side on the shelf. The man was Vasu, who kept a mother with ducks at Kalladi. Their cords were new. Not two hours old.

She was fighting in her own shape. Devan could not make out what it was. It was low and quick and dark, and the only thing he could see was that it was too quick, going forward as if no one stood behind her. She took a hooked blade across the shoulder. A shallow cut, nothing, a line.

Vasu screamed.

Not from the cut. From the share. Devan watched it travel up the man's arm as if a hand had closed there. Vasu's spear slipped, and he went down on one knee in the line with his mouth open. The animal turned her head, and in the half breath that she looked back for him, a Thurai captain stepped through the place where she had stood.

Raiders poured into the gap.

There were no more than eight of them, then twelve. There was the red horsehair crest of the captain, a long blade, two others with hooks. The guard-beast snapped at an ankle and was struck back. Vasu had his hands on the wall, trying to rise. Behind them stood three pairs, new ones, Pavi and Thilak's neighbours, animals half turned to see why the line had broken, and the men beside them with their spears pointed at the wrong dark.

If the gap stayed open for ten heartbeats, the line would fold from the middle and every pair on the wall would be behind the Thurai instead of before them.

Devan did not decide. He had decided already, a long time ago, in a tack room, over a bad bow.

He knew what the step would cost the spotted shape at his flank. He took it anyway, and felt her come with him, pace for pace, as if she had been asked.

"Left," he said, to nobody, to her. And stepped into the hole.

The captain saw him come. He was a lean man with a thin beard and a cap of beaten bronze, and he had the wide-footed stance of a man who had killed in rooms like this one before. His blade came up. Devan's spear was in his right hand, point low, and the captain's eyes went to the spear.

Devan dropped it.

He heard it ring on the stones. He had not used the other weapon since the bank of the lower ford at twenty-four, and then it had been on a man already on his knees. Eight years he had kept his hand for answering, hidden, a thing held back against the day it was forced. Now he used it openly, with the torches and the garrison and the Company behind him, and with a man's mother's ducks hanging on the line. The fear that had sat on his chest all these years went still.

The two fingers of his right hand went out and found the notch below the captain's ear.

It was no more than a tap. He felt the small hard place give. The captain's eyes widened, then emptied, and he went down in front of Devan with the sword still in his grip, like a man lying down to rest. Nobody had struck him. He had fallen.

Devan felt the quiet it left, a clean cold space where noise had been, and then the next man was there, and the next. He scooped up Vasu's fallen spear with his left hand. His left dropped, as it always did, and a spotted body slammed into the space and the hook meant for his ribs closed on fur instead. He felt it. A long burning drag along his own side, as though someone had pulled a rake through it. He did not stop.

"Back!" he shouted at the three pairs. "Close up, turn, face me! Left of me, now!"

They moved. An animal and a man, an animal and a man, and the gap had a wall in it. Devan stood in the middle of it with a borrowed spear, three newly bound pairs at his back and a leopard at his left flank. For three breaths, four, five, the line held.

Then he turned to take the next ladder man, and a different man came over the lip behind it.

Devan never saw where from. He caught only the shape of it at the edge of his eye: a raider with no cap, a short heavy spear, the haft sliding through two hands in the torchlight. Too close. Devan swung his borrowed shaft across, a beat late. The spear point came in under his right collarbone.

There was no pain at first. It felt like being struck with a fist and then with a cold finger. The point went through, he felt it go, felt the iron come out through the meat at the back and stop against his own shoulder blade. His breath left him in a hard push. The borrowed spear dropped. He looked down and saw the haft standing out of him, a polished ash shaft with a thumb-worn grip, ridiculous, like a post stuck in a field.

The fingers of his right hand would not close on it. They did not move.

Then something went out from behind him, the way a lamp goes out in a room you thought was empty.

He felt it before he heard it. A great drag pulled at the world on his left. Not a sound. A drop. A leopard's bulk sagged against his hip and went down, as if the share had put a hand on her neck and pushed. He felt the same spear in a different body. He felt, at the same moment, her cold: a chill climbing from the soles of her feet, a pull at the collarbone exactly where the spear had gone. It was the cost, and neither of them had understood how it would feel.

"Neela," he said. It came out of him in a rasp.

She did not answer. She could not. He felt her paws scrabble on the stones and fail.

The raider put his boot against Devan's chest to pull the spear free. Devan, with one working hand, caught the man's wrist and held it. It was a bad grip. It was the grip of a man who had run out of everything but refusal. The raider laughed, or began to, and then the sound changed.

A roar rolled up from the cut.

It was not like any sound the Thurai had made all night. It came from the narrow place at the top, where the walls drew in, and it was so large that the ladders shook with it. Devan smelled musk and old tamarind, scorched sap and wet fur. A huge black shape crossed the stones at a drag and a step, faster than it had any right to be.

Vellan's bear took the raider off Devan's chest as a man takes a toy from a child. It threw him a spear's length. Then it stood over the gap with its forelegs spread and its great head swinging, and the Thurai who had been pouring through stopped pouring. A hook rang against its shoulder. The bear took it in its teeth and broke it. Then it roared again, and the roar went down the line like a wind, and the three pairs at Devan's back found their feet.

The bear had left the narrow place. It had abandoned the one spot it had asked to hold, alone and unbound, so that no one would bleed for it. Devan thought, with a clear far-off wonder, that the cut was open behind him now.

He thought: someone should tell him.

His knees went. He did not fall so much as sit, and then lie back, and the stones were cold and rough under his shoulders. The spear shaft had been broken off short by someone's hand; he did not see whose. It lay across his chest like a lever. The sky above the shelf was black and torn with sparks. He could not tell if the stars were stars or embers.

He lifted his right hand. It lay where he put it and did nothing, open, as though it had never held a thing in its life. Two of its fingers had found a man's neck a little while ago. They would not find anything now.

*Eight years,* he thought. *I kept it to answer with. And I used it first.*

Something moved close at his left side.

He turned his head, and it cost him. A leopard lay on the stones beside him, so near that her flank was against his arm. Her eyes caught the torchlight and gave it back gold. A raw wet line ran along her ribs, and another across her shoulder, matching the burn he now felt on his own. Her breath came short and quick, and each breath pulled against the place in him where the iron had been.

He knew that breath. He had listened to it across a bed since their wedding night. He had counted its catch and its hitch in the dark and never told her what it did to him.

She could not speak. The shape did not allow it. She could not take his hand, because she had no hand, only a heavy paw that she laid on his wrist with great care, claws sheathed, as if his wrist might break. It did not close on anything. It did not hold. It only lay there, warm.

"Don't," he said. His mouth tasted of iron. "Don't try to stand. You'll tear it."

She turned her head and pressed her forehead against his jaw.

That was all she could do, and it was not nothing. Somewhere above them the bear was roaring, and a woman's voice was shouting for the stretcher bearers, and a horn he did not know the code for was going on and on. The Thurai drums had changed their beat. The ground shook with something big. He could feel the tower coming, and he could not rise to meet it.

He did not know whether he would see the dawn. He did not know whether she would. The share ran both ways, and what had gone into him had gone into her, and he could feel the cold coming up from her legs like water in a well.

Her breath was against his throat. It hitched, and caught, and went on.

His thoughts were thin now, and slow, and one at a time. The wall. The bear. The stakes. Muthu's arrows. Janu, asleep, somewhere in a court that still stood. All of it was far off, and getting farther, and what stayed was small and plain.

Since Asha, there had been a wall behind him and nothing on the other side of it.

He felt the leopard's bulk on his left, and her breath on his neck, and the bear above the gap, and the line holding.

His back was not empty.

# Chapter 26: Until First Light

They dragged him by the shoulders, and Neela went with him.

She could not stand. Her hind legs had stopped answering somewhere on the stones, and the cold climbed them like water filling a jar. So she crawled. She dragged her belly over the rock beside his boots, and her claws left white lines behind her. Kandan had Devan under the arms. Muthu had his legs. Neither man looked at her. They looked at the broken shaft standing out of Devan's chest and said nothing.

Under her own collarbone, a welt burned. It was deep and hot, and it sat exactly where the iron had gone into him. Every breath pulled at it. She was bleeding there, a little, in the thick fur at her throat. Only the shape of his wound, drawn on her. But the cold kept climbing, and she knew what the cold meant.

*Mortal wound drags the other.* Ammu had said it in the straw. Neither of them had understood it then.

She wanted to say his name. The leopard had no mouth for it. What came out of her was a low, wet cough.

"I know," Muthu said, hauling. His face was black with smoke and streaked clean under the eyes. "I know. Nearly there."

Nearly there was the rear of the shelf, where the cut narrowed and the wall made a pocket against the rock. Someone had hung a hooded lantern from a stake. Someone had spread a cart tarpaulin on the stones. Beyond it, the line was a noise: iron, bronze, shouting, the drums. The bear's roar came from above, from the top of the cut, from the place that should have been empty.

Kandan let Devan down onto the tarpaulin as if he were glass. Neela did not wait to be asked. She climbed onto him.

She laid herself across his chest, below the wound, with her forelegs over his ribs and her weight on the wet cloth they had packed in the hole. He was a long man and she was a heavy cat. His heart beat under her, quick and faint, like a thing running a long way off.

"Good," said a voice above her. "Stay exactly there."

A woman knelt at his head. She was past fifty, broad in the shoulder, her grey hair tied up in a strip of green. She had a leather roll open on the tarpaulin, and the lantern gleamed on what was in it. A clamp. A curved needle. A hank of waxed silk. Neela knew her: the woman from the Kodiyur drying shed, who had laid the paring knife down. It did not matter now.

"Thankam," the woman said, to the leopard, as though the leopard might not remember. "The queen's surgeon. I am going to cut his coat off and then I am going to hurt him. Your weight is better than any hand I've got. If you shift, he bleeds. Do you understand?"

Neela lowered her chin onto his breastbone.

"Good," Thankam said again.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The knife went through the cloth with a sound like tearing paper. Devan's breath changed. It went short and then long, and a noise came out of him. It was not a word.

Neela counted.

She always counted. Exits, steps, doors. Now it was breaths, his breaths under her ribs. *One. Two.* A third of the way through a long count, there was a hitch. A small catch, like a hand missing a rung. She had learned it in the dark of the warden's chamber, lying awake beside a man who pretended to sleep. She waited for it now as she had waited then. It came. She let the count go on.

*One. Two. Hitch.*

"Clamp," Thankam muttered. She was talking to herself, or to Muthu, who was holding the lantern too low. "Higher, Captain. Higher. I need to see the back of it."

Muthu lifted the light. His hand shook. He steadied it with the other.

Devan's eyes had opened. Neela knew it by the tightening of the chest beneath her. She raised her head and looked into his face, grey as ash, with sweat standing on the lip. His eyes found her and slid away, and found her again.

"Cold," he said. It was a thread of a voice. "Your legs. Someone. Her legs."

Neela went still.

He was bleeding on a tarpaulin with iron in his back, and the thing he had noticed was her hind legs.

Muthu did not argue. He tore off his own coat and laid it over her haunches and tucked it under her flank, clumsily, the way a man covers a sleeping dog. It smelled of smoke and horse and sweat. The cold in her legs did not stop. But it dulled, as if a door had been put between her and it.

Devan's eyelids drooped. His mouth moved.

"Go up," he said.

She did not move.

"The line. Vellan. You're a better..." His breath caught on the hitch and he had to start again. "Go. I'll keep."

Up the cut, the bear roared. Neela's whole body answered it. Her claws pricked the tarpaulin. She could have been up the stones in three bounds, if her legs would carry her. She could have put herself between the Thurai and the narrow place, and been useful, and loud, and a leopard for some good purpose.

And his blood would have come up through the cloth the moment she rose.

She knew it as one knows a step in the dark. The choice was not hard to see. It was only hard to do.

The other one's every instinct was for the fight. Hers was a small stubborn thing, learned in a room with a barred shutter. She laid her chin back on his chest, and pressed down.

He saw it. His lips moved again, not on words this time. His good hand, the left, came up off the tarpaulin and found the fur of her neck, and rested there.

"Stubborn," he whispered. "Good."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"The nerve is cut," Thankam said.

She said it in the flat voice of a woman who had said such things to soldiers all her life. She had the clamp in, and the bleeding had slowed to a seep, and she was working the silk through with a hand that did not shake. Devan was gone again, far under, his breath long and slow. Neela was grateful. She wished she could close her ears.

"Behind the collarbone, where the plexus runs. The spear went through it. The shoulder will mend, and the lung is whole, which is a mercy I'd not have bet on. But the nerve to the hand is cut clean." Thankam drew the thread. "I can close the wound. I can't close that. If it grows back, it will take a year, and the hand may never close hard again."

No one answered.

A light moved at the edge of the lantern's reach. Neela's ears turned, and a figure came around the rock with a second lamp in her fist. A small woman in a plain dark wrap, her hair coming out of its plait.

The queen did not look at her guard or at her captain. She came to the tarpaulin and knelt on the stones, in the blood, with no cloth under her knees. She held her left arm a little away from her body.

On the forearm, below the pushed-up sleeve, a welt had risen. It was a red slanting line, raised and angry, the length of a hand. A fresh cut. Not hers. Usha's, from somewhere in the line.

Nirmala saw Neela looking.

"It does sting," she said, very dry. "I was told it would. I find I can't stop attending to it." She set her lamp on a rock where Thankam could use it. "You'll want light on the shoulder. Give me his."

She took the lantern out of Muthu's hand. Muthu let her.

She held it steady. Neela watched her do it. The old woman knelt there with her wrist bleeding a little through its own cloth and the light never wavered, though the line was close and the ground shook with the tower's ram. She held it exactly where the surgeon needed it, as if she had done nothing else.

"I held lamps once," Nirmala said, to nobody. Her eyes were on Devan's face. "For each of them, at the first turning. A room, one flame, a child with resin on her wrists, and a woman who must not look afraid." She breathed. "I told myself the light was so that they could see a face. It was so that I could."

Thankam's needle went in. The queen's hand did not move. But Neela, who had spent twenty years learning what people did with their hands when they were afraid, saw the knuckles go white on the lantern's bail.

"I did not count the cost," the queen said. "I counted everything else."

Neela could not answer. She did not think an answer was wanted. She only lay across a man's chest and let a queen hold the light, and saw how small the old woman was in it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Time went by in breaths.

She counted them past a hundred and past a thousand, and lost the number, and began again. *One. Two. Hitch.* Each time the catch came, something gave in her own ribs. The cold in her legs held at the knees. It went no higher. She did not know if that was Thankam's silk, or her own body's weight, or the plain stubbornness of a man who had decided to be there in the morning.

She learned the sounds of the night. The ram stopped. The drums changed beat, then thinned, then began again from farther off. Garrison archers cursed and sang and cursed. Somewhere a man cried a name over and over. She did not know whose.

The bear roared. Each time, she counted the gap before the next. It was short at first. By the middle watch, it was long.

She lifted her head once, when the pain in her collarbone went quiet. Between two stakes, past the lip, the river lay black and moving, and something was in it.

It was long and low, and it rode very flat in the water. It was nothing she knew. Koman had called it a shape the Resin House had never made. It moved along the shingle bank where the Thurai dead lay half in the current, and it took a man by the harness in its jaws and drew him up out of the river, and left him above the water line. Then it went back for another. Raiders were calling from the far bank. A torch swung, and the creature slid under it and was gone. It did not fight. It did not look at the shelf. It pulled the wounded out and let them breathe.

She did not understand it. She was too tired to try. She put her head down on Devan's chest.

*One. Two.*

*Hitch.*


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The sky greyed from the east. First the stakes grew edges, and the stones showed their colour, and the faces around the tarpaulin turned from black to bone. Then the cold went out of her hind legs all at once.

She knew what was coming. She had always known. It came the way it had come every morning of her life, unasked.

The turning back was pain.

It began in her spine and ran out along her limbs. The leopard's muscle drew in and the woman's came up through it, and her bones remembered a shorter shape and fought to keep a longer one. Her claws were nails. Her paws were hands. The coat on her haunches grew heavy, and Muthu's shirt-sleeves were suddenly there in her fingers. Her skin was bare and wet to the dawn. She retched. She had no breath for a sound.

Someone had turned their back. Kandan, standing at the rock with his shoulders square and his face to the cliff. Muthu, too, with a blanket held out behind him at arm's length. The queen did not turn. She had seen it before, and Neela had already said her word to one man. A witness's silence was a favour now, not a need. It still counted.

She took the blanket. She did not remember doing it.

Her voice was a scrape. "Don't. Move. Him."

"We won't," said Thankam.

Neela got her hands under her. They worked. Her fingers were stiff and white and shaking, and they were hers. She crawled the last arm's length on her knees, to where his head lay, and bent over him.

His eyes were shut. His lips were blue. The seep at the pad over his wound had come back, red and slow, and she could feel it in her own chest like a thumb pressed on the bone.

She knew where the point was. Her mother's people could not have taught it, and the Resin House had. A hand's breadth under the jaw, at the hollow of the throat, where the pulse beat close to the skin. Heal or kill; it depended on the hand and the heart behind it.

She set two fingers on it.

*Breathe,* she thought, and did not say it. He had no need of an order. She leaned her weight into the touch, very light, then a little less. She counted three. She counted his hitch.

Under her fingers, the blood eased.

It was nothing a stranger would have seen. The red on the pad thinned from a seep to a bead and from a bead to a stain. Thankam's head came up. In Neela's own breastbone, the hot stabbing line she had carried all night settled, like a lamp turned low.

Above them, at the top of the cut, the roaring stopped.

It did not trail off. It stopped between one breath and the next, and the quiet that took its place was larger than any noise. Neela's fingers did not leave Devan's throat. She did not lift her head. She knew that silence. It was the silence of a house with the door left open.

The queen lowered the lantern. In the grey light it looked like a small, ordinary thing, a clay lamp with its flame gone pale.

"Who will go up?" Nirmala asked.

No one answered.

Devan's chest rose under Neela's hand, and fell, and caught on its small familiar hitch. She stayed where she was, bare and shaking, and counted it, and did not let go.

# Chapter 27: Keep the Light There

Devan woke to a hand he could not feel.

It lay on the tarpaulin beside him, palm up, and it looked like something a stranger had left there. He told the fingers to close. Nothing happened. The silence at the top of the cut was the second thing he noticed. The bear had stopped.

Grey light. Smoke and wet stone. Neela sat against his left side in Muthu's coat with a blanket over her shoulders and her bare feet drawn up. Her two fingers were still against the hollow of his throat, as if she had forgotten them there.

"Vellan," he said.

Her eyes came up. They told him she had been listening to that silence all night. "Muthu says he's sitting."

"Sitting where?"

"At the top. Against the rock. Nobody's gone near him." She swallowed. "Nobody knows what the bear left."

He tried to rise. The shoulder said no in a voice like a cleaver, and the dead arm dragged at his side. Thankam, asleep on her knees over her roll, jerked awake.

"Lie still, Warden, or I stitch you twice."

"I need to go up the cut."

"You need to lie flat for a week."

"A man is dying up there who held the narrow place for me." He heard how that sounded. "And he has something that's mine."

Thankam looked at him a long moment. Then at Neela, who said nothing and did not need to. The surgeon cursed in a coast dialect and called for the captain.

Muthu and Kandan did it with a door. It had come off a guard shed in the night, and it still had its iron strap hinge. They laid it by the tarpaulin and slid Devan onto it, and Thankam bound his arm to his chest with a green sash so the dead hand could not swing. Neela walked beside him, barefoot on the stones, holding the corner of the blanket with one fist. Every few steps her hand came up and touched his shoulder, then went away, as though checking a latch.

The cut climbed between walls of red rock. He saw it from his back, one ledge at a time. A bronze cap in the shingle. A ladder hook still sunk in a stake. Thurai dead lay in rows where the line had held them, and others lay singly where they had come over and not gone back. Past the stakes the river ran brown and quiet. Far down the road, toward the strand, a thin dust rose where the host was pulling away. The drums were still going, but slowly, like a heart that has finished running.

"They've drawn back," Kandan said, to nobody. "To the beach. They'll count us."

"Let them," said Muthu.

Devan said nothing. He was counting something else. At the narrow place the cut drew in until two carts could not pass, and there the dead lay thickest. They lay in a half circle, bronze and torn leather, and some of them bore no mark he could name.

The bear had made a wall of them. Vellan sat against the rock behind it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He was a man again, as the dawn had made him. He sat with his legs out and his big hands in his lap, in the grey wool of a Company tapper, and the wool was dark to the knees. Someone's hook had opened him from hip to ribs. He held his belly shut with one arm, and the other lay across his chest. The bad leg lay straight at last, with no drag left in it. A bird sat in his palm.

It was a bad bird. Devan saw that from the door, and so did the whole cut, probably. One wing was thicker than the other and the beak had split. Someone had cut it with a dull knife and no patience.

"Warden," Vellan said. His voice was a grumble with the bottom taken out. "You brought a door."

"Muthu did."

"Good man." His eyes went past Devan, to Neela, and stayed. "Seventeen. Sit. You're swaying."

Neela knelt in the blood. She did not argue. Kandan and Muthu lowered the door and stepped back, and Muthu pulled Kandan's sleeve until the two of them walked away down the cut, out of hearing. Devan was grateful. Nobody ought to see this.

"I can't do anything for it," Vellan said. "Thankam would say so. I'd not trouble her." He looked down at the arm across his belly. "Unbound. That was the point. Nobody bleeds for me. I'd have hated to hear that I'd cut anyone."

"You left the narrow place," Devan said.

"I did." A dry breath that was almost a laugh. "The line gapped. Somebody ought to fill a gap. It's an old habit."

"You told me you'd say it once you'd earned it."

"I haven't." The big hand tightened on the bird. "I've no way left to earn it. So I'll say it anyway and you can do what you like with the words. Is that fair?"

Devan found that he could not speak. Neela's fingers had come to rest on his left wrist. He turned the hand over and held on.

"Sit nearer," said Vellan.

They did.

"She came down the west stair in the dark," Vellan said. "Late. Past the second watch. She had no lamp. I was low on the stair, in shape, by the grate under the eleventh step, back against the wall where the dark was thickest. I heard her before I saw her. She was counting."

Devan's mouth went dry.

"Out loud, under her breath. Forty-four, forty-three, forty-two. Slow. One hand on the rail. I thought, this one's afraid of the dark. It's no business of mine, but I marked it." Vellan's eyes had gone far back. "She came down a long way before she saw anything. Then, far below, at the foot, a little glimmer. A shuttered lamp, opened a thumb's width."

Neela made a sound. It was very small.

"Yours," Vellan said. He did not look at her. "You'd been told to open it twice if anyone came into the court. She wasn't in the court. She was on the stair. You opened it once, I think. Just the once, out of fright. A thumb of light."

"I don't remember," Neela whispered. "I remember counting. I remember the dark."

"You opened it. She saw it." Vellan breathed, carefully, around his wound. "And she called down. Not loud. 'Who's there?'"

Devan shut his eyes.

"You didn't answer. You were twenty-one and you'd been blindfolded and told to be a post. Quite right. And she waited. And then she smiled." The big man's voice changed. It wasn't an imitation. It was only as careful as a man handing over something that might break. "I saw it. I was close enough. She smiled at the light, and she said, 'Dev? Keep the light there. I can see the steps.'"

The cut was so quiet Devan could hear the river.

"She thought it was you, Warden. At the foot of the stair, with a lamp, for her. She thought her brother had waited up." Vellan swallowed. "She came on down, counting, her palm sliding on the rail. Easy. She wasn't afraid. She only saw me at the last, when she was nearly on me, and I'll tell you, she looked at the bear and then she looked back down at the light. As if to check it was still there. She never took her eyes off it."

Neela had made herself small beside him. Her hand had gone to her mouth.

"That's the whole of it," Vellan said. "I've carried it six years and I thought it would be heavier to put down. It isn't."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


For a long moment Devan felt nothing. That was how he knew it was coming.

Then it came all at once, from under his right collarbone, where the spear had passed. Six years. Six years of a wall at his back. He had slept with it, eaten with it, and never let a living soul stand behind him on a stair.

Asha had come down forty-odd steps in the dark, afraid of it all her life, and she had looked at a thumb of lamplight and thought: *Dev's there.*

And someone had been. Not him. A lamp, and a woman behind it.

He did not choose to weep. The sound came up through his chest like water from a well, and the shoulder tore and the dead arm shook against the sash, and he could not stop. He wept as he had not at the pyre, or at the writ, or in the grey before dawn with Janu's lamp. It was loud and ugly. He did not turn his face.

Neela's arms came round him.

Both of them. She did not ask. She got her arms under his good shoulder and across his chest, below the wound, and pulled him against her, and held on with both hands as if he might go over an edge. He felt her cheek on his hair. Through her he felt the shape of her own grief, which was not his grief and was the same. Her breath shook. A tear fell on his scalp.

"I opened it once," she said into his hair. "Once. I didn't know."

"You couldn't have."

"I know."

"Don't ask me to forgive you." He wasn't certain it was his voice. "There's nothing there to forgive. You gave her a light."

She was quiet so long he thought she would say nothing. Then her voice came, very low, against his hair.

"Three hundred and ten," she said. "That's how long I stood at the foot of that stair, after. They'd unwrapped my eyes and told me to count, and I counted. I've never told anyone the number. I thought if I said it out loud it would be something I'd done."

"It was something you did," Devan said. "You stood where you were put, in the dark, and you counted. So did she. Two of you, counting, on the same stair." He turned his good hand over on her wrist. "I'll keep the three hundred and ten with you. Half of it's mine now."

She said no word. She only held him tighter, and the two of them rocked together on the red stone, a man with a dead hand and a woman in a borrowed coat, over a thing neither would put down and neither would carry alone.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


When he could look up, Vellan's eyes were on them.

"I'd have liked to hear what you'd have said," the big man murmured. "Well. It's said." The bird shifted in his palm. He closed his fingers over it with great care. "The girl's bird. Kamalakshi. Terrible, isn't it? She cut it with my own knife. I told her the beak was wrong. She said it was supposed to be." The grumble was fading. "Keep it where it is. I'd not like it taken."

"It stays," said Devan.

"Good. I've done what I could." The breath went in and out. "There's a Thurai boy at my feet. Fifteen. Someone ought to see that he's buried."

"I'll see it," said Neela.

"Aye. You would. You've the names." His head tipped back against the rock. His gaze went up, past them, to the top of the cut and the grey sky above it. "It's light," he said, a little surprised. "I can see the steps."

Devan did not move. Neela's arms had not loosened. After a long time, when the breath had not come again, she put two fingers to the big man's throat, where the pulse lay close. She held them there a good while.

Then she took her hand away and shook her head.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They carried him up the last of the cut on the door. Devan walked.

He had not asked leave. He had stood, with Muthu's arm under his good shoulder, and said, "Put Vellan on it." Nobody had argued. Four men lifted the big man between them, and his hand stayed shut on the bird, and the blanket over his face moved a little in the wind.

The cut climbed on in the new light, red and wet. Garrison men stood along it with their spears grounded. They did not salute. Some looked at the door, and some at Devan's sash, and Kuttu, the ferryman's son, stood with both hands over his mouth. Pavi had her arm round Thilak, who was not weeping but breathing like a man who had been under water. The Company had come out of the night thirty-odd strong where there had been forty-two. Nobody counted aloud.

The lower court of Kettamala had been turned into a camp. Cook-fires, a row of cots, a well with a line already at it. Janu stood by the kitchen door with a pot in each fist. When she saw the door and the blanket and the sash on Devan's arm, she set the pots down very slowly and did not come forward. He gave her a nod. She pressed her apron to her mouth.

Queen Nirmala was waiting by the well.

She sat on an overturned bucket, with her left arm in a bandage and Usha at her shoulder. In her lap lay a flat case of green-lacquered leather, scorched along one edge, brought up from the red chest in the night. She had it open. The leaves inside were thin and pale and mostly not leaves at all. They were black curls, ash, a drift of it on the blue cloth. A few remained whole.

She looked up as the door passed. She did not stand. She looked at the blanket, and her mouth moved a little, and she said, "The bear."

"Vellan," Neela said.

"I know his name. I paid for his kind for twenty years and never once asked it." The queen's voice was quite level. "I ask it now. It is a poor time to learn what a name weighs."

No one answered. Muthu set his end of the door in the shade of the wall, and the others laid it down with care.

Nirmala turned the case so that Devan could see.

"Twenty-six leaves have burned of themselves since the dusk," she said. "Each at its naming, as the witness entry went. Four are left, for the four who were not ready." She ran one finger along the cloth, and the ash came away grey on her skin. "Those are the four pages I can still be made to read. Mine is the voice that would cage them, if I were pressed, and every guard of the four knows it."

"Will you keep them?" said Devan.

"I have not decided. That is the worst thing I own." She closed the lid over the black. "You have your hand, Warden? No. Thankam told me. Then I will say it to the one who still has two." She looked at Neela. "You cannot hold what is left with a promise. A company needs a roll. It needs a first name on it, written by daylight where every witness can see, and someone to write it who is not afraid of the ink."

Neela stood very still in Muthu's coat, bare-footed on the court stones, with Vellan's blood to her knees.

"I will tell you what it costs, because no one told me what mine cost," Nirmala said. "The first name written binds that person to the Company's vote, whatever the hands decide. And it makes her the crown's first name to answer. Whatever the Company does, I will lay at that name. I would not ask it of anyone I did not mean to hear from."

She rose from the bucket, slowly, using Usha's arm, and the cold light caught the sunbird on the scorched case.

"Who signs first," she said, "I leave to you."

Devan looked at his own right hand hanging in its sash and thought of a pen. Then of a lamp on a stair.

Somewhere above the court, the first cistern chain clanked the hour.

# Chapter 28: First Name on the Roll

By midmorning the sun had reached the lower court, and Neela could not stop looking at the ink.

It sat in a cracked clay pot on the well curb, plain black, the kind a clerk buys by the measure. She had bent over it twice already, the way a woman checks a sleeping child. Both times it smelled of nothing at all.

"It won't take," Thilak said beside her. "I tried it on my thumb. Not a mark that stays."

"Good."

He had brought a sheet from the queen's green case, a clean crown leaf, ivory and heavy. He had flattened it on a board across two buckets and weighted the corners with pebbles. His big tapper's hands kept straightening it, though it was already straight. The lamp-black plank leaned against the well. Forty names ran down it in her own hand, and nine blanks had been filled since the Resin House.

Behind her the court was filling. She did not turn to count. She listened: boots on stone, a cough, Kuttu telling someone to move his feet. Garrison men in grey stood three deep along the kitchen wall, and the queen's riders in green along the other. The Company had no colour. They stood where they had slept, in tapper's wool and borrowed coats, thirty-odd where there had been forty-two. She knew every face. She had said every name.

Vellan lay in the shade of the barracks wall under a blanket. Someone had put a smear of lamp-black on the board's edge for him, near the top, so he would be on the plank.

She washed her hands at the well and dried them on Muthu's coat. They were steady. All night they had shaken.

Janu stood at the kitchen door with a pot in each fist, though no one had asked for food. At dawn Neela had told her the one thing Vellan said about the lamp, and Janu had said nothing at all. She had gone back to her pots and had not looked up since. Muthu stood near the gate arch with his hands behind him. The queen sat on her bucket with Usha at her shoulder and the scorched case on her knee, her bandaged arm across it. She did not look at Neela. She had the grace to look at the sky.

And Devan sat on the mounting block with his dead arm in its green sash, and watched her.

He had refused the cot. Thankam had said what she thought of that, at length, and then brought him a stool. He was grey. He did not smile. He held her eyes, and she heard the old click of a latch closing, the one she had learned in a warded room. This time it was a door she had chosen to stand in.

*Say it before you do it,* they had agreed once, in a tack room, with rain on the thatch.

"I'm going to write my name," she said across the court, loud enough for anyone. "First. Then I'm going to ask you to write yours."

"I heard," he said.

"You didn't hear the rest." She turned, so the court could hear her. She had not planned the next part. "The queen says the first name binds me to the Company's vote, whatever the hands decide. If they vote against me, I go with it. And it makes me the first name the crown comes to when the Company does something it dislikes. I'm telling you all now. By daylight. Not in a back room."

Nobody spoke.

"For twenty-one years I answered to a number," she said. "I wanted to be no one's. I wanted it so much I called it freedom." Her voice scraped. She let it. "It was a very quiet cage. Nobody in it ever bled for me."

She picked up the reed.

It was a poor reed, chewed at the end. She dipped it and her hand hung over the sheet. She had killed with that hand. She had tied a double hitch with it, in her sleep, without knowing where it came from.

*Indu.*

The first stroke was thin. She pressed, and the second was a bold black bar.

*Indu, called Neela, of Nellimala and Kettamala.*

It took less time than a breath. The ink lay wet and shining and did not burn or flare or draw blood. Something went through her anyway, from the base of her skull down into her hands. She would have called it a weight leaving, but it was more like a weight set down on a table where someone else could see it.

"Read it," said Janu, from the kitchen door.

"Janu—"

"Aloud. I'm sixty-one and half deaf and I want to hear."

So Neela read it. Her voice cracked on the second name. In the grey ranks a man said her birth name over under his breath, as if testing it for heft. Pavi pressed a fist to her teeth.

The queen said nothing. But Neela saw her lips move on the word *Kettamala*, and a small, odd stillness cross her face.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Devan stood.

It took him a moment. Muthu moved forward and Devan lifted his good hand, and Muthu stopped. The mounting block was six paces from the well. He walked them one at a time. She watched his feet.

"Reed," he said.
